Source: `verse-analysis/psalms/_base-sources/psalms__praise-extol-sing.json` + `verse-analysis/psalms/_narratives/psalms__praise-extol-sing__narratives.json`. ## Psa 6:1-10

> To the choirmaster : with stringed instruments ; according to The Sheminith . A Psalm of David . Psa 6:1 O Lord , rebuke me not in your anger , nor discipline me in your wrath . Psa 6:2 Be gracious to me, O Lord , for I am languishing ; heal me, O Lord , for my bones are troubled . Psa 6:3 My soul also is greatly troubled . But you , O Lord — how long ? Psa 6:4 Turn , O Lord , deliver my life ; save me for the sake of your steadfast love . Psa 6:5 For in death there is no remembrance of you; in Sheol who will give you praise ? Psa 6:6 I am weary with my moaning ; every night I flood my bed with tears; I drench my couch with my weeping . Psa 6:7 My eye wastes away because of grief ; it grows weak because of all my foes . Psa 6:8 Depart from me, all you workers of evil , for the Lord has heard the sound of my weeping . Psa 6:9 The Lord has heard my plea ; the Lord accepts my prayer . Psa 6:10 All my enemies shall be ashamed and greatly troubled ; they shall turn back and be put to shame in a moment .

**give you praise** — Psa 6:5  ·  `H3034:giveyouprais#1`

_Story_

Here praise shows up not as a song but as an argument for staying alive. Worn down by suffering and pleading to be rescued, the psalmist reasons that the dead cannot remember or praise God at all, so to die would be to fall silent for ever. Nothing in the verse says what triggered the thought or where inside him it sits; it simply rises out of the fear of death. What it reveals is striking: the deepest reason he wants to live is so that he can keep praising. The verse stops at the reasoning and never tells us how it turned out.

_Reading_

This is a 'cognition'-type (102) movement: praise appears here not as song but as an argument the interior makes to save its own life. The passage runs from rebuke and languishing (vv1-3) to a plea for deliverance 'for the sake of your steadfast love' (v4), and at v5 the reasoning surfaces: 'in death there is no remembrance of you; in Sheol who will give you praise?' The source (103) is absent - no cause is named for this thought; it arises directly out of the terror of dying, uncaused within the self. No seat (104) is recorded, so the movement is not lodged in a named organ; it is the whole self reasoning. The bearer (105) is the psalmist, and the operation (106) is a plea framed as a mortality-argument (107): the self reasons that death would end its praising, and so pleads to remain among the living worshippers. The coupling (112) that binds it is 'dead-cannot-praise' - praise and life are welded, so that losing life means losing the capacity to praise. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all silent: the verse does not colour how loudly, to what degree, or with what result - the record is only of the reasoning itself, not its outcome. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114) is that the interior argues for its own survival by its desire to keep praising: the motive to be rescued is the wish to stay a worshipper. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

## Psa 8:4-7

> Psa 8:4 what is man that you are mindful of him, and the son of man that you care for him ? Psa 8:5 Yet you have made him a little lower than the heavenly beings and crowned him with glory and honor . Psa 8:6 You have given him dominion over the works of your hands ; you have put all things under his feet , Psa 8:7 all sheep and oxen , and also the beasts of the field , Psa 8:9 O Lord , our Lord , how majestic is your name in all the earth !

**crowned** — Psa 8:5  ·  `H5849:crown#1`

_Story_

This is not an act of praise but the inner recognition of being lifted high. The psalm first asks what a human being even is that God should notice him, and then answers: made only a little lower than the heavenly beings, crowned with glory and honour, given rule over the works of God's hands. The verse never says what prompted the thought or where it sits inside; it simply holds the awareness of a dignity that has been given. What stands out is the paradox it keeps together - the same small, fragile creature is also a crowned one, and both truths are held at once.

_Reading_

This is a 'state'-type (102) reading - not an act of praising but the inner grasp of a bestowed condition. The passage asks what man is that God is mindful of him (v4), then answers with elevation: made a little lower than the heavenly beings and crowned with glory and honour (v5), given dominion (v6). The characteristic here is the interior's registering of its own crowned status. Source (103) is absent - the verse does not root this awareness in any prior cause within the self; the dignity is simply reflected on, arising unbidden. No seat (104): the state is not seated in a faculty but held by the whole self. Bearer (105) is mankind, the operation (106) a meditation on human dignity, its target (107) that dignity itself. The coupling (112) is 'crowned-with-glory' - the self bound to a conferred honour. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: the verse weighs no degree and traces no consequence beyond the fact of the crowning. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114) is a held paradox: the frail creature of v4 is also a crowned one - smallness and dignity kept together in one movement, neither cancelling the other. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

## Psa 9:1-19

> To the choirmaster : according to Muth-labben . A Psalm of David . Psa 9:1 I will give thanks to the Lord with my whole heart ; I will recount all of your wonderful deeds. Psa 9:2 I will be glad and exult in you; I will sing praise to your name , O Most High . Psa 9:3 When my enemies turn back , they stumble and perish before your presence. Psa 9:4 For you have maintained my just cause ; you have sat on the throne , giving righteous judgment. Psa 9:5 You have rebuked the nations ; you have made the wicked perish ; you have blotted out their name forever and ever . Psa 9:6 The enemy came to an end in everlasting ruins ; their cities you rooted out ; the very memory of them has perished . Psa 9:7 But the Lord sits enthroned forever ; he has established his throne for justice , Psa 9:8 and he judges the world with righteousness ; he judges the peoples with uprightness . Psa 9:9 The Lord is a stronghold for the oppressed , a stronghold in times of trouble . Psa 9:10 And those who know your name put their trust in you, for you, O Lord , have not forsaken those who seek you. Psa 9:11 Sing praises to the Lord , who sits enthroned in Zion ! Tell among the peoples his deeds ! Psa 9:12 For he who avenges blood is mindful of them; he does not forget the cry of the afflicted . Psa 9:13 Be gracious to me, O Lord ! See my affliction from those who hate me, O you who lift me up from the gates of death , Psa 9:14 that I may recount all your praises , that in the gates of the daughter of Zion I may rejoice in your salvation . Psa 9:15 The nations have sunk in the pit that they made ; in the net that they hid , their own foot has been caught . Psa 9:16 The Lord has made himself known ; he has executed judgment ; the wicked are snared in the work of their own hands . Higgaion . Selah Psa 9:17 The wicked shall return to Sheol , all the nations that forget God . Psa 9:18 For the needy shall not always be forgotten , and the hope of the poor shall not perish forever . Psa 9:19 Arise , O Lord ! Let not man prevail ; let the nations be judged before you! Psa 9:20 Put them in fear, O Lord! Let the nations know that they are but men! Selah

**Sing** — Psa 9:11  ·  `H2167:sing#1`

_Story_

Praise here becomes an act of will that turns outward. Having thanked God with his whole heart and remembered all God's deeds, the psalmist cannot keep it to himself: he calls others to sing and to tell the nations what God has done. The verse names no inner trigger and no bodily seat for the impulse; it simply refuses to stay private. Song and testimony are bound together - to praise is to broadcast, and the gratitude reaches for the whole world to hear.

_Reading_

This is a 'volition'-type (102) movement: praise here is the will turning outward. The psalm gives thanks with the whole heart and recounts God's wonderful deeds (vv1-2), rehearses God enthroned for justice (vv4-8), and at v11 issues the summons: 'Sing praises to the Lord, who sits enthroned in Zion! Tell among the peoples his deeds!' The characteristic is the interior's refusal to keep its gratitude private - it summons others to sing and broadcasts God's acts to the nations. Source (103) is absent: no prior cause within the self is named; the impulse simply arises from the rehearsed deeds. No seat (104) - not lodged in an organ; the whole self wills. Bearer (105) is the psalmist; operation (106) is praise turned outward and public; target (107) is proclamation. The coupling (112) is 'tell-the-deeds': singing welded to telling, worship inseparable from testimony. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - the verse does not measure volume or trace a result. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): interior gratitude will not stay private; it broadcasts God's deeds among the peoples. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* Among the readings of this term ('sing/make music'), this one sits at the outward, public pole: its target is proclamation among the peoples, and the bearer summons others as well as singing. It differs from the same-family readings that turn the song toward God as worship of his might (Psa 21:13) or erupt in joyful sacrifice (Psa 27:6), and from the insistent fivefold call at Psa 47:6. The shift from private gratitude to broadcast testimony is meaningful: it marks how far this term can carry praise beyond the self.

## Psa 13:1-5

> To the choirmaster . A Psalm of David . Psa 13:1 How long , O Lord ? Will you forget me forever ? How long will you hide your face from me ? Psa 13:2 How long must I take counsel in my soul and have sorrow in my heart all the day ? How long shall my enemy be exalted over me ? Psa 13:3 Consider and answer me, O Lord my God ; light up my eyes , lest I sleep the sleep of death , Psa 13:4 lest my enemy say , “ I have prevailed over him,” lest my foes rejoice because I am shaken . Psa 13:5 But I have trusted in your steadfast love ; my heart shall rejoice in your salvation . Psa 13:6 I will sing to the Lord, because he has dealt bountifully with me.

**sing** — Psa 13:6  ·  `H7891:sing#1`

_Story_

This is gratitude turning into song at the close of a hard prayer. The psalm begins almost in despair - 'How long will you forget me?' - and ends in melody: 'I will sing to the Lord, because he has dealt bountifully with me.' The verse never lodges this in a particular place inside; it simply lets remembered goodness carry the whole self from complaint to song. The movement is the point: sorrow gives way to singing once the bounty is recalled.

_Reading_

This is an 'affect'-type (102) movement - gratitude become song at the end of a lament. The psalm opens in near-despair ('How long, O Lord? Will you forget me for ever?', vv1-2) and turns at v5 to trust in steadfast love, closing at v6: 'I will sing to the Lord, because he has dealt bountifully with me.' The characteristic is the affect of gratitude resolving into song. Source (103) is left empty as a field, even though the verse's own 'because' points to remembered bounty as the felt ground - the record does not formalise the cause. No seat (104): not organ-located, the whole self sings. Bearer (105) is the psalmist; operation (106) is the resolve to sing for bounty; target (107) is grateful-song; coupling (112) is 'sing-for-bounty' - song welded to the remembered dealing. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no volume, no traced consequence. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114) is the arc: the interior moves from 'how long' to melody; remembered bounty turns complaint into song. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* Across this term's readings ('sing'), this one is distinctive for its arc: it closes a lament ('How long, O Lord?') by turning remembered personal bounty into song - a descent-to-song movement. It differs from the corporate praise of God's power (Psa 21:13), the anticipatory victory-sacrifice (Psa 27:6), and the crafted 'new song' (Psa 33:3). The difference is meaningful: here song is the resolution of private complaint, not a public or festal call.

## Psa 18:43-50

> Psa 18:42 I beat them fine as dust before the wind; I cast them out like the mire of the streets. Psa 18:43 You delivered me from strife with the people ; you made me the head of the nations ; people whom I had not known served me . Psa 18:44 As soon as they heard of me they obeyed me; foreigners came cringing to me . Psa 18:45 Foreigners lost heart and came trembling out of their fortresses . Psa 18:46 The Lord lives , and blessed be my rock , and exalted be the God of my salvation — Psa 18:47 the God who gave me vengeance and subdued peoples under me , Psa 18:48 who rescued me from my enemies ; yes , you exalted me above those who rose against me; you delivered me from the man of violence . Psa 18:49 For this I will praise you, O Lord , among the nations , and sing to your name . Psa 18:50 Great salvation he brings to his king , and shows steadfast love to his anointed , to David and his offspring forever .

**praise** — Psa 18:49  ·  `H3034:prais#1`

_Story_

Here the will to praise pushes past every border. After recounting how God rescued him and set him over nations, the psalmist resolves to praise God out loud 'among the nations' and to sing to his name. Nothing in the verse names an inner cause or a place inside where this rises; it simply insists that a private rescue be told publicly, far beyond his own people. Gratitude here refuses to stay at home.

_Reading_

A 'volition'-type (102) reading: the will to broadcast praise beyond Israel. After a long recital of deliverance and dominion (vv43-48), the self resolves at v49: 'For this I will praise you, O Lord, among the nations, and sing to your name.' The characteristic is gratitude widened into international, public praise. Source (103) is absent as a field, though the whole preceding rescue is the plain occasion; no seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is the psalmist; operation (106) is to praise God among the nations and sing to his name; target (107) is proclamation. Coupling (112) is 'praise-among-nations': praising welded to its public, cross-border reach. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - no measure of degree or outcome. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): the operation carries private deliverance into public, international praise. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* Within this term's many readings (yadah, 'praise/give thanks'), this one occupies the outward, international pole - praise carried 'among the nations' after deliverance. It stands opposite the mortality-argument reading (Psa 30:9, cf. Psa 6:5) where praise is a reason to be kept alive, and differs from the future-hope refrain (Psa 42:11; 43:4) and the acclaim of a human king (Psa 45:17). The distinctive is meaningful: private rescue deliberately broadcast to the nations.

## Psa 21:1-11

> To the choirmaster . A Psalm of David . Psa 21:1 O Lord , in your strength the king rejoices , and in your salvation how greatly he exults ! Psa 21:2 You have given him his heart’s desire and have not withheld the request of his lips . Selah Psa 21:3 For you meet him with rich blessings ; you set a crown of fine gold upon his head . Psa 21:4 He asked life of you; you gave it to him, length of days forever and ever . Psa 21:5 His glory is great through your salvation ; splendor and majesty you bestow on him . Psa 21:6 For you make him most blessed forever ; you make him glad with the joy of your presence . Psa 21:7 For the king trusts in the Lord , and through the steadfast love of the Most High he shall not be moved . Psa 21:8 Your hand will find out all your enemies ; your right hand will find out those who hate you . Psa 21:9 You will make them as a blazing oven when you appear . The Lord will swallow them up in his wrath , and fire will consume them. Psa 21:10 You will destroy their descendants from the earth , and their offspring from among the children of man . Psa 21:11 Though they plan evil against you, though they devise mischief , they will not succeed . Psa 21:13 Be exalted , O Lord , in your strength ! We will sing and praise your power .

**sing** — Psa 21:13  ·  `H7891:sing#2`

_Story_

Praise here is the whole people answering a victory with song. The psalm has celebrated all that God gave the king, and it ends with the community turning to God himself: 'Be exalted... we will sing and praise your power.' The verse names no inner trigger and locates the singing in no particular part; it simply lifts God's strength into song. What the many do together is take the triumph and hand it back to God as praise.

_Reading_

An 'affect'-type (102) reading whose bearer is the people, not the individual. The royal psalm celebrates the king's God-given blessings and coming victory (vv1-12) and closes at v13: 'Be exalted, O Lord, in your strength! We will sing and praise your power.' The characteristic is corporate worship of the victorious God. Source (103) is absent; no seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is the people; operation (106) is their singing and praising of God's power together with a call for God to be exalted; target (107) is praise. Coupling (112) is 'sing-of-power': song welded to God's might as its content. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - the verse names no volume or result. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): the operation closes the psalm by turning victory into song directed at God's strength. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* Among this term's readings, this one is set apart by its bearer - the people, corporate rather than individual - and by its content, God's power and strength, as it closes a royal victory psalm. It differs from the personal gratitude of Psa 13:6, the individual anticipatory worship of Psa 27:6, and the crafted new song of Psa 33:3. The shift to communal praise of divine might is meaningful.

## Psa 27:1-9

> Of David . Psa 27:1 The Lord is my light and my salvation ; whom shall I fear ? The Lord is the stronghold of my life ; of whom shall I be afraid ? Psa 27:2 When evildoers assail me to eat up my flesh , my adversaries and foes , it is they who stumble and fall . Psa 27:3 Though an army encamp against me, my heart shall not fear ; though war arise against me, yet I will be confident . Psa 27:4 One thing have I asked of the Lord , that will I seek after: that I may dwell in the house of the Lord all the days of my life , to gaze upon the beauty of the Lord and to inquire in his temple . Psa 27:5 For he will hide me in his shelter in the day of trouble ; he will conceal me under the cover of his tent ; he will lift me high upon a rock . Psa 27:6 And now my head shall be lifted up above my enemies all around me, and I will offer in his tent sacrifices with shouts of joy; I will sing and make melody to the Lord . Psa 27:7 Hear , O Lord , when I cry aloud ; be gracious to me and answer me ! Psa 27:8 You have said, “ Seek my face .” My heart says to you, “Your face , Lord , do I seek .” Psa 27:9 Hide not your face from me. Turn not your servant away in anger , O you who have been my help . Cast me not off; forsake me not, O God of my salvation !

**sing** — Psa 27:6  ·  `H7891:sing#3`

_Story_

This is joy breaking out in worship before the danger has even passed. Sure that God will hide and lift him, the psalmist already sees his head raised above his enemies, and answers with loud shouts of joy, sacrifice, and song. The verse does not say what stirred it or where inside it sits; it simply erupts. Confidence turns straight into loud, glad music offered to God.

_Reading_

An 'affect'-type (102) reading of exultant, anticipatory worship. Confident amid threat ('though an army encamp against me, my heart shall not fear', v3) and longing to dwell in God's house (v4), the self foresees rescue and at v6 responds: 'my head shall be lifted up above my enemies... I will offer... sacrifices with shouts of joy; I will sing and make melody to the Lord.' The characteristic is praise erupting before the victory is in hand. Source (103) is absent as a field, though the anticipated lifting is the occasion; no seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is the psalmist; operation (106) is offering sacrifice with shouts and singing; target (107) is joyful-praise. Coupling (112) is 'shouts-and-song': loud shout welded to melody. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - though the 'shouts of joy' carry a loudness the intensity-field does not formally record. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): the lifted head turns to loud, musical joy - exultant worship anticipating deliverance. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is distinctive for its anticipatory, cultic setting: praise erupting in sacrifice and shouts before the victory is secured, from a self confident under threat. It differs from the gratitude-after-rescue of Psa 13:6, the corporate power-praise of Psa 21:13, and the crafted new song of Psa 33:3. The difference - praise that precedes rather than follows deliverance - is meaningful.

## Psa 29:1-5

> A Psalm of David . Psa 29:1 Ascribe to the Lord , O heavenly beings , ascribe to the Lord glory and strength . Psa 29:2 Ascribe to the Lord the glory due his name ; worship the Lord in the splendor of holiness . Psa 29:3 The voice of the Lord is over the waters ; the God of glory thunders , the Lord , over many waters . Psa 29:4 The voice of the Lord is powerful ; the voice of the Lord is full of majesty . Psa 29:5 The voice of the Lord breaks the cedars ; the Lord breaks the cedars of Lebanon .

**Ascribe** — Psa 29:1  ·  `H3051:ascrib#1`

_Story_

Here worship is a summons to give God what is his. Before the storm of God's voice breaks over the waters, the psalm calls the heavenly beings - and every worshipper - to ascribe glory and strength to the Lord and to bow before his holiness. The command is repeated until it presses. Nothing names an inner cause or a bodily seat; the movement is simply the self being called to acknowledge how great God is and to render it back to him.

_Reading_

An 'affect'-type (102) reading: the interior summoned to render God his due. The theophany psalm opens with a threefold command - 'Ascribe to the Lord, O heavenly beings, ascribe to the Lord glory and strength... the glory due his name; worship the Lord in the splendour of holiness' (vv1-2) - before the voice of the Lord thunders over the waters (vv3-5). The characteristic is the giving-back of glory: the self called to acknowledge God's weight and bow. Source (103) is absent; no seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is the heavenly beings / worshippers; operation (106) is the summons to ascribe glory and strength and to worship in holiness; target (107) is ascription. Coupling (112) is 'ascribe-glory': worship welded to the rendering of what is due. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - no measure of degree or outcome, though the threefold repeat presses the demand. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): the operation is the giving-back of glory - the interior summoned to recognise God's weight and bow. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* This term's other readings all fall in Psalm 96, where the bearer is the families and peoples of the earth. This one differs by its bearer - the heavenly beings summoned in the theophany psalm - while the operation, the giving-back of glory, holds constant. The difference is mainly one of who is called to ascribe; the movement itself is stable across the readings.

## Psa 29:7-11

> Psa 29:7 The voice of the Lord flashes forth flames of fire . Psa 29:8 The voice of the Lord shakes the wilderness ; the Lord shakes the wilderness of Kadesh . Psa 29:9 The voice of the Lord makes the deer give birth and strips the forests bare , and in his temple all cry , “ Glory !” Psa 29:10 The Lord sits enthroned over the flood ; the Lord sits enthroned as king forever . Psa 29:11 May the Lord give strength to his people ! May the Lord bless his people with peace !

**Glory** — Psa 29:9  ·  `H3519:glory#1`

_Story_

As God's voice tears through the wilderness, splits fire, and strips the forests, the worshippers in his temple can only cry out one word: 'Glory!' This is awe that cannot hold itself in - not a composed song but a single involuntary shout. The verse does not say where inside it comes from; it simply bursts out. The overwhelming display of power leaves the self with nothing to give but that one word.

_Reading_

An 'affect'-type (102) reading: the involuntary cry wrung from the worshippers by theophany. As the voice of the Lord flashes fire, shakes the wilderness, and strips the forests bare (vv7-9a), 'in his temple all cry, Glory!' (v9b). The characteristic is awe forced up into a single word. Source (103) is absent as a field, though the shaking creation is plainly the occasion; no seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is the worshippers; operation (106) is their spontaneous exclamation as creation quakes; target (107) is exclamation. Coupling (112) is 'cry-glory': the response welded to the one word it produces. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - the verse does not measure the cry beyond its bare eruption. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): the operation is the involuntary response of the interior to the theophany - awe compressed into a single syllable of praise. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* This term (kavod) carries two very different senses across its readings. Here it is the worshippers' spontaneous corporate cry 'Glory!' at the theophany; in its sibling (Psa 57:8, 'Awake, my glory!') the same word names the psalmist's own noblest self rousing itself to praise. The gap - an outward exclamation versus the inner self called 'glory' - is large and meaningful.

## Psa 30:3-12

> A Psalm of David . A song at the dedication of the temple . Psa 30:1 I will extol you, O Lord , for you have drawn me up and have not let my foes rejoice over me . Psa 30:2 O Lord my God, I cried to you for help, and you have healed me. Psa 30:3 O Lord , you have brought up my soul from Sheol ; you restored me to life from among those who go down to the pit . Psa 30:4 Sing praises to the Lord , O you his saints , and give thanks to his holy name . Psa 30:5 For his anger is but for a moment , and his favor is for a lifetime . Weeping may tarry for the night , but joy comes with the morning . Psa 30:6 As for me , I said in my prosperity , “I shall never be moved .” Psa 30:7 By your favor , O Lord , you made my mountain stand strong ; you hid your face ; I was dismayed . Psa 30:8 To you, O Lord , I cry , and to the Lord I plead for mercy: Psa 30:9 “What profit is there in my death , if I go down to the pit ? Will the dust praise you? Will it tell of your faithfulness ? Psa 30:10 Hear , O Lord , and be merciful to me! O Lord , be my helper !” Psa 30:11 You have turned for me my mourning into dancing ; you have loosed my sackcloth and clothed me with gladness , Psa 30:12 that my glory may sing your praise and not be silent . O Lord my God , I will give thanks to you forever !

**extol** — Psa 30:1  ·  `H7311:extol#1`

_Story_

The praise here answers a rescue in kind: because God drew him up out of the depths, the psalmist will lift God high in return. Saved from the very edge of the grave and spared the mockery of his enemies, he raises God in praise. The verse names no bodily seat for this; it simply mirrors what happened to him - he was lifted, so he lifts God. The movement matches gift with gratitude.

_Reading_

An 'affect'-type (102) reading: praise that lifts God as high as the self has been lifted. The psalm opens at v1: 'I will extol you, O Lord, for you have drawn me up and have not let my foes rejoice over me,' recalling rescue from Sheol and the pit (v3). The characteristic is praise for being lifted from the depths. Source (103) is absent as a field, though the 'for you have drawn me up' gives the felt ground; no seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is the psalmist; operation (106) is extolling God for the rescue; target (107) is exalting-praise. Coupling (112) is 'extol-drawn-up': the lifting of God welded to the lifting the self received. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - no measure of degree or outcome. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): the operation lifts God high in praise, matching the lifting the self has received - the very verb mirrors the rescue. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* Among this term's readings (rum, 'extol/lift high'), this one is the most grounded in a rescue narrative - praise that lifts God high precisely because the self was drawn up from the depths. It differs from the sailors' extolling in the congregation (Psa 107:32) and the psalmist's extolling (Psa 118:28), which are less tied to a specific lifting. The mirroring of rescue and praise is the meaningful distinctive.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7696857, 7696858, 7696859, 7696860, 7696861, 7696862, 7696863, 7696864, 7696866, 7696867, 7696865</sub>

**Sing** — Psa 30:12  ·  `H2167:sing#2`

_Story_

Rescued and remade - mourning turned to dancing, grief-clothes swapped for gladness - the psalmist vows that his very self will never stop singing God's praise. The whole point of the deliverance, he says, is that he might praise and 'not be silent,' giving thanks for ever. The verse names no particular seat inside; the whole rescued self is what sings. Gratitude here becomes a lifelong pledge, a refusal ever to fall quiet.

_Reading_

A 'volition'-type (102) reading: a vow of unceasing praise. After mourning turned to dancing and sackcloth to gladness (v11), the self declares at v12 the purpose of it all: 'that my glory may sing your praise and not be silent. O Lord my God, I will give thanks to you for ever.' The characteristic is the interior binding itself to perpetual praise. Source (103) is absent as a field, though the transformation of v11 is the occasion; no seat (104) - not organ-located; the 'glory' that sings is the self, not a named faculty; bearer (105) is the psalmist; operation (106) is the vow that its glory will sing and not fall silent, giving thanks for ever; target (107) is unceasing-praise. Coupling (112) is 'not-be-silent': praise welded to the refusal of silence. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - the one measure is duration ('for ever'), carried in the sense rather than the intensity-field. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): the rescued self will not fall silent; it binds itself to praise without end. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* Within this term's readings, this one is distinctive as a vow of perpetual, unceasing praise - the rescued self binding itself never to fall silent, giving thanks 'for ever.' It differs from the outward proclamation of Psa 9:11, the corporate summons of the same psalm (Psa 30:4), and the fivefold call of Psa 47:6. The move from a single act to a lifelong pledge is meaningful.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7696956, 7696957, 7696958, 7696959, 7696960, 7696961, 7696962, 7696963, 7696965, 7696966, 7696964</sub>

**Sing** — Psa 30:4  ·  `H2167:sing#3`

_Story_

His own healing will not stay his own. Lifted from the edge of death, the psalmist immediately turns to God's faithful people and calls them to join him: 'Sing praises to the Lord... give thanks to his holy name.' The verse names no inner cause or bodily seat; it simply reaches outward. What God did for one person becomes a reason for the whole community to sing.

_Reading_

A 'volition'-type (102) reading: private healing widened into a corporate summons. Mid-psalm, having been brought up from Sheol (v3), the self turns to the community at v4: 'Sing praises to the Lord, O you his saints, and give thanks to his holy name.' The characteristic is the will to draw others into thanksgiving. Source (103) is absent as a field, though the personal healing is the occasion; no seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is the saints who are summoned; operation (106) is the call to God's saints to sing and give thanks to his holy name; target (107) is summoning-praise. Coupling (112) is 'saints-sing': the summons welded to the community's response. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - no measure of degree or outcome. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): the operation widens the private healing into a corporate summons to praise. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is set apart by its bearer - the saints/community summoned - widening a private healing into corporate thanksgiving. It sits beside its own-psalm twin (Psa 30:12), which is instead a personal vow of unending praise, and differs from the nations-facing proclamation of Psa 9:11. The distinctive is the outward, communal reach within the same rescue psalm.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7696879, 7696880, 7696881, 7696882, 7696883, 7696884, 7696885, 7696886, 7696888, 7696889, 7696887</sub>

**praise** — Psa 30:9  ·  `H3034:prais#2`

_Story_

As in Psalm 6, praise here becomes a bargaining chip for staying alive. Facing the pit, the psalmist reasons: what good is my death to you? Can dust praise you or tell of your faithfulness? Nothing names what set the thought going except the fear of dying. The logic reveals what he treasures most - he wants to live precisely so that he can go on praising, staying among the living who worship.

_Reading_

A 'cognition'-type (102) reading: praise wielded as an argument for life, twin to Psalm 6:5. In distress the self pleads at v9: 'What profit is there in my death, if I go down to the pit? Will the dust praise you? Will it tell of your faithfulness?' The characteristic is the interior reasoning that its death would silence its praise, and pleading to live. Source (103) is absent - no cause named for the thought; it arises from the fear of the pit, uncaused within the self. No seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is the psalmist; operation (106) is the argued case; target (107) is mortality-argument. Coupling (112) is 'dust-cannot-praise': praise welded to life, so that death would end it. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - the record is the reasoning, not its volume or outcome. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): the operation pleads for life by the desire to keep praising; the interior wants to remain among the worshippers. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is the mirror-opposite of the outward, international praise elsewhere in this term (e.g. Psa 18:49): here praise is turned into a mortality-argument - 'Will the dust praise you?' - used as a reason to be kept alive, twin to Psalm 6:5. Against the future-hope refrains (Psa 42:11; 43:4) and the king's acclaim (Psa 45:17), this cognition-type plea is a distinct and meaningful pole: praise as survival-argument, not act.

## Psa 33:1-6

> Psa 33:1 Shout for joy in the Lord , O you righteous ! Praise befits the upright . Psa 33:2 Give thanks to the Lord with the lyre ; make melody to him with the harp of ten strings ! Psa 33:3 Sing to him a new song ; play skillfully on the strings, with loud shouts . Psa 33:4 For the word of the Lord is upright , and all his work is done in faithfulness . Psa 33:5 He loves righteousness and justice ; the earth is full of the steadfast love of the Lord . Psa 33:6 By the word of the Lord the heavens were made , and by the breath of his mouth all their host .

**sing** — Psa 33:3  ·  `H7891:sing#4`

_Story_

Praise here is meant to be fresh and finely made, never routine. The psalm calls the upright to take up lyre and harp and sing God a brand-new song, played skilfully, with loud shouts. The verse names no inner trigger or seat; it simply demands craft and newness. Worship, it insists, deserves invention and skill, not a worn-out repetition.

_Reading_

An 'affect'-type (102) reading: fresh, crafted praise. The psalm calls the upright to shout for joy and give thanks with lyre and harp (vv1-2), then at v3: 'Sing to him a new song; play skilfully on the strings, with loud shouts.' The characteristic is inventive, well-made praise. Source (103) is absent; no seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is the worshippers; operation (106) is the summons to a new song, skilfully played with loud shouts; target (107) is fresh-praise. Coupling (112) is 'new-song-skilful': novelty welded to craft. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - though 'loud shouts' and 'skilfully' hint at a texture the fields do not formally hold. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): the interior brings craft and freshness, not rote - praise is to be newly made and well made. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* Among this term's readings, this one is distinctive for craft and freshness - a 'new song,' skilfully played, with loud shouts. It differs from the gratitude-after-rescue of Psa 13:6, the corporate power-praise of Psa 21:13, and the anticipatory victory-worship of Psa 27:6. The emphasis on invention and skill, rather than occasion, is the meaningful distinctive.

## Psa 34:1-22

> Of David , when he changed his behavior before Abimelech , so that he drove him out , and he went away . Psa 34:1 I will bless the Lord at all times ; his praise shall continually be in my mouth . Psa 34:2 My soul makes its boast in the Lord ; let the humble hear and be glad . Psa 34:3 Oh, magnify the Lord with me, and let us exalt his name together ! Psa 34:4 I sought the Lord , and he answered me and delivered me from all my fears . Psa 34:5 Those who look to him are radiant , and their faces shall never be ashamed . Psa 34:6 This poor man cried , and the Lord heard him and saved him out of all his troubles . Psa 34:7 The angel of the Lord encamps around those who fear him, and delivers them . Psa 34:8 Oh, taste and see that the Lord is good ! Blessed is the man who takes refuge in him ! Psa 34:9 Oh, fear the Lord , you his saints , for those who fear him have no lack ! Psa 34:10 The young lions suffer want and hunger ; but those who seek the Lord lack no good thing. Psa 34:11 Come , O children , listen to me; I will teach you the fear of the Lord . Psa 34:12 What man is there who desires life and loves many days , that he may see good ? Psa 34:13 Keep your tongue from evil and your lips from speaking deceit . Psa 34:14 Turn away from evil and do good ; seek peace and pursue it . Psa 34:15 The eyes of the Lord are toward the righteous and his ears toward their cry . Psa 34:16 The face of the Lord is against those who do evil , to cut off the memory of them from the earth . Psa 34:17 When the righteous cry for help , the Lord hears and delivers them out of all their troubles . Psa 34:18 The Lord is near to the brokenhearted and saves the crushed in spirit . Psa 34:19 Many are the afflictions of the righteous , but the Lord delivers him out of them all . Psa 34:20 He keeps all his bones ; not one of them is broken . Psa 34:21 Affliction will slay the wicked , and those who hate the righteous will be condemned . Psa 34:22 The Lord redeems the life of his servants ; none of those who take refuge in him will be condemned .

**magnify** — Psa 34:3  ·  `H1431:magnify#1`

_Story_

This praise reaches for company. Having resolved to bless God at all times, the psalmist cannot enlarge God alone: 'magnify the Lord with me, and let us exalt his name together.' The verse names no bodily seat for the impulse; it simply wants others alongside. Making God great, here, is something the self longs to do shoulder to shoulder with a crowd.

_Reading_

A 'volition'-type (102) reading: the will to enlarge God in company. The psalm resolves to bless the Lord at all times (v1) and boasts in him (v2), then reaches out at v3: 'Oh, magnify the Lord with me, and let us exalt his name together!' The characteristic is praise made communal - the interior wanting company in enlarging God. Source (103) is absent as a field, though the personal deliverance from fear (v4) is the occasion; no seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is the psalmist; operation (106) is the call to others to magnify and exalt God's name together; target (107) is summoning-praise. Coupling (112) is 'magnify-together': the enlarging of God welded to shared company. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - no measure of degree or outcome. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): the interior wants company in enlarging God - praise reaches out for others. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* This term's two readings both enlarge God, but differ in reach. Here (Psa 34:3) the magnifying is communal - 'with me... together,' wanting company. In its sibling (Psa 69:30) it is an individual act, magnifying God with thanksgiving. The outward, gathering impulse is this reading's meaningful distinctive.

## Psa 40:1-17

> To the choirmaster . A Psalm of David . Psa 40:1 I waited patiently for the Lord ; he inclined to me and heard my cry . Psa 40:2 He drew me up from the pit of destruction , out of the miry bog , and set my feet upon a rock , making my steps secure . Psa 40:3 He put a new song in my mouth , a song of praise to our God . Many will see and fear , and put their trust in the Lord . Psa 40:4 Blessed is the man who makes the Lord his trust , who does not turn to the proud , to those who go astray after a lie ! Psa 40:5 You have multiplied , O Lord my God , your wondrous deeds and your thoughts toward us; none can compare with you! I will proclaim and tell of them, yet they are more than can be told . Psa 40:6 In sacrifice and offering you have not delighted , but you have given me an open ear . Burnt offering and sin offering you have not required . Psa 40:7 Then I said , “ Behold , I have come ; in the scroll of the book it is written of me : Psa 40:8 I delight to do your will , O my God ; your law is within my heart .” Psa 40:9 I have told the glad news of deliverance in the great congregation ; behold , I have not restrained my lips , as you know , O Lord . Psa 40:10 I have not hidden your deliverance within my heart ; I have spoken of your faithfulness and your salvation ; I have not concealed your steadfast love and your faithfulness from the great congregation . Psa 40:11 As for you , O Lord , you will not restrain your mercy from me; your steadfast love and your faithfulness will ever preserve me ! Psa 40:12 For evils have encompassed me beyond number ; my iniquities have overtaken me, and I cannot see ; they are more than the hairs of my head ; my heart fails me . Psa 40:13 Be pleased , O Lord , to deliver me! O Lord , make haste to help me! Psa 40:14 Let those be put to shame and disappointed altogether who seek to snatch away my life ; let those be turned back and brought to dishonor who delight in my hurt ! Psa 40:15 Let those be appalled because of their shame who say to me, “ Aha , Aha !” Psa 40:16 But may all who seek you rejoice and be glad in you; may those who love your salvation say continually , “ Great is the Lord !” Psa 40:17 As for me , I am poor and needy , but the Lord takes thought for me. You are my help and my deliverer ; do not delay , O my God !

**praise** — Psa 40:3  ·  `H8416:prais#1`

_Story_

A rescued song here becomes contagious. Lifted out of the miry pit and set on solid rock, the psalmist finds a new song of praise put in his mouth - and as others watch, they come to fear God and trust him too. The verse does not lodge this in any bodily seat; it simply lets one person's testimony ripple outward. His deliverance, turned to song, draws strangers into the same trust.

_Reading_

An 'affect'-type (102) reading: praise that spreads trust. Drawn up from the pit and set on a rock (vv1-2), the self receives at v3: 'He put a new song in my mouth, a song of praise to our God. Many will see and fear, and put their trust in the Lord.' The characteristic is rescued praise that propagates faith in others. Source (103) is absent as a field, though God's own act ('he put') is the plain cause; no seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is the psalmist; operation (106) is the new song of praise placed in the mouth, moving many to fear and trust; target (107) is contagious-praise. Coupling (112) is 'new-song': praise welded to its newness. Manner (108), intensity (109) and specifier (110) are silent; notably effect (111) is also unrecorded even though the verse describes an outcome (many trust) - the field is left empty though the sense carries the propagation. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114): the interior's rescued song moves others to reverent trust - testimony that spreads. Locus (116) is internal:ib-state.

*Why this reading differs:* Across this term's many readings (tehillah), this one is distinctive for its propagating outcome - a rescued 'new song' that moves many others to fear and trust God. It differs from the straightforward render-praise readings (e.g. Psa 100:4; 102:21) and the continual personal praise of Psalm 71. Praise as contagious testimony is the meaningful distinctive.

## Psa 42:1-11

> To the choirmaster . A Maskil of the Sons of Korah . Psa 42:1 As a deer pants for flowing streams , so pants my soul for you, O God . Psa 42:2 My soul thirsts for God , for the living God . When shall I come and appear before God ? Psa 42:3 My tears have been my food day and night , while they say to me all the day long , “Where is your God ?” Psa 42:4 These things I remember , as I pour out my soul : how I would go with the throng and lead them in procession to the house of God with glad shouts and songs of praise, a multitude keeping festival . Psa 42:5 Why are you cast down, O my soul , and why are you in turmoil within me? Hope in God ; for I shall again praise him, my salvation Psa 42:6 and my God . My soul is cast down within me; therefore I remember you from the land of Jordan and of Hermon , from Mount Mizar . Psa 42:7 Deep calls to deep at the roar of your waterfalls ; all your breakers and your waves have gone over me. Psa 42:8 By day the Lord commands his steadfast love , and at night his song is with me, a prayer to the God of my life . Psa 42:9 I say to God , my rock : “ Why have you forgotten me? Why do I go mourning because of the oppression of the enemy ?” Psa 42:10 As with a deadly wound in my bones , my adversaries taunt me, while they say to me all the day long, “ Where is your God ?” Psa 42:11 Why are you cast down, O my soul , and why are you in turmoil within me? Hope in God ; for I shall again praise him, my salvation and my God .

**praise** — Psa 42:11  ·  `H3034:prais#3`

_Story_

In the middle of thirst, tears, and mockery, the psalmist speaks to his own downcast heart and clings to one certainty: 'I shall again praise him.' The praise is not happening yet - it is a future he holds onto by hope, repeated like a refrain through the darkness. Nothing names what stirs it or where it sits; it stands bare, aimed at God his salvation, not tied to any other act. The psalm never resolves; it simply ends holding on to the promise of praise still to come.

_Reading_

An 'action'-type (102) reading whose distinctive is that the action is future and hoped-for, not present. Amid thirst, tears, and taunts ('Where is your God?'), the refrain answers the downcast soul at v11 (and v5, and 43:5): 'Hope in God; for I shall again praise him, my salvation and my God.' The characteristic is praise held as sure hope in the dark. Source (103) is absent; no seat (104) recorded - not organ-located; bearer (105) is David; operation (106) is to praise 'again'; target (107) is God his salvation; locus (116) is external:god - the movement directed outward toward God rather than resting as an inner state. Manner (108) is none, and coupling (112) is none [res=none]: the praise stands alone, not welded to another act and not qualified by a mode - bare, future-tense thanksgiving. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent. No prohibition (113). The recurrences (Psa 42:5, 43:5) mark this as a refrain: the same anticipated praise repeated across the twin psalms, faith clinging to a thanksgiving not yet possible. The discovery (114): the psalm ends on hope, not resolution - praise is what the self reaches for through the darkness.

*Why this reading differs:* Within this term's readings, this one is the future-tense, hoped-for praise of the twin-psalm refrain - 'I shall again praise him' - held by faith in unresolved distress, and directed outward toward God rather than resting as an inner state. It differs from the outward international praise (Psa 18:49), the mortality-argument (Psa 30:9), and the altar-vow with the lyre (Psa 43:4). The distinctive - praise as the object of hope rather than a present act - is meaningful, underscored by its recurrence at Psa 42:5 and 43:5.

*Recurs at:* Psa 42:5, Psa 43:5

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7649781, 7649782, 7649783, 7649784, 7649785, 7649786, 7649787, 7649788, 7649790, 7649791, 7649789</sub>

**songs** — Psa 42:4  ·  `H8426:song#1`

_Story_

Here the songs of thanksgiving are not something happening now but something remembered and missed. Cut off and taunted with 'Where is your God?', the psalmist recalls how he used to walk with the festive crowd to God's house amid glad shouts and songs of praise. The verse records no act of singing in the present and no one being praised right now - and that emptiness is exactly the ache: the praise lives only as a memory, paired with the remembered shouts of the crowd. What the longing reveals is how far he now feels from the worship he once shared.

_Reading_

This is a 'status'-type (102) reading in which much is recorded as absent, and the absences carry the meaning. The passage is a lament of thirst and taunting ('Where is your God?'), and at v4 the self pours itself out remembering 'how I would go with the throng... to the house of God with glad shouts and songs of praise.' The characteristic is not an act being performed but a remembered condition - thanksgiving-song (todah) as a past state now lost. Operation (106) is explicitly none, and target (107) is none: the verse records no present doing and no present object, because the singing is not happening - it is remembered, not enacted. That double silence is the point: the praise exists only as memory. Source (103), seat (104), manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all silent too; the one positive relation is the coupling (112), a pairing with the 'glad shouts' of the same festal scene - song and shout remembered together. Bearer (105) is the festal throng. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114) is that the remembered thanksgiving is the very joy the exile aches to rejoin; the absence of a present operation measures the distance between where the self is and where its praise belongs. Locus (116) is external:god.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7649656, 7649657, 7649658, 7649659, 7649660, 7649661, 7649662, 7649663, 7649665, 7649666, 7649664</sub>

## Psa 43:1-5

> Psa 43:1 Vindicate me, O God , and defend my cause against an ungodly people , from the deceitful and unjust man deliver me ! Psa 43:2 For you are the God in whom I take refuge ; why have you rejected me? Why do I go about mourning because of the oppression of the enemy ? Psa 43:3 Send out your light and your truth ; let them lead me; let them bring me to your holy hill and to your dwelling ! Psa 43:4 Then I will go to the altar of God , to God my exceeding joy , and I will praise you with the lyre , O God , my God . Psa 43:5 Why are you cast down , O my soul , and why are you in turmoil within me? Hope in God ; for I shall again praise him, my salvation and my God .

**praise** — Psa 43:4  ·  `H3034:prais#4`

_Story_

Longing to be led back to God's holy hill, the psalmist vows what he will do when he arrives: go to the altar, to the God who is his exceeding joy, and praise him with the lyre. The praise is still ahead of him - a promise made far from the sanctuary. Nothing names a seat inside for it, but here it comes with an instrument in hand. The pilgrimage he hopes for ends, in his mind, in music before God.

_Reading_

An 'action'-type (102) reading: the vow of restored worship at the altar. Pleading for vindication and for God's light and truth to lead him back (vv1-3), the self vows at v4: 'Then I will go to the altar of God, to God my exceeding joy, and I will praise you with the lyre, O God, my God.' The characteristic is anticipated thanksgiving located at the altar. Source (103) is absent; no seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is David; operation (106) is to praise; target (107) is God ('O God, my God'); manner (108) is recorded here - 'with the lyre,' giving this reading a concrete instrument. Coupling (112) is none [res=none]: the praise is not welded to another act. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent. No prohibition (113). Locus (116) is external:god - directed toward God at his altar. The discovery (114): the joy of the pilgrimage issues in song; the vow anticipates the refrain's praise (v5).

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is the altar-located twin of the refrain (Psa 42:11): a vow to praise God at his altar 'with the lyre' once his light leads the self back. It differs from that bare refrain by naming a concrete instrument and cultic setting, and from the international praise (Psa 18:49) and the mortality-argument (Psa 30:9). The difference is meaningful: anticipated worship given a place and an instrument.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7649884, 7649885, 7649886, 7649887, 7649888, 7649889, 7649890, 7649891, 7649893, 7649894, 7649892</sub>

## Psa 45:1-15

> To the choirmaster : according to Lilies . A Maskil of the Sons of Korah ; a love song . Psa 45:1 My heart overflows with a pleasing theme ; I address my verses to the king ; my tongue is like the pen of a ready scribe . Psa 45:2 You are the most handsome of the sons of men ; grace is poured upon your lips ; therefore God has blessed you forever . Psa 45:3 Gird your sword on your thigh , O mighty one , in your splendor and majesty ! Psa 45:4 In your majesty ride out victoriously for the cause of truth and meekness and righteousness ; let your right hand teach you awesome deeds ! Psa 45:5 Your arrows are sharp in the heart of the king’s enemies ; the peoples fall under you. Psa 45:6 Your throne , O God , is forever and ever . The scepter of your kingdom is a scepter of uprightness ; Psa 45:7 you have loved righteousness and hated wickedness . Therefore God , your God , has anointed you with the oil of gladness beyond your companions ; Psa 45:8 your robes are all fragrant with myrrh and aloes and cassia . From ivory palaces stringed instruments make you glad ; Psa 45:9 daughters of kings are among your ladies of honor ; at your right hand stands the queen in gold of Ophir . Psa 45:10 Hear , O daughter , and consider , and incline your ear : forget your people and your father’s house , Psa 45:11 and the king will desire your beauty . Since he is your lord , bow to him . Psa 45:12 The people of Tyre will seek your favor with gifts , the richest of the people . Psa 45:13 All glorious is the princess in her chamber , with robes interwoven with gold . Psa 45:14 In many-colored robes she is led to the king , with her virgin companions following behind her . Psa 45:15 With joy and gladness they are led along as they enter the palace of the king . Psa 45:17 I will cause your name to be remembered in all generations ; therefore nations will praise you forever and ever .

**praise** — Psa 45:17  ·  `H3034:prais#5`

_Story_

Unusually, the praise here is aimed not at God but at a human king. The wedding song has celebrated the king's splendour, throne and majesty, and it ends with the poet promising to keep the king's name alive through every generation, so that the nations will praise him for ever. The verse names no inner cause and no place inside where this sits; it simply binds the endless acclaim to the remembered name - the more the name is kept alive, the more the peoples praise. The one thing it measures is duration: this praise is meant to last for ever.

_Reading_

This 'action'-type (102) reading is distinctive in the whole family because its target (107) is not God but the king - locus (116) external:person. The royal wedding psalm celebrates the king's beauty, throne and majesty, and closes at v17 with the poet's vow to make the king's name remembered so that 'nations will praise you for ever and ever.' The characteristic is the acclaim of the peoples directed at the king. Source (103) is absent; no seat (104) - not organ-located; bearer (105) is the nations; operation (106) is praise; manner (108) is 'for ever and ever,' giving the one recorded qualifier - perpetual duration. The coupling (112) is a pairing with the remembered name: praise welded to the immortalising of the king's name, so that memory and acclaim reinforce each other. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - no degree or consequence beyond the endless acclaim itself. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114) is that this praise is the royal song's enduring end: the poet's craft (making the name remembered) generates the nations' perpetual praise. Read without valence-filtering, it is worth noting the object here is a human king, not God - the same praising movement aimed at a person.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is unique in the family for its target: the praise is directed not at God but at a human king - the nations' perpetual acclaim of the one whose name the poet immortalises. It stands apart from the God-directed readings, whether outward proclamation (Psa 18:49), mortality-argument (Psa 30:9), or refrain-hope (Psa 42:11). The redirection of the same praising movement toward a person is a striking and meaningful distinctive.

## Psa 47:1-9

> To the choirmaster . A Psalm of the Sons of Korah . Psa 47:1 Clap your hands , all peoples ! Shout to God with loud songs of joy ! Psa 47:2 For the Lord , the Most High , is to be feared , a great king over all the earth . Psa 47:3 He subdued peoples under us, and nations under our feet . Psa 47:4 He chose our heritage for us, the pride of Jacob whom he loves . Selah Psa 47:5 God has gone up with a shout , the Lord with the sound of a trumpet . Psa 47:6 Sing praises to God , sing praises! Sing praises to our King , sing praises! Psa 47:7 For God is the King of all the earth ; sing praises with a psalm ! Psa 47:8 God reigns over the nations ; God sits on his holy throne . Psa 47:9 The princes of the peoples gather as the people of the God of Abraham . For the shields of the earth belong to God ; he is highly exalted !

**Sing** — Psa 47:6  ·  `H2167:sing#4`

_Story_

This song of praise has a clear cause: it rises because God has chosen and loved his people and reigns as king over all the earth. The psalm calls every nation to clap and shout, and then drives the point home with an almost breathless, fivefold command to sing praises to God the King. The force here is in the repetition - the same call pressed over and over on the worshippers. Nothing names a bodily place for it; it is the whole gathered assembly lifting the same insistent praise.

_Reading_

This 'action'-type (102) reading is unusually well-furnished: unlike its siblings, it records a source (103) - the singing is prompted by God's choosing and loving of his people (v4), so the praise is caused, not self-arising. The passage summons all peoples to clap and shout (v1), grounds it in God's subduing of nations and choice of Jacob's heritage (vv3-4), and then at v6 issues the insistent command: 'Sing praises to God, sing praises! Sing praises to our King, sing praises!' The characteristic is this commanded, enthronement praise. Bearer (105) is the worshippers; operation (106) is to sing praise; target (107) is God our King. The manner (108) is a pairing - emphatically, fivefold repeated - and the coupling (112) is another pairing capturing that same repetition across vv6-7; together they mark insistence as the texture of this praise. No seat (104): not organ-located, the whole congregation sings. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent - the repetition itself carries the force the intensity-field does not name. No prohibition (113). The discovery (114) is the fivefold call: worship pressed again and again on the assembly, due to the God who both chose and reigns. Locus (116) is external:god.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is unusually furnished among the term's readings: it names a source - the singing is prompted by God's choosing and loving of his people - where the others leave the cause unstated, and its texture is an insistent fivefold repetition. It differs from the outward proclamation (Psa 9:11), the perpetual vow (Psa 30:12), and the corporate summons (Psa 30:4). The caused, enthronement-praise with its hammered repetition is the meaningful distinctive.

## Psa 48:1-2

> A Song . A Psalm of the Sons of Korah . Psa 48:1 Great is the Lord and greatly to be praised in the city of our God ! His holy mountain , Psa 48:2 beautiful in elevation , is the joy of all the earth , Mount Zion , in the far north , the city of the great King . Psa 48:3 Within her citadels God has made himself known as a fortress.

**Praise** — Psa 48:1  ·  `H1984:prais#1`

_Story_

Praise here is less something the worshippers do than a fact they recognise: God is great, and so he is greatly to be praised. What draws it out is what God has shown himself to be — a stronghold made known within his own city. The whole people, not a lone heart, lift this praise, and they lift it lavishly, in the place set apart for worship. Nothing in the verse locates the praise in a particular part of a person; it simply rises outward toward God as the fitting answer to his greatness.

_Reading_

Here praise (halal) is registered as a status (102), not a discrete act: 'greatly to be PRAISED' names God as one whose worth stands as a settled fact, and the inner movement is the answering acknowledgement of that worth. Its source (103) is paired to God's self-revelation as a fortress made known within Zion (v3) — the praise is not self-generated but caused by, and grounded in, what God has shown himself to be. The operation (106) is praise; the bearer (105) is inferred as the worshippers, his people, so this is a communal rather than a private movement; the target (107) is the LORD. The manner (108) is 'greatly, in the city of God' — praise sized to the greatness confessed and located in the place of worship. There is no seat (104 none): praise is not lodged in a named organ or faculty, which fits its character here as the shared act of a people rather than an interior organ-event. Intensity (109) has no row of its own though the manner-word 'greatly' already carries the weight; specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) are all absent, so the verse traces no downstream consequence and no forbidding. Coupling (112 none) means the verse does not weld this praise to a twin movement; it stands as the direct response of a people to God's disclosed worth. The locus (116) is external:god — the movement reaches outward toward God as its arena.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is a status (God as worthy of praise), carried by the whole worshipping people and anchored to a stated ground — God revealed as a fortress in Zion. That sets it apart from the Psa 56 siblings of the same item, where halal is the psalmist's own act of glorying in God's word, with no stated ground, doubled as a personal refrain. The shift from communal status-with-source to private act-without-source is meaningful: here praise answers a public disclosure of God, there it is the individual's defiant confidence.

## Psa 48:8-14

> Psa 48:8 As we have heard , so have we seen in the city of the Lord of hosts , in the city of our God , which God will establish forever . Selah Psa 48:9 We have thought on your steadfast love , O God , in the midst of your temple . Psa 48:10 As your name , O God , so your praise reaches to the ends of the earth . Your right hand is filled with righteousness . Psa 48:11 Let Mount Zion be glad ! Let the daughters of Judah rejoice because of your judgments ! Psa 48:12 Walk about Zion , go around her, number her towers , Psa 48:13 consider well her ramparts , go through her citadels , that you may tell the next generation Psa 48:14 that this is God , our God forever and ever . He will guide us forever .

**praise** — Psa 48:10  ·  `H8416:prais#2`

_Story_

This praise is pictured not as something being done in the moment but as a fact stretching as wide as God's own reputation — as far as his name is known, so far his praise reaches. It rises out of the worshippers turning over his faithful love in their minds within his temple. The verse does not show them performing it or aiming it at anyone; it simply describes praise that fills the earth to its edges, as fitting to God as his own name. Nothing measures it or names what it leads to — it is left to stand as a reality as broad as the world.

_Reading_

Praise (tehillah) is again a status (102): 'as your name, so your PRAISE reaches to the ends of the earth' — the praise is spoken of as a spreading reality that matches the scope of God's name, not primarily as a verb being performed. Its source (103) is paired to the worshippers' meditation on God's steadfast love in the midst of the temple (v9); the praise wells up out of that inward dwelling on his chesed. Strikingly, both operation (106 none) and target (107 none) are explicitly found absent by the reader: no act-verb and no aimed direction are recorded, which reads the praise here as a state co-extensive with God's name rather than a directed action — praise that simply reaches, as far as his renown reaches. The bearer (105) is the worshippers, whose praise this is; there is no seat (104 none), so it is not organ-located but a corporate condition. The manner (108) is 'to the ends of the earth' — its measure is geographic reach, its worth matched to his righteousness-filled right hand. Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111), prohibition (113) and coupling (112 none) are all absent: the verse neither grades the praise, nor traces a result, nor welds it to a paired movement — it lets the praise stand as a fact as wide as the world. The locus (116) is external:god.

*Why this reading differs:* Uniquely among the tehillah siblings, this reading records no act and no aim — praise is a spreading status, not a directed doing — while its neighbour at Psa 51:15 shows the mouth actively declaring praise to God, and Psa 40:3 has God putting a fresh song of praise in the mouth. The difference is meaningful: here praise is described as a condition as wide as God's name, elsewhere it is a concrete utterance from a specific speaker.

## Psa 49:5-20

> Psa 49:5 Why should I fear in times of trouble , when the iniquity of those who cheat me surrounds me , Psa 49:6 those who trust in their wealth and boast of the abundance of their riches ? Psa 49:7 Truly no man can ransom another , or give to God the price of his life , Psa 49:8 for the ransom of their life is costly and can never suffice , Psa 49:9 that he should live on forever and never see the pit . Psa 49:10 For he sees that even the wise die ; the fool and the stupid alike must perish and leave their wealth to others . Psa 49:11 Their graves are their homes forever , their dwelling places to all generations , though they called lands by their own names . Psa 49:12 Man in his pomp will not remain ; he is like the beasts that perish . Psa 49:13 This is the path of those who have foolish confidence; yet after them people approve of their boasts . Selah Psa 49:14 Like sheep they are appointed for Sheol ; death shall be their shepherd , and the upright shall rule over them in the morning . Their form shall be consumed in Sheol , with no place to dwell . Psa 49:15 But God will ransom my soul from the power of Sheol , for he will receive me. Selah Psa 49:16 Be not afraid when a man becomes rich , when the glory of his house increases . Psa 49:17 For when he dies he will carry nothing away; his glory will not go down after him. Psa 49:18 For though, while he lives , he counts himself blessed — and though you get praise when you do well for yourself — Psa 49:19 his soul will go to the generation of his fathers , who will never again see light . Psa 49:20 Man in his pomp yet without understanding is like the beasts that perish .

**praise** — Psa 49:18  ·  `H3034:prais#6`

_Story_

This is praise gone wrong, and it is worth looking at squarely. People applaud a man because he has done well for himself — the crowd's easy approval of anyone who prospers. Nothing prompts this praise beyond success itself; it simply springs up, aimed at the man and at his self-serving gain rather than at God. The verse gives it no weight, no depth, and no result on purpose: this is flattery that cannot follow him past the grave. Behind it lies the man's own quiet self-congratulation, and the crowd's cheer is merely the outward face of that inward pride.

_Reading_

This is a dark, self-referring instance and must be read at full weight: 'though you get PRAISE (yadah) when you do well for yourself.' The type (102) is action, an actual giving of applause, but its recipient is the self-serving prosperous man, and its worth in the passage is hollow. The source (103) is explicitly absent (present:false): no ground or cause is given for this praise — it simply arises, the world's reflexive approval of success, uncaused and therefore arbitrary rather than answering any real worth. The bearer (105) is others, those who applaud the prosperous; the operation (106) is praise; the target (107) is 'the one who does well for himself' — praise bent toward a man, not toward God, and toward him precisely for his self-interested gain. There is no seat (104 none), no manner (108 none), no intensity (109), no specifier (110), no effect (111), no coupling (112 none) and no prohibition (113): the verse strips this praise of any qualifier, any traced consequence, any partner-movement — a flatness that itself says something, for the applause is weightless, going nowhere, following the man not one step past death (v19). The locus (116) is internal:ib-state, which fits: the surrounding movement is the man's inner self-congratulation ('he counts himself blessed'), and the crowd's praise is the outward face of that self-flattering state. This is praise misdirected — the same faculty that should rise to God turned into the currency of admiring the successful self.

*Why this reading differs:* This yadah reading stands apart from its siblings by direction and worth: elsewhere the same item is praise offered to God (Psa 42:11, 43:4, 67:3-5, 71:22, 99:3) or even wrung from human wrath toward him (Psa 76:10), but here the applause is bent toward a prosperous man for his own advantage and marked internal rather than God-ward. The difference is fully meaningful — it is the one place the movement is misdirected, praise turned into flattery of self-serving success.

## Psa 50:13-23

> Psa 50:13 Do I eat the flesh of bulls or drink the blood of goats ? Psa 50:14 Offer to God a sacrifice of thanksgiving , and perform your vows to the Most High , Psa 50:15 and call upon me in the day of trouble ; I will deliver you, and you shall glorify me .” Psa 50:16 But to the wicked God says : “ What right have you to recite my statutes or take my covenant on your lips ? Psa 50:17 For you hate discipline , and you cast my words behind you . Psa 50:18 If you see a thief , you are pleased with him, and you keep company with adulterers . Psa 50:19 “You give your mouth free rein for evil , and your tongue frames deceit . Psa 50:20 You sit and speak against your brother ; you slander your own mother’s son . Psa 50:21 These things you have done , and I have been silent ; you thought that I was one like yourself. But now I rebuke you and lay the charge before you. Psa 50:22 “ Mark this , then , you who forget God , lest I tear you apart , and there be none to deliver ! Psa 50:23 The one who offers thanksgiving as his sacrifice glorifies me; to one who orders his way rightly I will show the salvation of God !”

**glorify** — Psa 50:15  ·  `H3513:glorify#1`

_Story_

God tells the one in trouble: call on me, I will rescue you, and then you will honour me. The honouring is the rescued person's answer — not something with a hidden cause of its own, but the natural far end of having cried out and been delivered. It is the whole person, not some inner faculty, turning to give God his due once the danger has passed. Trouble leads to a cry, the cry to rescue, and rescue to honour handed back to God. This is exactly the response God says he is looking for.

_Reading_

Glorify (kabed) is an action (102): 'you shall GLORIFY me' — the honour a rescued person renders to God. Its source (103) is absent (present:false): no prior cause is stated for the glorifying itself, because in this clause it is set as the appointed response, the thing that will follow deliverance rather than something with its own antecedent ground. The bearer (105) is the delivered worshipper; the operation (106) is glorify; the target (107) is God. There is no seat (104 none) — the honouring is not lodged in a named organ but is the whole person's answering act — and no manner (108 none), no intensity (109), no specifier (110), no effect (111), no prohibition (113). The one welded relation is coupling (112, pair): this glorifying is bound to the sequence of calling in the day of trouble and being delivered — it is the fruit of that call-and-rescue, the far end of a movement that begins in crying out. So the inner process runs: trouble prompts a call, God delivers, and the delivered self turns that rescue into honour rendered back to him. The locus (116) is external:god — the movement completes itself by reaching toward God. The absence of a stated source here is telling: the glorifying is presented not as caused by an inner state but as owed, the response God explicitly seeks (echoed in v23).

*Why this reading differs:* Here glorifying is welded to a specific rescue — it is the fruit of calling in trouble and being delivered — whereas the siblings at Psa 86:9 and 86:12 give it a wider frame (all nations glorifying God's name, the psalmist honouring it forever) without that tight rescue-coupling. The difference is meaningful: this reading is honour born of a concrete deliverance, the others honour born of God's greatness at large.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7651521, 7651522, 7651523, 7651524, 7651525, 7651526, 7651527, 7651528, 7651530, 7651531, 7651529</sub>

**glorifies** — Psa 50:23  ·  `H3513:glorif#1`

_Story_

The one who brings thanksgiving as his offering is the one who truly honours God — and to that person God promises to show his salvation. Here the honouring and the rescue belong together: a grateful, rightly-lived life gives God his due, and God answers by delivering. It is not a feeling tucked away inside or a showy display, but simply the shape of a thankful life turned toward God. Thanksgiving becomes honour, and honour meets God's saving hand.

_Reading_

Glorify (kabed) is an action (102): 'the one who offers thanksgiving as his sacrifice GLORIFIES me.' Unlike its companion at v15, this reading carries a stated source (103, pair): the glorifying issues in God showing his salvation (v23) — the honouring and the promised deliverance are paired, so the movement is grounded in and opens onto God's saving response. The bearer (105) is the thankful worshipper; the operation (106) is glorify; the target (107) is God; there is no seat (104 none) and no manner (108 none) — the honouring is the whole right-ordered life, not an organ-act. Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) are absent. Coupling (112, pair) welds this glorifying to the thank-offering: it is the fruit of thanksgiving, the honour that a thankful and rightly-ordered life renders. So the process reads: a life ordered rightly and offering thanks honours God, and that honouring is met by God showing his salvation — worship and rescue joined at both ends. The locus (116) is external:god. The two absences worth reading are the missing seat and manner: honouring God is presented not as a feeling in a faculty nor as a styled performance but as the plain shape of a grateful, well-ordered life.
