## Psa 51:1-19

> To the choirmaster . A Psalm of David , when Nathan the prophet went to him, after he had gone in to Bathsheba . Psa 51:1 Have mercy on me, O God , according to your steadfast love ; according to your abundant mercy blot out my transgressions . Psa 51:2 Wash me thoroughly from my iniquity , and cleanse me from my sin ! Psa 51:3 For I know my transgressions , and my sin is ever before me. Psa 51:4 Against you, you only , have I sinned and done what is evil in your sight , so that you may be justified in your words and blameless in your judgment . Psa 51:5 Behold , I was brought forth in iniquity , and in sin did my mother conceive me. Psa 51:6 Behold , you delight in truth in the inward being , and you teach me wisdom in the secret heart . Psa 51:7 Purge me with hyssop , and I shall be clean ; wash me, and I shall be whiter than snow . Psa 51:8 Let me hear joy and gladness ; let the bones that you have broken rejoice . Psa 51:9 Hide your face from my sins , and blot out all my iniquities . Psa 51:10 Create in me a clean heart , O God , and renew a right spirit within me. Psa 51:11 Cast me not away from your presence , and take not your Holy Spirit from me . Psa 51:12 Restore to me the joy of your salvation , and uphold me with a willing spirit . Psa 51:13 Then I will teach transgressors your ways , and sinners will return to you. Psa 51:14 Deliver me from bloodguiltiness , O God , O God of my salvation , and my tongue will sing aloud of your righteousness . Psa 51:15 O Lord , open my lips , and my mouth will declare your praise . Psa 51:16 For you will not delight in sacrifice , or I would give it; you will not be pleased with a burnt offering . Psa 51:17 The sacrifices of God are a broken spirit ; a broken and contrite heart , O God , you will not despise . Psa 51:18 Do good to Zion in your good pleasure ; build up the walls of Jerusalem ; Psa 51:19 then will you delight in right sacrifices , in burnt offerings and whole burnt offerings; then bulls will be offered on your altar .

**sing aloud** — Psa 51:14  ·  `H7442:singaloud#1`

_Story_

Once God clears him of bloodguilt, the psalmist's tongue is set free to ring out — loud and glad — about God's righteousness. The very thing that had been stopped by guilt, his own voice, now becomes the instrument of jubilant praise, singing of the justice that rescued him. This ringing song is matched by his open mouth declaring God's praise; the two go together, one freed worship in two voices. Forgiveness opens the lips, and the opened lips can only sing.

_Reading_

Sing aloud (ranan) is an action (102), a jubilant ringing cry: 'my tongue will SING ALOUD of your righteousness.' Its source (103, pair) is God's righteousness, the very theme sung (v14) — the singing is generated by, and about, God's justice, drawn out after deliverance from bloodguilt. Unusually for this family, a seat (104) is present: the tongue — the exultant cry is located in the organ of speech, the tongue that was silenced by guilt now freed to ring out. The bearer (105) is the penitent; the operation (106) is sing aloud; the target (107, pair) is God's righteousness. The manner (108) is 'aloud, jubilantly' — the cry is loud and glad, not quiet. Coupling (112, pair) twins it with the mouth declaring God's praise (v15): singing and declaring are set as a matched pair, two voices of the one freed worship. Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) are absent. So the process runs: forgiveness of bloodguilt releases the tongue, and the freed tongue rings out God's righteousness, twinned with the mouth's declaration. The locus (116) is external:god. The one notable move is that the seat is named where most singing-items leave it blank — here the tongue is specifically the pardoned instrument, the point where guilt's silence gives way to song.

*Why this reading differs:* This ranan reading names its seat (the pardoned tongue) and its theme (God's righteousness) and twins it with the mouth's declaration, whereas its sibling at Psa 59:16 leaves the seat blank, gives no stated source, and sings instead of God's steadfast love in the morning. The difference is meaningful: here the singing is worship freed by forgiveness and located in the released tongue; there it is a morning-song of gratitude for God as refuge.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7652613, 7652614, 7652621, 7652615, 7652616, 7652617, 7652618, 7652619, 7652620, 7652623, 7652624, 7652622</sub>

**praise** — Psa 51:15  ·  `H8416:prais#3`

_Story_

The psalmist knows he cannot even praise until God enables it: 'open my lips, and my mouth will declare your praise.' The praise flows straight out of the rescue he has just asked for — God's saving righteousness lifting the weight of bloodguilt. It rises from his mouth, but only because God first unstops it; the ability to worship is itself a gift, not something he can force from himself. Forgiveness opens the lips, and out comes praise.

_Reading_

Praise (tehillah) is an action (102) here: 'my mouth will declare your PRAISE.' Its source (103, pair) is God's deliverance, salvation and righteousness (v14) — the praise is prompted by, and grounded in, the rescue from bloodguilt that precedes it. A seat (104) is present: the mouth, the opened lips — the praise is located in the organ that God must first unstop ('O Lord, open my lips'). The bearer (105) is the penitent; the operation (106) is declare / show forth, praise as public utterance; the target (107) is God. The manner (108) is 'lips opened by God' — the striking note that the very capacity to praise is given, not summoned by the self. Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111), prohibition (113) and coupling (112 none) are absent: the verse does not grade the praise, trace a consequence, or weld it to a twin movement — it stands as the plain outflow of forgiven lips. The locus (116) is external:god. The process is dependent and clear: God delivers and opens the lips, and only then does the mouth declare his praise — the inner capacity for worship is itself received, not self-produced.

*Why this reading differs:* Against the tehillah siblings this reading marks praise as an act dependent on God opening the lips, sourced in his deliverance — sharper than Psa 48:10, where praise is a wide status with no act and no aim, and than Psa 40:3, where God puts a new song of praise in the mouth. The difference carries weight: here the very power to praise is confessed as received, the point of a penitent stripped of any self-sufficiency.

## Psa 55:1-5

> To the choirmaster : with stringed instruments . A Maskil of David . Psa 55:1 Give ear to my prayer , O God , and hide not yourself from my plea for mercy ! Psa 55:2 Attend to me, and answer me; I am restless in my complaint and I moan , Psa 55:3 because of the noise of the enemy , because of the oppression of the wicked . For they drop trouble upon me, and in anger they bear a grudge against me . Psa 55:4 My heart is in anguish within me; the terrors of death have fallen upon me . Psa 55:5 Fear and trembling come upon me, and horror overwhelms me.

**trouble** — Psa 55:3  ·  `H0205:troubl#1`

_Story_

The psalmist is under attack: his enemies heap trouble on him, dropping it down like a weight, driven by an angry grudge against him. Nothing is said to lie behind their malice — it simply comes from them, hostility with no deeper cause given. The harm does not stay outside him; it sinks into his inner life, turning into the anguish of his heart and the terror of death closing in. This is malice made active, and its true damage is what it does within the man it targets.

_Reading_

This is a dark movement and is read at full weight. Trouble / mischief (aven) is a status (102): 'they drop TROUBLE upon me' — a harm-laden condition dropped down onto the psalmist. Its source (103) is absent (present:false): no cause is given behind the trouble beyond the wicked who wield it, so the malice is self-arising, traced no further than the enemies' own will — an uncaused hostility. The bearer (105) is the wicked, and the operation (106) is 'drop / bring down' upon the psalmist: the trouble is actively lowered onto him, not merely present. The target (107) is the psalmist himself. There is no seat (104 none), no manner (108 none), no intensity (109), no specifier (110), no effect-row (111) and no prohibition (113): the verse withholds any grading, styling or traced outcome, letting the bare fact of dropped harm stand. Coupling (112, pair) welds it to the harm the wicked inflict — it is bound to their angry grudge (v3), so the trouble is malice made active, joined to hostile anger. The locus (116) is internal:ib-state, and this is the key: though the wicked are the agents, the trouble registers as a state within the psalmist's inner life — it becomes the anguish of his heart and the terror of death that fall on him (vv4-5). So the process runs: causeless malice, borne by the wicked, is dropped as active harm onto the psalmist, and lands inside him as inner anguish.

*Why this reading differs:* This aven reading is malice actively dropped by the wicked onto the psalmist and landing as inner anguish, whereas the sibling at Psa 90:10 uses the same word for the trouble that fills the span of a human life, paired with toil and sorrow. The difference is meaningful: here trouble is inflicted hostility experienced within, there it is the general burden of mortal existence.

## Psa 56:1-13

> To the choirmaster : according to The Dove on Far-off Terebinths . A Miktam of David , when the Philistines seized him in Gath . Psa 56:1 Be gracious to me, O God , for man tramples on me; all day long an attacker oppresses me ; Psa 56:2 my enemies trample on me all day long, for many attack me proudly . Psa 56:3 When I am afraid , I put my trust in you. Psa 56:4 In God , whose word I praise , in God I trust ; I shall not be afraid . What can flesh do to me ? Psa 56:5 All day long they injure my cause ; all their thoughts are against me for evil . Psa 56:6 They stir up strife , they lurk ; they watch my steps , as they have waited for my life . Psa 56:7 For their crime ^ will they escape ? In wrath cast down the peoples , O God ! Psa 56:8 You have kept count of my tossings ; put my tears in your bottle . Are they not in your book ? Psa 56:9 Then my enemies will turn back in the day when I call . This I know , that God is for me . Psa 56:10 In God , whose word I praise , in the Lord , whose word I praise , Psa 56:11 in God I trust ; I shall not be afraid . What can man do to me ? Psa 56:12 I must perform my vows to you, O God ; I will render thank offerings to you . Psa 56:13 For you have delivered my soul from death , yes , my feet from falling , that I may walk before God in the light of life .

**Praise** — Psa 56:10  ·  `H1984:prais#2`

_Story_

Under attack from enemies who trample him all day, the psalmist's answer is to boast in God's word — to glory in what God has promised. Nothing outside prompts this; the praise itself is where his courage comes from, rising from within as the stance of a heart that has decided to trust. It returns here as a refrain, said before with the same words and now repeated with more force. Glorying in God's promise is not the reward of rescue already come — it is the very ground on which he refuses to be afraid.

_Reading_

Praise / boast in (halal) is an action (102): 'In God, whose word I PRAISE' — the psalmist glorying in God's word. Its source (103) is absent (present:false): no external cause is named, because this praise is itself the ground of his confidence rather than a response caused by something prior — it wells from within as the settled act of a trusting heart. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is praise / glory in; the target (107) is God, specifically his word. There is no seat (104 none), no manner (108 none), no intensity (109), no specifier (110), no effect (111) and no prohibition (113): the verse leaves the praise unqualified and untraced, a bare glorying. Coupling (112, pair) welds it to the refrain of v4, now doubled — this is the recurring confession of trust returning with added force. The locus (116) is external:god. The process reads: pressed by trampling enemies (vv1-2), the psalmist's inner answer is to glory in God's word, and that glorying is not caused by relief already given but is itself the stance from which fear is refused ('I shall not be afraid'). The absent source is significant — praise here is the root of confidence, not its fruit.

*Why this reading differs:* This halal reading is the psalmist's own act of glorying in God's word, with no stated cause and welded into a personal refrain — quite unlike the Psa 48:1 sibling, where halal is a communal status grounded in God's self-revelation in Zion. Within Psa 56 it also pairs with its twin at v10b (the covenant name) and its origin at v4, the refrain deepening as the psalm moves toward assured deliverance; the repetition itself is the meaningful movement.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7654360, 7654361, 7654362, 7654363, 7654364, 7654365, 7654366, 7654367, 7654369, 7654370, 7654368</sub>

**Praise** — Psa 56:10  ·  `H1984:prais#3`

_Story_

The psalmist repeats his boast, but this second time he uses God's personal, covenant name: 'in the LORD, whose word I praise.' It is the same glorying in God's promise, rising from within as the source of his courage rather than as a reward for anything received. By saying it twice — once naming God, once naming the LORD — he doubles the confession and leans it on the God who has bound himself by covenant. The repeated name is not idle; it deepens an assurance that fear cannot quiet.

_Reading_

Praise / boast in (halal) is an action (102): 'in the LORD, whose word I PRAISE' — the same glorying as the first half of the verse, but now voiced with the covenant name. Its source (103) is absent (present:false): as before, no prior cause is named, for the praise is the ground of confidence rather than its effect. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is praise / glory in; the target (107) is the LORD, whose word he praises. There is no seat (104 none), no manner (108 none), no intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) or prohibition (113): the glorying stands bare and ungraded. Coupling (112, pair) welds it to its twin, the doubled refrain that names first God (Elohim) and then the LORD (YHWH) — the two divine names set side by side. The locus (116) is external:god. The process is the same trusting movement as its pair, but the doubling with the covenant name intensifies it: the inner confidence now rests not only on God as God but on the LORD who has bound himself by promise. The distinction from its twin is not collapsed — the shift from Elohim to YHWH is itself the finding, the personal, covenant name deepening the assurance that fear cannot silence.

*Why this reading differs:* This is the twin of the same verse's first clause, distinguished only by the divine name — here the covenant LORD (YHWH) rather than God (Elohim) — and the pairing of the two names is what intensifies the confidence. It should not be collapsed into its sibling: the deliberate move from Elohim to YHWH is the meaningful shift, leaning the trust on God's covenant self.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7654371, 7654372, 7654373, 7654374, 7654375, 7654376, 7654377, 7654378, 7654380, 7654381, 7654379</sub>

**Praise** — Psa 56:4  ·  `H1984:prais#4`

_Story_

This is the first sounding of the refrain the psalm will repeat: 'In God, whose word I praise.' Fear is real — he admits, 'when I am afraid' — but his response is to boast in God's promise, and out of that boast trust grows until he can say, 'I shall not be afraid.' Nothing outside prompts the praise; it is the heart's own first move, both the ground of his confidence and the way he voices it. What he says here once he will say twice more, the confidence building as the psalm goes on.

_Reading_

Praise / boast in (halal) is an action (102): 'In God, whose word I PRAISE' — the first statement of the refrain that v10 will double. Its source (103) is absent (present:false): no antecedent cause is named, for the glorying is the ground of confidence, not its consequence. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is praise / glory in; the target (107) is God, whose word he praises. There is no seat (104 none), no manner (108 none), no intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) or prohibition (113): the praise is unqualified, a plain glorying in the reliability of God's promise. Coupling (112, pair) welds it forward to the refrain as restated twice in v10 — this v4 utterance is the seed of the repetition to come. The locus (116) is external:god. The process is precise: 'When I am afraid, I put my trust in you. In God, whose word I praise... I shall not be afraid.' Fear is present, but the inner answer is to glory in God's word, and out of that glorying trust rises and fear is refused. The absent source underscores that praise here is both the ground and the expression of confidence — the trusting heart's first move, not a reaction to rescue already granted.

*Why this reading differs:* This is the origin-statement of the Psa 56 refrain that vv10a/10b will double; it differs from the Psa 48:1 sibling (a communal, sourced status) by being the individual's self-arising boast that grounds his refusal of fear. Its recurrence across the psalm is the meaningful movement — the same confession deepening from a single utterance into an emphatic, twice-repeated refrain as assurance grows.

## Psa 57:1-11

> To the choirmaster : according to Do Not Destroy . A Miktam of David , when he fled from Saul , in the cave . Psa 57:1 Be merciful to me, O God , be merciful to me, for in you my soul takes refuge ; in the shadow of your wings I will take refuge , till the storms of destruction pass by . Psa 57:2 I cry out to God Most High , to God who fulfills his purpose for me . Psa 57:3 He will send from heaven and save me; he will put to shame him who tramples on me. Selah God will send out his steadfast love and his faithfulness ! Psa 57:4 My soul is in the midst of lions ; I lie down amid fiery beasts— the children of man , whose teeth are spears and arrows , whose tongues are sharp swords . Psa 57:5 Be exalted , O God , above the heavens ! Let your glory be over all the earth ! Psa 57:6 They set a net for my steps ; my soul was bowed down . They dug a pit in my way , but they have fallen into it themselves. Selah Psa 57:7 My heart is steadfast , O God , my heart is steadfast ! I will sing and make melody ! Psa 57:8 Awake , my glory ! Awake , O harp and lyre ! I will awake the dawn ! Psa 57:9 I will give thanks to you, O Lord , among the peoples ; I will sing praises to you among the nations . Psa 57:10 For your steadfast love is great to the heavens , your faithfulness to the clouds . Psa 57:11 Be exalted , O God , above the heavens ! Let your glory be over all the earth !

**make melody** — Psa 57:7  ·  `H2167:makemelody#1`

_Story_

The psalmist's heart has grown steady and sure, and out of that settledness comes music: 'I will sing and make melody!' Nothing outside sets it off — it rises straight from the confident heart, which only moments before had been bowed low. He reaches for harp and lyre, and would even wake the dawn to join in. Song and instrument go together here, two forms of one praise pouring from a soul that has found its footing.

_Reading_

Make melody (zamar) is an action (102): 'I will sing and MAKE MELODY!' — instrumental praise, music played to God. Its source (103) is absent (present:false): no prior cause is named for the melody, because it springs from the settled inner state just declared — 'My heart is steadfast' — rather than from an external prompt. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is make melody / play praise; the target (107) is God. The manner (108) is 'with harp and lyre' — the praise takes instrumental form, summoning the strings (v8). Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) are absent. Coupling (112, pair) welds it to singing — melody and song are a matched pair, the voice and the instrument together. The locus (116) is external:god. The process reads: a heart made steadfast (v7), lifted from the earlier dejection where the soul was bowed down (v6), overflows into music — rousing not only harp and lyre but the very dawn to join the praise. There is no seat named (104 none): the melody is not lodged in a faculty but issues from the whole roused self. The steadfast heart is the spring; the melody, twinned with song, is its outflow.

*Why this reading differs:* This zamar reading is instrumental praise springing from a steadfast heart and twinned with singing, whereas its sibling at Psa 108:1 (the same opening reused) gives no such stated frame beyond directing melody to God. The difference is modest but real: here the melody is explicitly the overflow of a heart that has moved from dejection to confidence.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7654606, 7654607, 7654608, 7654609, 7654610, 7654611, 7654612, 7654613, 7654615, 7654616, 7654614</sub>

**sing** — Psa 57:7  ·  `H7891:sing#5`

_Story_

With his heart now firm, the psalmist declares he will sing — song and music together — to the God he longs to see lifted high above the heavens and over all the earth. His singing is not a small, private thing; it wakes his own inmost self, calls for harp and lyre, and would rouse the dawn itself to join in. What is striking is that this praise comes before any rescue has arrived; it flows purely from settled confidence in God, a song born of trust rather than of deliverance already seen.

_Reading_

Sing (shir) is an action (102): 'I will SING and make melody!' Its source (103, pair) is praising the God who is to be exalted above the heavens (v5) — the singing is generated by, and reaches toward, the exaltation of God over the whole earth. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is sing; the target (107) is God. The manner (108) is expansive — 'rousing his glory, harp, lyre, and the dawn' — the song calls on the whole of himself and creation to wake and join. Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) are absent. Coupling (112, pair) welds it to making melody — song and instrument as a matched pair. There is no seat (104 none): the singing is not organ-located but the movement of the whole roused person. The locus (116) is external:god. The process runs: a steadfast heart (v7) overflows into song aimed at the God who is exalted above the heavens, a song that summons harp, lyre, glory and even the dawn. Notably, the note observes this is worship 'born of confidence, not yet of deliverance' — the singing rises before rescue, out of settled trust rather than accomplished rescue, which the absent effect-row (no traced outcome) quietly matches.

*Why this reading differs:* This shir reading is song rising from confidence before deliverance, sourced in God's exaltation above the heavens and twinned with making melody — distinct from its sibling at Psa 59:16, where the singing answers deliverance already given (God proved a fortress in distress) and is set at morning. The difference is meaningful: here praise precedes rescue and rests on trust; there it follows rescue and rests on experience.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7654594, 7654595, 7654602, 7654596, 7654597, 7654598, 7654599, 7654600, 7654601, 7654604, 7654605, 7654603</sub>

**Glory** — Psa 57:8  ·  `H3519:glory#2`

_Story_

'Awake, my glory!' — the psalmist is not talking about God's splendour here but about his own deepest, noblest self, his soul. Not long before, that soul had been bowed down in gloom; now he commands it to wake up and rise. This is a person turning inward and speaking to himself, stirring the best of who he is to shake off dejection and get ready to praise. It is not yet praise aimed at God; it is the inner self rousing itself, calling on harp and lyre and the dawn as it comes awake.

_Reading_

This is an inward-turned instance. Glory (kavod) is a status (102), and the note is careful: 'AWAKE, my GLORY!' here means the psalmist's own noblest self — his soul — not God's glory (which is the concern of v5 and v11). The source (103) is absent (present:false): nothing outside is named as rousing this self; the summons is reflexive, the person calling on his own inmost part to wake. The bearer (105) is the psalmist, his noblest self; the operation (106) is 'awake / rouse itself to praise' — the self stirring itself from dejection into worship. The target (107) is explicitly absent (res=none): the movement is not directed outward at an object but is self-reflexive, the glory rousing itself. The manner (108) is 'rousing harp, lyre, and the dawn.' Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) are absent; there is no seat (104 none). Coupling (112, pair) welds this roused self to the self that sings and gives thanks (vv7, 9) — the awakened glory is the same self that will sing and thank. The locus (116) is internal:ib-state — the movement happens inside, the inner being addressing itself. The process reads: from the dejection where 'my soul was bowed down' (v6), the psalmist commands his own noblest self to wake and rise into praise. The absent target confirms the shape: this is not yet praise aimed at God but the self summoning itself to be ready to praise — an interior act of self-rousing.

*Why this reading differs:* This kavod reading is 'my glory' — the psalmist's own inmost self roused from dejection, marked internal and with no outward object — unlike its sibling at Psa 29:9, where 'Glory!' is the worshippers' outward cry in the temple as God's voice shakes creation. The difference is fully meaningful: one is the self turning in to wake itself, the other is a congregation crying out toward God.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7654617, 7654618, 7654619, 7654620, 7654621, 7654622, 7654623, 7654624, 7654626, 7654627, 7654625</sub>

**sing praises** — Psa 57:9  ·  `H2167:singprais#1`

_Story_

Moved by a faithfulness he pictures as reaching all the way to the clouds, the psalmist resolves to sing God's praises — and to do it out loud among the nations, not just in private. His singing is matched by thanksgiving offered where the peoples can hear, turning his own experience of God into public testimony. What fills him is the sheer scale of God's steadfast love, and it spills over into praise he wants the whole world to catch.

_Reading_

Sing praises (zamar) is an action (102): 'I will SING PRAISES to you among the nations.' Its source (103, pair) is God's faithfulness reaching to the clouds (v10) — the singing is generated by the greatness of his steadfast love and faithfulness. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is sing praises; the target (107) is God, and the setting is 'among the nations' — the praise is deliberately made public and international. The manner (108) is 'among the nations' — worship carried out beyond Israel, in the hearing of all peoples. Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) are absent; there is no seat (104 none). Coupling (112, pair) welds it to giving thanks among the peoples (v9a) — singing praises and giving thanks are a matched pair, both aimed outward at the watching nations. The locus (116) is external:god. The process reads: the psalmist's grasp of God's faithfulness, great as the sky, moves him to sing — and to sing where the nations can hear, so that his private rescue becomes public testimony. The absent seat and absent effect leave the movement uncomplicated: a heart full of God's faithfulness overflowing into praise it wants the whole world to overhear.

*Why this reading differs:* Among the many sing-praises siblings, this reading is distinctive for being sourced in God's cloud-high faithfulness and aimed publicly 'among the nations,' twinned with giving thanks — where later instances (Psa 61:8, 71:22-23, 75:9) more often give no stated source and address God privately or with the lyre. The public, international reach and the explicit ground make this reading's outward, testimonial thrust the meaningful difference.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7654640, 7654641, 7654648, 7654642, 7654643, 7654644, 7654645, 7654646, 7654647, 7654650, 7654651, 7654649</sub>

## Psa 59:16-17

> Psa 59:15 They wander about for food and growl if they do not get their fill. Psa 59:16 But I will sing of your strength ; I will sing aloud of your steadfast love in the morning . For you have been to me a fortress and a refuge in the day of my distress . Psa 59:17 O my Strength , I will sing praises to you, for you, O God , are my fortress , the God who shows me steadfast love .

**sing aloud** — Psa 59:16  ·  `H7442:singaloud#2`

_Story_

After a night of enemies prowling like dogs, the psalmist greets the morning with a ringing, joyful song — and what he sings about is God's steadfast love, which proved to be a stronghold in his day of trouble. Paired with this is a song of God's strength; the two go together, one glad response to a God who held firm. The song is not tucked into any one part of him; it is his whole self meeting the dawn with praise for the refuge that saw him through the dark.

_Reading_

Sing aloud (ranan) is an action (102), a jubilant ringing cry: 'I will SING ALOUD of your steadfast love in the morning.' Its source (103) is absent (present:false) — the singing's ground is not named as a separate row here, though the following clause ('for you have been to me a fortress and a refuge') supplies the reason in the passage. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is sing aloud / ring out; the target (107, pair) is God's steadfast love, the theme celebrated. The manner (108) is 'in the morning' — the song is set at daybreak, after a night of prowling enemies (v15). Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) are absent; there is no seat (104 none). Coupling (112, pair) welds it to singing of God's strength (v16a) — the two songs, of strength and of steadfast love, are a matched pair. The locus (116) is external:god. The process reads: the long night of harassment gives way to a morning cry that rings out God's chesed, which proved a refuge in the day of distress. The absent seat leaves the song un-lodged in any organ — it is the whole self greeting the dawn in praise — and the absent source-row lets the praise stand as the natural morning-response to a fortress that held.

*Why this reading differs:* This ranan reading leaves the seat and the stated source blank, sings of God's steadfast love, and is set at morning after deliverance — unlike its sibling at Psa 51:14, which names the pardoned tongue as its seat, sings of God's righteousness, and rises from forgiveness of guilt. The difference is meaningful: here the jubilant cry answers rescue from enemies at daybreak; there it answers cleansing from sin.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7655310, 7655311, 7655312, 7655313, 7655314, 7655315, 7655316, 7655317, 7655319, 7655320, 7655318</sub>

**sing** — Psa 59:16  ·  `H7891:sing#6`

_Story_

Set against enemies who prowl and growl through the night, the psalmist says: 'But I will sing of your strength.' His song has a solid reason behind it — God has actually been his fortress and refuge when trouble came. So when morning breaks, the night of watching turns into a song about the God who held him. Alongside it runs a song of God's faithful love; the two themes belong together in one morning of praise, grounded not in hope alone but in rescue already known.

_Reading_

Sing (shir) is an action (102): 'But I will SING of your strength.' Its source (103, pair) is that God has been his fortress and refuge in the day of distress (v16) — the singing is caused by, and grounded in, rescue already experienced. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is sing; the target (107, pair) is God's strength, the theme sung. The manner (108) is 'in the morning' — song at daybreak, set against the night of the growling enemies (v15). Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) are absent; there is no seat (104 none). Coupling (112, pair) welds it to singing aloud of God's steadfast love (v16b) — strength and steadfast love, the two matched themes of one morning-song. The locus (116) is external:god. The process reads: the earlier watching and waiting through the night (v9) turns, at dawn, into song — the believer greets the morning singing of the fortress that held him. The 'But' is the hinge: over against the wandering, growling enemies of v15 stands the psalmist's song. The absent seat leaves the singing as the whole person's response; the stated source grounds it firmly in tested experience rather than mere anticipation.

*Why this reading differs:* This shir reading is grounded in tested experience — God already proved a fortress in distress — and set at morning, unlike its sibling at Psa 57:7, where the song rises from confidence before any deliverance and is sourced in God's exaltation above the heavens. The difference is meaningful: here praise follows rescue and rests on what God has done; there it precedes rescue and rests on trust.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7655298, 7655299, 7655306, 7655300, 7655301, 7655302, 7655303, 7655304, 7655305, 7655308, 7655309, 7655307</sub>

**sing praises** — Psa 59:17  ·  `H2167:singprais#2`

_Story_

The psalm ends with the psalmist turning his whole song back to God: 'O my Strength, I will sing praises to you, for you are my fortress, the God who shows me steadfast love.' Earlier he had used those same words — 'my Strength' — while he was still watching and waiting for God to act. Now that watching has become singing. The long night of vigilance resolves, at the close, into straightforward praise of the God who proved to be his stronghold and his faithful love.

_Reading_

Sing praises (zamar) is an action (102): 'O my Strength, I will SING PRAISES to you.' Its source (103, pair) is that God is his fortress, the God who shows him steadfast love (v17) — the singing is grounded in God's proven character as stronghold and in his chesed. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is sing praises; the target (107, pair) is God addressed directly as 'my Strength' and 'my fortress.' There is no seat (104 none) and no manner (108 none); intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) are absent. Coupling (112, pair) welds it to the refrain of v9 now fulfilled in praise — earlier the psalmist watched and waited ('O my Strength, I will watch for you'), and here that watching turns into singing. The locus (116) is external:god. The process reads: the whole psalm's movement from vigilant waiting to accomplished praise closes here, the same address ('my Strength') that once framed watching now framing song. The absent manner and seat leave the praise plain and direct — a closing seal on the psalm, the inner posture of watchfulness resolving into worship of God as Strength, fortress, and giver of steadfast love.

*Why this reading differs:* This sing-praises reading is the psalm's closing seal, sourced in God as proven fortress and welded to the earlier 'watch for you' refrain (v9) now fulfilled — distinct from the Psa 57:9 sibling, sourced in cloud-high faithfulness and aimed publicly among the nations. The difference is meaningful: there praise reaches outward to the peoples, here it turns back to God as the resolution of a psalm-long posture of watching.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7655321, 7655322, 7655323, 7655324, 7655325, 7655326, 7655327, 7655328, 7655331, 7655332, 7655330</sub>

## Psa 61:1-5

> To the choirmaster : with stringed instruments . Of David . Psa 61:1 Hear my cry , O God , listen to my prayer ; Psa 61:2 from the end of the earth I call to you when my heart is faint . Lead me to the rock that is higher than I, Psa 61:3 for you have been my refuge , a strong tower against the enemy . Psa 61:4 Let me dwell in your tent forever ! Let me take refuge under the shelter of your wings ! Selah Psa 61:5 For you , O God , have heard my vows ; you have given me the heritage of those who fear your name .

**higher** — Psa 61:2  ·  `H7311:higher#1`

_Story_

Worn down and far from home, his heart failing, the psalmist cries out to be led to 'the rock that is higher than I.' The reach comes from his own weakness — he feels how far he is from safety and knows he cannot climb to it by himself. So he does not claim to reach it; he asks to be brought there, because only God can lead him up to a security that stands above his own strength. And that longing opens onto rest: to dwell forever in God's tent, sheltered under his wings.

_Reading_

This is an inward movement of reaching beyond the self. Be high / higher (rum) is a status (102): 'Lead me to the rock that is HIGHER than I' — a height, a security, that stands above the person's own strength. The source (103) is absent (present:false): nothing is named as originating the reach except the fainting of the heart itself ('when my heart is faint') — it arises from within, out of felt weakness, uncaused by any external prompt. The bearer (105) is the psalmist, longing to be led there; the operation (106) is 'long to be led up to it' — not a self-achievement but a yearning to be brought. The target (107) is 'the rock higher than himself.' The manner (108, pair) is the crucial qualifier: 'led there by God' — he cannot climb to this height on his own; God must lead him. Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) are absent; there is no seat (104 none). Coupling (112, pair) welds this reaching to its outcome — dwelling and taking refuge in God (v4). The locus (116) is internal:ib-state — the movement is an interior confession. The process reads: from a fainting heart at the end of the earth, the inner being reaches for a safety above its own capacity, confessing it cannot get there unaided and must be led — and that reach issues in dwelling under God's shelter. The absent seat and source underline the shape: this is not a faculty acting but the whole weakened self admitting that security lies higher than itself.

## Psa 61:7-8

> Psa 61:7 May he be enthroned forever before God ; appoint steadfast love and faithfulness to watch over him ! Psa 61:8 So will I ever sing praises to your name , as I perform my vows day after day .

**sing praises** — Psa 61:8  ·  `H2167:singprais#3`

_Story_

Because God has set his steadfast love and faithfulness to watch over the king, the psalmist answers with praise that never stops: 'So will I ever sing praises to your name.' This is not a one-off song but a lifelong habit, matched by keeping his promises to God day after day. The guarding love he has received calls out a worship stretched across all his days — singing and vow-keeping woven together into a continual return of thanks.

_Reading_

Sing praises (zamar) is an action (102): 'So will I ever SING PRAISES to your name.' Its source (103, pair) is God's steadfast love and faithfulness appointed to watch over the king (v7) — the singing answers that guarding love and faithfulness. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is sing praises; the target (107, pair) is God's name. The manner (108) is 'ever / continually' — the praise is not a single event but a perpetual, ongoing offering. Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) are absent; there is no seat (104 none). Coupling (112, pair) welds it to performing his vows 'day after day' — singing and vow-keeping are a matched pair, both continual. The locus (116) is external:god. The process reads: because steadfast love and faithfulness are set to keep the king enthroned before God (v7), the answering movement is a praise that never stops, bound together with a daily discharge of vows. The 'ever' and the 'day after day' make this a lifelong posture rather than a moment; the absent seat leaves it as the whole life's ongoing response, worship stretched out across all his days as the return for God's guarding love.

*Why this reading differs:* This sing-praises reading is marked by continuity — praise offered 'ever,' twinned with performing vows day after day — and sourced in God's guarding love and faithfulness, unlike the Psa 57:9 sibling (public, among the nations, sourced in cloud-high faithfulness) or Psa 59:17 (a closing seal grounded in God as fortress). The difference is meaningful: here the accent falls on perpetuity, praise as a lifelong, daily discipline rather than a single testimony or a psalm's resolution.

## Psa 63:1-11

> A Psalm of David , when he was in the wilderness of Judah . Psa 63:1 O God , you are my God ; earnestly I seek you; my soul thirsts for you; my flesh faints for you, as in a dry and weary land where there is no water . Psa 63:2 So I have looked upon you in the sanctuary , beholding your power and glory . Psa 63:3 Because your steadfast love is better than life , my lips will praise you . Psa 63:4 So I will bless you as long as I live ; in your name I will lift up my hands . Psa 63:5 My soul will be satisfied as with fat and rich food , and my mouth will praise you with joyful lips , Psa 63:6 when I remember you upon my bed , and meditate on you in the watches of the night ; Psa 63:7 for you have been my help , and in the shadow of your wings I will sing for joy . Psa 63:8 My soul clings to you; your right hand upholds me. Psa 63:9 But those who seek to destroy my life shall go down into the depths of the earth ; Psa 63:10 they shall be given over to the power of the sword ; they shall be a portion for jackals . Psa 63:11 But the king shall rejoice in God ; all who swear by him shall exult , for the mouths of liars will be stopped .

**praise** — Psa 63:3  ·  `H7623:prais#1`

_Story_

In a parched, exhausting place where his very body faints with longing for God, the psalmist reaches a settled conviction: God's faithful love is worth more than life itself. And from that conviction his praise rises to his lips — he will laud God with his mouth. It is spoken praise, formed on the lips, and it is echoed later when his satisfied soul praises again with joyful lips. Its whole strength comes from that deepest judgement of value: nothing, not even life, is worth as much to him as God's steadfast love.

_Reading_

Praise / laud (shabach) is an action (102): 'Because your steadfast love is better than life, my lips will PRAISE you.' Its source (103, pair) is precisely that valuation — God's steadfast love reckoned better than life itself (v3); the praise flows from the deepest estimate of what is best. A seat (104) is present: the lips — the praise is located in the organ of speech, the mouth that will laud God. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is praise / laud; the target (107) is God. There is no manner (108 none), no intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) or prohibition (113): the praise is ungraded, its whole weight carried by the valuation that grounds it. Coupling (112, pair) welds it to the praise repeated with joyful lips (v5) — the lauding here is twinned with the later praise fed by the soul's satisfaction. The locus (116) is external:god. The process reads: in a dry and weary land where the flesh faints (v1), the soul's thirst for God resolves into a judgement — his chesed outweighs life — and out of that judgement the lips are moved to praise. The named seat matters: praise takes verbal, spoken form on the lips. The absent manner leaves the praise plain; its force lies entirely in the astonishing ground, that God's love is prized above life itself.

*Why this reading differs:* This shabach reading grounds praise in a striking valuation — God's steadfast love reckoned better than life — locates it on the lips, and twins it with the joyful-lipped praise of v5, whereas its sibling at Psa 147:12 is a corporate summons calling Zion and Jerusalem to praise the God who strengthens and blesses them. The difference is meaningful: here praise is an individual's lauding drawn out by the supreme worth he assigns God's love; there it is a city called collectively to extol its benefactor.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7656433, 7656434, 7656441, 7656435, 7656436, 7656437, 7656438, 7656439, 7656440, 7656443, 7656444, 7656442</sub>

**Praise** — Psa 63:5  ·  `H1984:prais#5`

_Story_

In the parched wilderness the psalmist is nonetheless full — his inner self is fed and satisfied as if at a rich feast — and out of that fullness his mouth opens in glad praise to God. The gladness is not an afterthought but the whole colour of the words on his lips. Nothing in the verse says what triggered the praise from outside; it simply overflows from a contented heart. It is aimed wholly at God, and the passage never says what it accomplishes — the praise is offered for its own sake, the natural voice of a soul satisfied in God.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is halal, praise, worked as an action (102): 'my mouth will praise you with joyful lips.' No source (103) is recorded as a row, yet the coupling (112) supplies the movement's origin from within the passage itself — the praise is welded to the soul's satisfaction of v5 ('My soul will be satisfied as with fat and rich food'), so the operation (106) of praising rises out of an inner fullness rather than out of nothing. The seat (104) is the mouth and the bearer (105) is the psalmist: this is worship located in the organ of speech, a filled inner life finding its outlet at the lips. The target (107) is God, and the manner (108) is 'with joyful lips' — the gladness is not incidental but the very texture of the utterance. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) carry no rows: the verse does not measure how loud or rank the praise, nor state what it changes; the movement is described as pure expression, not as a lever producing an outcome. No prohibition (113) touches it. The role (115) is characteristic and the locus (116) is external:god — the inner satisfaction turns outward toward God as its arena. The full arc is thus contentment-in-God (coupling) feeding the mouth (seat) into glad praise (operation) directed at God (target), with the passage leaving the effect unstated because the praise is offered as its own end.

*Why this reading differs:* Among this word's readings many stand with no stated cause (Psa 113, 135, 150), and a few name an external ground (God as fortress in Zion, Psa 48:1; the saving God, Psa 74:21). This reading is distinctive in binding the praise explicitly to the soul's own satisfaction — the movement is contentment overflowing into worship, an inner state becoming the spring of praise, which is a meaningful shift from praise summoned by command or by God's remembered deeds.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7656478, 7656479, 7656480, 7656481, 7656482, 7656483, 7656484, 7656485, 7656487, 7656488, 7656486</sub>

**sing for joy** — Psa 63:7  ·  `H7442:singforjoy#1`

_Story_

Remembering that God has been his help, the psalmist bursts into joyful song — and he sings it from a place of shelter, as though tucked safely beneath sheltering wings. The joy is not floating free; it is the direct response to having been rescued. It rises from his whole being, not from any single part of him, and it is aimed entirely at God. The verse never measures how great the joy is or says what the singing achieves; safety and gladness simply come together in the song.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is ranan, to sing for joy or ring out, an action (102): 'in the shadow of your wings I will sing for joy.' Here the source (103) is present and named — 'for you have been my help' (v7) — so the jubilant song is caused, springing from remembered rescue rather than arising uncaused. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the joy is not located in a bodily organ but rises from the whole person, the bearer (105) being the psalmist. The operation (106) is the ringing shout of joy, and the target (107) is God. The manner (108) is 'in the shadow of God's wings' — the song is sung from a place of shelter, safety and gladness held together. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: the passage does not grade the joy or state what it produces; it is expression, not instrument. Coupling (112) records none, so the singing stands as its own act rather than being welded to a neighbouring movement. No prohibition (113) constrains it. Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The process runs from God-as-help (source), remembered under the shelter of his wings (manner), overflowing in the whole self into jubilant song (operation) toward God (target) — gladness that is the direct answer to having been helped.

*Why this reading differs:* This word's other readings differ in who sings and why: at Psa 67:4 the bearer is 'the nations' with no stated cause, coupled to their gladness at God's just rule, and at Psa 84:2 and 92:4 the joy answers longing for God or the works of his hands. This reading is distinctive in naming a personal source (God has been my help) and a sheltering manner (under his wings) — a single sheltered self singing from rescue, over against the wider corporate joy elsewhere; the difference is meaningful.

## Psa 65:1-6

> To the choirmaster . A Psalm of David . A Song . Psa 65:1 Praise is due to you, O God , in Zion , and to you shall vows be performed . Psa 65:2 O you who hear prayer , to you shall all flesh come . Psa 65:3 When iniquities prevail against me, you atone for our transgressions . Psa 65:4 Blessed is the one you choose and bring near , to dwell in your courts ! We shall be satisfied with the goodness of your house , the holiness of your temple ! Psa 65:5 By awesome deeds you answer us with righteousness , O God of our salvation , the hope of all the ends of the earth and of the farthest seas ; Psa 65:6 the one who by his strength established the mountains , being girded with might ;

**praise** — Psa 65:1  ·  `H8416:prais#4`

_Story_

In Zion praise waits for God like a debt ready to be paid — it is owed to him, and with it the worshippers make good the vows they promised. The praise is grounded in the God who visits the earth and fills it with plenty. It rises not from any single part of a person but from the gathered people, and it is directed to God in his holy place. The verse does not weigh how great the praise is or say what it brings about; it simply names praise as the fitting tribute owed to God.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is tehillah, praise, treated as an action (102): 'Praise is due to you, O God, in Zion.' The source (103) is present, reaching forward to the God who visits and enriches the earth (v9), so the owed praise is grounded in God's provident action. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): this is not organ-located worship but the corporate response of a people, the bearer (105) being the worshippers in Zion. The operation (106) is to render praise, and the target (107) is God in Zion. The manner (108) is 'as a due, with performed vows' — praise framed as a debt owed, waiting to be paid, joined to the fulfilling of vows. The coupling (112) confirms this by welding the praise to the performing of vows (v1). Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: the verse does not grade the praise or state its result; it names praise as a fitting obligation, not as a cause of any outcome. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from the provident God (source) to a waiting, owed praise (operation) rendered by the Zion worshippers (bearer) toward God (target), coupled to the paying of vows — worship as tribute properly due.

*Why this reading differs:* This word's readings vary in ground and posture: some name a source (God's steadfast love, Psa 48:10 and 51:15) while others state none (Psa 66:2, 66:8). This reading is distinctive in casting praise as a due or debt that waits in silence for God and is paid alongside vows — worship as owed tribute rather than as spontaneous overflow or public proclamation; the difference in posture is meaningful.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7657001, 7657002, 7657009, 7657003, 7657004, 7657005, 7657006, 7657007, 7657008, 7657011, 7657012, 7657010</sub>

## Psa 66:1-9

> To the choirmaster . A Song . A Psalm . Psa 66:1 Shout for joy to God , all the earth ; Psa 66:2 sing the glory of his name ; give to him glorious praise ! Psa 66:3 Say to God , “ How awesome are your deeds ! So great is your power that your enemies come cringing to you. Psa 66:4 All the earth worships you and sings praises to you; they sing praises to your name .” Selah Psa 66:5 Come and see what God has done: he is awesome in his deeds toward the children of man . Psa 66:6 He turned the sea into dry land ; they passed through the river on foot . There did we rejoice in him , Psa 66:7 who rules by his might forever , whose eyes keep watch on the nations — let not the rebellious exalt themselves. Selah Psa 66:8 Bless our God , O peoples ; let the sound of his praise be heard , Psa 66:9 who has kept our soul among the living and has not let our feet slip . Psa 66:10 For you, O God, have tested us; you have tried us as silver is tried. Psa 66:11 You brought us into the net ; you laid a crushing burden on our backs ;

**Sing** — Psa 66:2  ·  `H2167:sing#5`

_Story_

All the earth is called to sing — and the song is aimed squarely at the glory of God's name, not at anything vague. Nothing in the verse names what stirred the singing; it is simply owed to who God is. It rises from the whole earth rather than from any one part of a person, and it stands alongside the offering of glorious praise. The verse never says how the song sounds or what it accomplishes; it is music lifted to the great name.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is zamar, to sing or make music, an action (102): 'sing the glory of his name.' No source (103) is recorded: the summons to sing arises without a stated cause — the psalm simply calls it forth, so the movement is presented as owed to God's worth rather than triggered by a named event. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): this is not organ-bound but the response of the whole earth, the bearer (105) being 'all the earth.' The operation (106) is to sing, and the target (107) is specifically 'the glory of God's name' — the song is aimed not vaguely but at the weight and splendour of the name. No manner (108) is recorded (res=none): the verse does not describe the style of the singing. Coupling (112) welds it to 'give to him glorious praise,' so the singing stands beside a parallel act of praise. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated outcome — the singing is expression toward God, not a lever. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from all the earth (bearer) singing (operation) toward the glory of God's name (target), coupled to glorious praise — universal music offered to the name above every name.

*Why this reading differs:* This word's other readings sometimes name a cause (God's choosing and loving his people, Psa 47:6) and elsewhere leave it unstated (Psa 135:3). This reading is distinctive in fixing the target precisely on 'the glory of his name' — the song is directed at God's glory as its object, and summoned from all the earth rather than a single worshipper; the sharpened target and universal bearer make the difference meaningful.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7658203, 7658204, 7658205, 7658206, 7658207, 7658208, 7658209, 7658210, 7658212, 7658213, 7658211</sub>

**praise** — Psa 66:2  ·  `H8416:prais#5`

_Story_

All the earth is told to give God glorious praise — to hand him praise that is weighty and splendid, worthy of the God of awesome deeds. Nothing in the verse names what prompted it; the praise is simply what fits him. It comes from the whole earth, not from any one part of a person, and it stands together with singing of his glory. The verse never measures the praise or says what it brings about; it is the deliberate giving of glory back to God.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is tehillah, praise, an action (102): 'give to him glorious praise.' No source (103) is recorded: the call to praise arises without a named cause, presented as fitting to God rather than sparked by a stated event. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): this is the whole earth's response, the bearer (105) being 'all the earth,' not a bodily organ. The operation (106) is to give or render praise, and the target (107) is God. The manner (108) is 'glorious' — the praise is rendered as a weighty, splendid offering, fitting the God of awesome deeds. Coupling (112) welds it to 'sing his glory,' so the giving of praise stands beside the singing. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: the verse does not grade the praise or name its result; praise is the giving of glory, not a means to an outcome. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from all the earth (bearer) rendering (operation) glorious praise (manner) to God (target), coupled to singing his glory — worship as the deliberate giving of glory back to God.

*Why this reading differs:* Where a sibling names the ground of praise (God's provident visiting of the earth, Psa 65:1), this reading records no source and instead stresses the manner — praise rendered as 'glorious,' a splendid gift matching God's awesome deeds. The shift from grounded, owed praise to praise weighed by its splendour is a meaningful distinction of texture.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7658214, 7658215, 7658216, 7658217, 7658218, 7658219, 7658220, 7658221, 7658223, 7658224, 7658222</sub>

**praises** — Psa 66:4  ·  `H2167:prais#1`

_Story_

All the earth sings its praises to God, and the singing goes hand in hand with bowing down before him. Nothing in the verse names what set off the praise; it is simply the earth's answer to the awesome God around it. The praise rises from the whole world rather than from any one part of a person, and it is aimed at God. The verse does not say how loud or lavish it is, or what it achieves; the song and the bowing belong together.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is zamar, to sing praises, an action (102): 'all the earth... sings praises to you.' No source (103) is recorded: the praise arises without a named trigger, set as the earth's fitting answer to the awesome God of the surrounding verses. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): this is corporate, the bearer (105) being 'all the earth,' not an organ. The operation (106) is to sing praises, and the target (107) is God. No manner (108) is recorded (res=none): the verse does not describe how the praise is offered. Coupling (112) welds it to worship (bowing) and to the repetition 'to his name,' so the singing stands beside the act of worship and is about to be re-aimed at the name. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated result. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from all the earth (bearer) singing praises (operation) to God (target), welded to the bowing of worship — sung worship joined to the posture of homage.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading and its immediate sibling both sit in Psa 66:4, but this one aims the praise at God generally and welds it to the act of worship (bowing), whereas the sibling re-fixes the same singing precisely on 'your name.' The movement here is sung homage joined to bowing; the difference is a real narrowing of target between the two.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7658248, 7658249, 7658250, 7658251, 7658252, 7658253, 7658254, 7658255, 7658257, 7658258, 7658256</sub>

**praises** — Psa 66:4  ·  `H2167:prais#2`

_Story_

Again all the earth sings its praises, and this time the song is aimed exactly at God's name — worship that is named and pointed, not vague. Nothing in the verse says what prompted it; it is simply the earth answering God. The praise comes from the whole world, not from any single part of a person. The verse never grades the praise or says what it accomplishes; what it stresses is that the song is directed straight to God's name.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is zamar, to sing praises, an action (102): 'they sing praises to your name.' No source (103) is recorded: the praise arises without a named cause, the earth's fitting answer to God. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is 'all the earth,' corporate rather than organ-located. The operation (106) is to sing praises, and here the target (107) is specifically 'to God's name' — the worship is named and directed, not vague. No manner (108) is recorded (res=none). Coupling (112) welds it to the preceding line, the praise directed to God's name in tandem with the singing of v4. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated outcome. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from all the earth (bearer) singing praises (operation) precisely to God's name (target) — worship focused and addressed, not diffuse.

*Why this reading differs:* Set against its twin in the same verse, this reading sharpens the target from God generally to 'your name' specifically — the same earthwide singing is now addressed, named and directed. The difference is meaningful: it moves the praise from homage-in-general to worship fixed on the name.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7658259, 7658260, 7658261, 7658262, 7658263, 7658264, 7658265, 7658266, 7658268, 7658269, 7658267</sub>

**Exalt** — Psa 66:7  ·  `H7311:exalt#1`

_Story_

Here the inner movement is not praise but pride: the rebellious lift themselves up against God. Nothing outside sets it off — the arrogance rises on its own, self-generated, which is exactly its character. It is not tied to any single part of a person but is the whole self taking a defiant stance, aimed against God. The verse does not measure the pride or spell out its end, but it stands under a warning — God is watching the nations and will not let the proud raise themselves. Unlike the outward-turned praise around it, this movement curls back on the self.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is rum, to exalt or lift up oneself, an action (102): 'let not the rebellious exalt themselves.' Read at full weight, this is a descent-movement of pride, not an ascent of praise. No source (103) is recorded: the self-exaltation arises uncaused from within, self-generated rather than provoked — which is itself the point, arrogance that springs from itself. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the pride is not organ-located but a posture of the whole self, the bearer (105) being 'the rebellious.' The operation (106) is to exalt or lift oneself up, and the target (107) is 'against God' — the lifting is directed at God as its arena of defiance. No manner (108) is recorded (res=none). Coupling (112) welds it to the self-exaltation of the rebellious as a single defiant motion. Notably no prohibition (113) row is recorded even though the verse says 'let not': the inner-being item is coded as the movement of self-exaltation itself, with the restraint coming from outside — God 'whose eyes keep watch on the nations' — not as a prohibition lodged within the faculty. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: the verse does not grade the pride or state its outcome, though the surrounding line implies God will not permit it. Role (115) is characteristic, and crucially the locus (116) is internal:ib-state — unlike the God-directed praise around it, this movement turns inward on the self. The arc runs from a self-arising, uncaused pride (no source) lifting the whole rebellious self (operation) against God (target), a motion God's watching restrains from without.

*Why this reading differs:* This verb's other readings are ascent: worshippers 'exalt the LORD our God' (Psa 99:5, 99:9), lifting God up. This reading inverts every axis — the bearer is 'the rebellious' not worshippers, the target is 'against God' not toward him, and the locus is internal (a state of the self) rather than external toward God. The same word for lifting up becomes self-exaltation instead of exaltation of God; the reversal is fully meaningful and read at equal weight.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7658306, 7658307, 7658308, 7658309, 7658310, 7658311, 7658312, 7658313, 7658315, 7658316, 7658314</sub>

**praise** — Psa 66:8  ·  `H8416:prais#6`

_Story_

Let the sound of God's praise be heard — the peoples are to make his praise ring out in the open, not keep it to themselves. Nothing in the verse names what prompted it; it is simply the fitting public response to God. The praise rises from the peoples together, and it is about him. The verse does not say how loud it grows or what it achieves; its whole stress falls on making the praise audible, carried abroad for all to hear.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is tehillah, praise, but here typed as a status (102) rather than a plain action: 'let the sound of his praise be heard.' No source (103) is recorded: the praise-to-be-heard arises without a named cause, framed as the fitting public response to God. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is 'the peoples,' corporate rather than organ-bound. The operation (106) is that the praise is 'made to be heard' — caused to resound abroad, so the movement is not merely offering praise but making it audible and public. The target (107) is God ('his praise'). No manner (108) is recorded (res=none). Coupling (112) welds it to the praise the peoples are called to make resound. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: the verse does not grade the sound or state its result, though the point is publicity, not measurable outcome. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from the peoples (bearer) causing God's praise to be heard (operation as status) toward God (target) — worship that is not kept private but proclaimed aloud.

*Why this reading differs:* Where this word's siblings mostly render praise as a direct action (Psa 65:1, 66:2), this reading is typed as a status — praise 'made to be heard,' caused to resound. The distinctive movement is publicity: not just offering praise but proclaiming it aloud among the peoples; the shift from act to made-audible status is meaningful.

## Psa 66:14-20

> Psa 66:13 I will come into your house with burnt offerings; I will perform my vows to you, Psa 66:14 that which my lips uttered and my mouth promised when I was in trouble . Psa 66:15 I will offer to you burnt offerings of fattened animals , with the smoke of the sacrifice of rams ; I will make an offering of bulls and goats . Selah Psa 66:16 Come and hear , all you who fear God , and I will tell what he has done for my soul . Psa 66:17 I cried to him with my mouth , and high praise was on my tongue . Psa 66:18 If I had cherished iniquity in my heart , the Lord would not have listened . Psa 66:19 But truly God has listened ; he has attended to the voice of my prayer . Psa 66:20 Blessed be God , because he has not rejected my prayer or removed his steadfast love from me !

**high praise** — Psa 66:17  ·  `H7318:highprais#1`

_Story_

As the psalmist cries out to God for help, high praise sits on his very tongue at the same moment — petition and worship carried together on one breath. Nothing in the verse names what sparked the praise; what it shows is prayer and praise fused. The extolling rests on his tongue, the organ that is crying, and it is lifted to God. The verse does not weigh the praise or say what it gains; it simply shows a faith that praises even while it asks.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is romam, high praise or extolling, typed as a status (102): 'high praise was on my tongue.' No source (103) is recorded: the extolling arises without a named cause, though the surrounding lines set it amid the cry of need. The seat (104) is the tongue and the bearer (105) is the psalmist — the high praise is located in the organ of speech, sitting on the tongue even as the mouth cries out. The operation (106) is to extol or lift up praise, and the target (107) is God. No manner (108) is recorded (res=none). Coupling (112) is the telling feature: the high praise is welded to 'I cried to him with my mouth' (v17) — petition and praise together on the same tongue. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: the verse does not grade the praise or state its outcome; it is expression, not a lever. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from a crying, needy self (coupling) whose tongue (seat) simultaneously lifts high praise (operation) to God (target) — faith that praises even while it asks, extolling mingled with the plea for help.

*Why this reading differs:* This word's other reading fuses praise with agency — 'high praises of God in their throat and a two-edged sword in their hand' (Psa 149:6), militant praise. Here the fusion is instead with petition: high praise coupled to the cry for help. Both share the pattern of praise welded to a second act, but the companion differs (prayer versus sword), and that difference is meaningful.

## Psa 67:3-7

> To the choirmaster : with stringed instruments . A Psalm . A Song . Psa 67:1 May God be gracious to us and bless us and make his face to shine upon us, Selah Psa 67:2 that your way may be known on earth, your saving power among all nations. Psa 67:3 Let the peoples praise you, O God ; let all the peoples praise you! Psa 67:4 Let the nations be glad and sing for joy , for you judge the peoples with equity and guide the nations upon earth . Selah Psa 67:5 Let the peoples praise you, O God ; let all the peoples praise you! Psa 67:6 The earth has yielded its increase ; God , our God , shall bless us. Psa 67:7 God shall bless us; let all the ends of the earth fear him!

**praise** — Psa 67:3  ·  `H3034:prais#7`

_Story_

Let the peoples praise God — this is the longing at the heart of the psalm, that not Israel alone but all nations everywhere would give him thanks. The praise is bound up with God's ways becoming known across the earth, its fitting fruit. It rises from whole peoples rather than any single part of a person, and it is aimed at God. The verse does not measure the praise or say what it produces; it is a cry that the worship of God would spread to every nation.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is yadah, to praise or give thanks, an action (102): 'Let the peoples praise you, O God.' No source (103) is recorded as a row, though the psalm's frame is God's way and saving power being known on earth (v2); the praise is presented as the fruit of that knowing, sought rather than sprung from a stated trigger. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is 'the peoples,' corporate and worldwide, not an organ. The operation (106) is to praise or give thanks, and the target (107) is God. The manner (108) is 'all the peoples' — the reach is universal, not confined to Israel. Coupling (112) welds this line to its refrain repeated in v5, so the praise brackets the psalm. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated result; the praise is longed-for expression, not a lever. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from God's way being known (the frame) to a longed-for praise (operation) offered by all peoples (bearer, manner) toward God (target) — the psalm's heartbeat, that the nations join the worship.

*Why this reading differs:* This word's readings scatter widely in who praises and whom: some direct praise at God (Psa 42:11, 43:4), some at a king (Psa 45:17), and one strikingly at a self-made prosperous man (Psa 49:18). This reading is distinctive as the universal refrain — the peoples, indeed all peoples, praising God — worship reaching beyond Israel; its worldwide bearer marks it off meaningfully from the more personal or misdirected instances.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7658649, 7658650, 7658651, 7658652, 7658653, 7658654, 7658655, 7658656, 7658658, 7658659, 7658657</sub>

**praise** — Psa 67:3  ·  `H3034:prais#8`

_Story_

Let all the peoples praise God — every one of them, no nation left out. This line lifts the cry of the verse before it, pressing the longing to its widest reach. Nothing names what prompts the praise; it is simply the hope that the whole world would thank God. The praise comes from all peoples together and is aimed at God, and the same refrain sounds again later in the psalm. The verse does not weigh the praise or say what it brings about; its force is entirely in the word 'all.'

_Reading_

The sense (101) is yadah, to praise or give thanks, an action (102): 'let all the peoples praise you!' No source (103) is recorded: the praise arises without a named trigger, longed-for as the fruit of God's known ways. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is 'all the peoples,' emphatically universal, not an organ. The operation (106) is to praise or give thanks, and the target (107) is God. The manner (108) is 'all of them' — the reach is total, no nation left out. Coupling (112) welds it to the emphatic doubling of the refrain, this line intensifying the one before it. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated outcome. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The recurrence at Psa 67:5 shows the same refrain returning. The arc runs from an unstated ground to an all-encompassing praise (operation) by every people without exception (bearer, manner) toward God (target) — the psalm's longing that no nation be left outside the worship of God.

*Why this reading differs:* Paired with the line just before it (Psa 67:3a), this reading intensifies by the emphatic 'all' — the bearer widens from 'the peoples' to 'all the peoples,' every nation without exception. The doubling is meaningful: it drives the psalm's longing to its fullest universal reach.

*Recurs at:* Psa 67:5

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7658660, 7658661, 7658662, 7658663, 7658664, 7658665, 7658666, 7658667, 7658669, 7658670, 7658668</sub>

**sing for joy** — Psa 67:4  ·  `H7442:singforjoy#2`

_Story_

Let the nations be glad and sing for joy — their gladness spilling over into ringing song because God judges the peoples fairly and guides them. The joy and the song belong together, one flowing into the other. It rises from whole nations, not from any single part of a person, and it is aimed at God. The verse does not measure the joy or say what it accomplishes; it is gladness at God's just rule breaking out in song from all peoples.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is ranan, to sing for joy or ring out, an action (102): 'Let the nations be glad and sing for joy.' No source (103) is recorded as a row, though the following line ('for you judge the peoples with equity') supplies the joy's occasion; the singing is set as the answer to God's just rule rather than sprung from a stated inner trigger. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is 'the nations,' corporate rather than organ-bound. The operation (106) is the ringing shout of joy, and the target (107) is God. No manner (108) is recorded (res=none). Coupling (112) welds it to 'be glad,' so the singing stands beside gladness as its overflow. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated outcome. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from gladness (coupling) among the nations (bearer) overflowing into jubilant song (operation) toward God (target) — corporate joy answering God's equitable judging of the peoples.

*Why this reading differs:* Set beside its sibling at Psa 63:7, this reading shifts the bearer from a single psalmist to 'the nations,' drops the named personal source (there, God's help) and the sheltering manner (under his wings), and instead couples the singing to corporate gladness at God's just rule. Both are ascent, but the movement widens from one sheltered self to the joy of all peoples; the shift is meaningful.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7658683, 7658684, 7658685, 7658686, 7658687, 7658688, 7658689, 7658690, 7658692, 7658693, 7658691</sub>

**praise** — Psa 67:5  ·  `H3034:prais#9`

_Story_

The refrain comes back: let the peoples praise God, all of them. Sounding again, it wraps around the psalm's vision of God ruling the nations fairly, so that praise brackets the whole picture. Nothing names what prompts it; it is the fitting answer to God's just rule. The praise rises from all peoples together and is aimed at God. The verse does not weigh it or say what it achieves; its repetition is what gives it force, enclosing the psalm in worship.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is yadah, to praise or give thanks, an action (102): 'Let the peoples praise you, O God' — the refrain returning to frame the psalm's centre. No source (103) is recorded: the praise arises without a named trigger, longed-for as the fruit of God's just rule set out in the surrounding verses. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is 'the peoples,' corporate rather than organ-bound. The operation (106) is to praise or give thanks, and the target (107) is God. The manner (108) is 'all the peoples' — universal reach. Coupling (112) welds it to the refrain of v3 repeated, so this line brackets the vision of God's equitable governing of the nations. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated result. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from an unstated ground to a returning, all-peoples praise (operation) toward God (target), the repeated refrain enclosing the psalm's heart — worship as the frame around God's just rule over the world.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is the return of the Psa 67:3 refrain, repeated to bracket the psalm's centre. Against its siblings it is distinctive not by a new element but by its structural role — the same all-peoples praise sounding a second time to frame the vision of God's just governing of the nations; the repetition itself carries the meaning.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7658694, 7658695, 7658696, 7658697, 7658698, 7658699, 7658700, 7658701, 7658703, 7658704, 7658702</sub>
