## Psa 68:1-7

> To the choirmaster . A Psalm of David . A Song . Psa 68:1 God shall arise , his enemies shall be scattered ; and those who hate him shall flee before him! Psa 68:2 As smoke is driven away , so you shall drive them away ; as wax melts before fire , so the wicked shall perish before God ! Psa 68:3 But the righteous shall be glad ; they shall exult before God ; they shall be jubilant with joy ! Psa 68:4 Sing to God , sing praises to his name ; lift up a song to him who rides through the deserts ; his name is the Lord ; exult before him! Psa 68:5 Father of the fatherless and protector of widows is God in his holy habitation . Psa 68:6 God settles the solitary in a home ; he leads out the prisoners to prosperity , but the rebellious dwell in a parched land . Psa 68:7 O God , when you went out before your people , when you marched through the wilderness , Selah

**sing praises** — Psa 68:4  ·  `H2167:singprais#4`

_Story_

Sing to God, sing his praises to his name — the worshippers are called to lift music to the God who rides through the deserts in triumph. Nothing in the verse names what stirred the song; it is simply owed to who he is. It rises from the worshippers together and is aimed at his name, standing alongside the parallel call to sing to God. The verse does not measure the praise or say what it brings about; it is music offered to the name of the mighty God.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is zamar, to sing praises, an action (102): 'Sing to God, sing praises to his name.' No source (103) is recorded: the summons to sing praises arises without a named cause, owed to the God who rides through the deserts (v4). No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is 'the worshippers,' corporate rather than organ-bound. The operation (106) is to sing praises, and the target (107) is specifically 'to God's name.' No manner (108) is recorded (res=none). Coupling (112) welds it to the parallel 'sing to God' of the same verse, the two summons standing together. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated outcome. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from an unstated ground to the worshippers (bearer) singing praises (operation) directed to God's name (target), coupled to the twin call to sing — music offered to the name of the God who rides in triumph.

*Why this reading differs:* This word's earlier readings all name a personal ground and a single bearer — the psalmist sings praises because God's faithfulness reaches the clouds (Psa 57:9), because God is his fortress (Psa 59:17), in response to God's steadfast love (Psa 61:8). This reading drops the stated source and shifts the bearer to 'the worshippers,' a corporate summons to sing to God's name. The move from grounded personal praise to communal call is meaningful.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7659442, 7659443, 7659444, 7659445, 7659446, 7659447, 7659448, 7659449, 7659451, 7659452, 7659450</sub>

**sing** — Psa 68:4  ·  `H7891:sing#7`

_Story_

Sing to God, lift up a song to the one who rides through the deserts — the worshippers are summoned to sing, and to do it exulting before him. Their song is grounded in who God is: mighty enough to ride the wastes, yet father to the fatherless and protector of widows. It rises from the worshippers together and is aimed at God and his name, bound up with shouts of joy and praise. The verse does not measure the singing or say what it achieves; it is worship of a God both powerful and tender.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is shir, to sing, an action (102): 'Sing to God, sing praises to his name; lift up a song to him who rides through the deserts.' The source (103) is present and rich — the God who rides through the deserts and is father of the fatherless (vv4-5) — so the singing is grounded in the character of a God at once mighty and compassionate. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is 'the worshippers,' corporate rather than organ-bound. The operation (106) is to sing, and the target (107) is God, indeed his name. The manner (108) is 'exulting before him' — the singing is offered in exultation. Coupling (112) welds it to the parallel singing of praises and to the exulting, a cluster of praise-acts moving together. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated outcome. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from the mighty-yet-compassionate God (source) to the worshippers (bearer) singing and exulting (operation, manner) toward God and his name (target) — worship of the rider of the deserts who also cares for the helpless.

*Why this reading differs:* This word's readings range from grateful song for God's bounty (Psa 13:6) to the stark refusal to sing in exile (Psa 137:4). This reading is distinctive in its richly stated source — the God who rides the deserts and is father of the fatherless — and in its manner of exulting, drawing the worshippers to sing of a God at once mighty and compassionate; the fullness of its ground marks it off meaningfully.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7659430, 7659431, 7659438, 7659432, 7659433, 7659434, 7659435, 7659436, 7659437, 7659440, 7659441, 7659439</sub>

## Psa 68:23-35

> Psa 68:23 that you may strike your feet in their blood , that the tongues of your dogs may have their portion from the foe .” Psa 68:24 Your procession is seen , O God , the procession of my God , my King , into the sanctuary — Psa 68:25 the singers in front , the musicians last , between them virgins playing tambourines : Psa 68:26 “ Bless God in the great congregation , the Lord , O you who are of Israel’s fountain !” Psa 68:27 There is Benjamin , the least of them, in the lead , the princes of Judah in their throng , the princes of Zebulun , the princes of Naphtali . Psa 68:28 Summon your power , O God , the power , O God , by which you have worked for us . Psa 68:29 Because of your temple at Jerusalem kings shall bear gifts to you. Psa 68:30 Rebuke the beasts that dwell among the reeds , the herd of bulls with the calves of the peoples . Trample underfoot those who lust after tribute ; scatter the peoples who delight in war . Psa 68:31 Nobles shall come from Egypt ; Cush shall hasten to stretch out her hands to God . Psa 68:32 O kingdoms of the earth , sing to God ; sing praises to the Lord , Selah Psa 68:33 to him who rides in the heavens , the ancient heavens ; behold , he sends out his voice , his mighty voice . Psa 68:34 Ascribe power to God , whose majesty is over Israel , and whose power is in the skies . Psa 68:35 Awesome is God from his sanctuary ; the God of Israel — he is the one who gives power and strength to his people . Blessed be God !

**singers** — Psa 68:25  ·  `H7891:singer#1`

_Story_

In the procession into God's sanctuary the singers go at the front, the musicians behind, and between them young women playing tambourines — an ordered march of praise. They are named simply as those who lead the worship, with nothing said of what set them going. Their singing moves toward God in his holy place, and it opens straight into the whole congregation's call to bless him. The verse does not weigh the song or say what it accomplishes; it pictures human voices leading God's festal march.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is shir in its agent form, the singers, typed as a status (102): 'the singers in front, the musicians last.' No source (103) is recorded: the singers are named without a stated cause, taking their place in God's festal procession. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is 'the singers in the procession,' a body of people rather than an organ. The operation (106) is to sing in procession, and the target (107) is God, into whose sanctuary the march moves. The manner (108) is richly given by inference: 'in front, musicians behind, maidens with tambourines between' — the ordered shape of the worshipping march. Coupling (112) welds this to the call to 'Bless God in the great congregation' (v26), so the singers lead directly into corporate blessing. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated outcome. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from the singers (bearer) leading the ordered procession (operation, manner) toward God in his sanctuary (target), opening into the congregation's blessing — human voices at the head of God's triumphal march.

*Why this reading differs:* The other reading of this agent-word sets the singers alongside dancers, celebrating that 'all my springs are in you' (Psa 87:7). Both picture singers in festive, corporate worship; this reading is distinctive in placing them at the head of an ordered procession into the sanctuary, leading into the congregation's blessing — a processional role, which is a meaningful particular.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7659510, 7659511, 7659512, 7659513, 7659514, 7659515, 7659516, 7659517, 7659519, 7659520, 7659518</sub>

**sing praises** — Psa 68:32  ·  `H2167:singprais#5`

_Story_

The kingdoms of the earth are called to sing God's praises, to lift music to the Lord who rides the ancient heavens. Nothing in the verse names what prompts it; the praise is simply owed to him. It rises from the nations of the world, not from any one part of a person, and it is aimed at the Lord, standing beside the parallel call to sing to God. The verse does not measure the praise or say what it achieves; it is a summons for whole kingdoms to worship the God of heaven.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is zamar, to sing praises, an action (102): 'sing praises to the Lord.' No source (103) is recorded: the call to sing praises arises without a named cause, owed to the God who rides the heavens (v33). No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is 'the kingdoms of the earth,' worldwide rather than organ-bound. The operation (106) is to sing praises, and the target (107) is 'to the Lord.' No manner (108) is recorded (res=none). Coupling (112) welds it to the parallel 'sing to God' of the same verse. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated outcome. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from an unstated ground to the kingdoms of the earth (bearer) singing praises (operation) to the Lord (target), coupled to the twin call to sing — the nations summoned to praise the God who rides the heavens.

*Why this reading differs:* Against its immediate sibling at Psa 68:4, where the bearer is 'the worshippers,' this reading widens the bearer to 'the kingdoms of the earth' — the same sung praise now demanded of whole nations. The move from the gathered worshippers to a worldwide summons is a meaningful broadening.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7659557, 7659558, 7659559, 7659560, 7659561, 7659562, 7659563, 7659564, 7659566, 7659567, 7659565</sub>

**sing** — Psa 68:32  ·  `H7891:sing#8`

_Story_

Sing to God, all you kingdoms of the earth — the whole world's nations are called to worship the God who rides the ancient heavens and sends out his mighty voice. Their song is grounded in his sovereignty over the skies. It rises from all the kingdoms, not from any single part of a person, and it is aimed at God, standing together with the call to sing his praises. The verse does not measure the song or say what it brings about; it summons universal praise to the sovereign God.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is shir, to sing, an action (102): 'Sing to God, O kingdoms of the earth.' The source (103) is present — the God who rides the ancient heavens and sends out his mighty voice (v33) — so the singing is grounded in the sovereignty of the God who rules the skies. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is 'the kingdoms of the earth,' worldwide rather than organ-bound. The operation (106) is to sing, and the target (107) is God. No manner (108) is recorded (res=none). Coupling (112) welds it to 'sing praises to the Lord' of the same verse, the two summons together. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated outcome. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from the sovereign rider of the heavens (source) to the kingdoms of the earth (bearer) singing (operation) toward God (target) — universal praise summoned from all nations to the God whose mighty voice rules the skies.

*Why this reading differs:* Set beside its sibling at Psa 68:4, where 'the worshippers' sing of the God who cares for the fatherless, this reading widens the bearer to 'the kingdoms of the earth' and grounds the song in God's sovereign rule over the ancient heavens. The movement broadens from a gathered congregation to a worldwide summons, and the pictured God from tender-and-mighty to cosmically sovereign; the shift is meaningful.

## Psa 69:26-32

> Psa 69:26 For they persecute him whom you have struck down , and they recount the pain of those you have wounded . Psa 69:27 Add to them punishment upon punishment ; may they have no acquittal from you. Psa 69:28 Let them be blotted out of the book of the living ; let them not be enrolled among the righteous . Psa 69:29 But I am afflicted and in pain ; let your salvation , O God , set me on high ! Psa 69:30 I will praise the name of God with a song ; I will magnify him with thanksgiving . Psa 69:31 This will please the Lord more than an ox or a bull with horns and hoofs . Psa 69:32 When the humble see it they will be glad ; you who seek God , let your hearts revive . Psa 69:33 For the Lord hears the needy and does not despise his own people who are prisoners. Psa 69:34 Let heaven and earth praise him, the seas and everything that moves in them . Psa 69:35 For God will save Zion and build up the cities of Judah, and people shall dwell there and possess it; Psa 69:36 the offspring of his servants shall inherit it, and those who love his name shall dwell in it .

**magnify** — Psa 69:30  ·  `H1431:magnify#2`

_Story_

Though afflicted and in pain, the psalmist resolves to make God great, lifting him high with thanksgiving. The magnifying grows out of his suffering turned toward hope, not out of any triumph already in hand. It comes from his whole self and is aimed at God, carried on gratitude and joined to praise in song. The verse does not measure it or spell out its result, though it adds that such worship pleases God more than any sacrifice — a hurting soul enlarging God's name even before rescue arrives.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is gadal, to magnify or make great, an action (102): 'I will magnify him with thanksgiving.' No source (103) is recorded as a row, yet the passage's frame is the afflicted, hoping self ('I am afflicted and in pain... let your salvation set me on high,' v29); the magnifying rises out of affliction turned toward hope rather than out of a stated event. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is the psalmist, the whole self rather than an organ. The operation (106) is to magnify or exalt, making God great, and the target (107) is God, magnified 'with thanksgiving.' The manner (108) is likewise 'with thanksgiving' — the making-great is carried on gratitude. Coupling (112) welds it to the praise in song of v30a, so magnifying and singing move together. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated outcome, though v31 adds that this pleases God more than sacrifice. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from an afflicted, hoping self (frame) to the enlarging of God (operation) carried on thanksgiving (manner) toward God (target), coupled to praise in song — the suffering soul making God great before deliverance is seen.

*Why this reading differs:* This word's other reading is a summons to others — 'magnify the LORD with me, let us exalt his name together' (Psa 34:3), praise made communal. This reading is instead the afflicted individual's own vow, magnifying God with thanksgiving out of pain and hope. The shift from calling a community to a single suffering self is meaningful.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7660165, 7660166, 7660167, 7660168, 7660169, 7660170, 7660171, 7660172, 7660174, 7660175, 7660173</sub>

**Praise** — Psa 69:30  ·  `H1984:prais#6`

_Story_

Out of affliction the psalmist vows to praise God's name with a song — the lament turning to worship even before any rescue has come. Nothing in the verse names an outward trigger; the praise is promised from within the pain. It rises from his whole self, takes the form of song, and is aimed at God's name, joined to grateful, magnifying praise. The verse does not measure it or say what it gains, though it adds that such praise pleases God more than any animal sacrifice.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is halal, praise, an action (102): 'I will praise the name of God with a song.' No source (103) is recorded as a row, yet the passage's frame is the afflicted self of v29; the praise is vowed out of affliction, before deliverance is in hand. No seat (104) is recorded (res=none): the bearer (105) is the psalmist, the whole self rather than an organ. The operation (106) is to praise, and the target (107) is 'the name of God, with a song.' The manner (108) is 'with a song' — the praise takes musical form. Coupling (112) welds it to the magnifying of God with thanksgiving in v30b, so praise-in-song and magnifying move together. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are silent: no measure, no rank, no stated outcome, though v31 notes this pleases God more than an ox or bull. No prohibition (113). Role (115) is characteristic, locus (116) external:god. The arc runs from an afflicted self (frame) to a vowed praise (operation) of God's name in song (target, manner), coupled to grateful magnifying — the lament turning to worship even before rescue is seen, praise that God prizes above sacrifice.

*Why this reading differs:* Against this word's other reading in the batch (Psa 63:5), where praise overflows from a satisfied soul, this reading arises from the opposite state — an afflicted, hurting self vowing praise before deliverance is seen. The same verb for praise moves from contentment to lament-turned-worship; the difference in the inner state that spawns the praise is meaningful.

## Psa 70:1-5

> To the choirmaster . Of David , for the memorial offering . Psa 70:1 Make haste, O God , to deliver me! O Lord , make haste to help me! Psa 70:2 Let them be put to shame and confusion who seek my life ! Let them be turned back and brought to dishonor who delight in my hurt ! Psa 70:3 Let them turn back because of their shame who say , “ Aha , Aha !” Psa 70:4 May all who seek you rejoice and be glad in you! May those who love your salvation say evermore , “ God is great !” Psa 70:5 But I am poor and needy ; hasten to me, O God ! You are my help and my deliverer ; O Lord , do not delay !

**great** — Psa 70:4  ·  `H1431:great#1`

_Story_

This is the human act of calling God great — not a feeling that sits still but something people keep doing. The verse never says what sets it off; it simply flows out of a settled love of being rescued, needing no other spark. It isn't tucked into the heart or confined to the lips — it is the whole person's confession, thrown toward God without ever falling silent. Those who love God's saving cannot stop saying that he is great, and that endless saying is nothing more than the overflow of their love.

_Reading_

The characteristic here is the inner act of magnifying — ascribing greatness to God (gadal), the mouth's "God is great!" The sense (101) is precisely this exalting confession, and the type (102) is an action, a doing rather than a static condition. No source (103) is recorded: the verse traces the magnifying to no prior trigger, so it reads as self-arising from the standing disposition of those who love God's salvation, not provoked by a fresh event. No seat (104) is given — the movement is not lodged in heart or mouth as an organ but is the whole person's confession, unlocalised. The bearer (105) is those who seek God and love his salvation; the operation (106) is to declare God great; the target (107) is God himself, the arena toward which the inner act is flung. The manner (108) is "evermore, continually" — an unceasing saying, not an occasional outburst. The coupling (112) binds this magnifying to the praise and gladness that flow from loving God's salvation: it is the fruit of that love, welded to it, not a detached liturgical routine. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) all speak: the verse does not grade the force, does not narrow the kind, does not report what the magnifying achieves, and sets no bar against it — the whole weight rests in the perpetual confession itself. Read on the inner being, love of God's rescue here overflows outward as ceaseless ascription of greatness — worship becomes the default posture of the whole self.

## Psa 71:1-10

> Psa 71:1 In you, O Lord , do I take refuge ; let me never be put to shame ! Psa 71:2 In your righteousness deliver me and rescue me; incline your ear to me, and save me ! Psa 71:3 Be to me a rock of refuge , to which I may continually come ; you have given the command to save me, for you are my rock and my fortress . Psa 71:4 Rescue me, O my God , from the hand of the wicked , from the grasp of the unjust and cruel man . Psa 71:5 For you, O Lord , are my hope , my trust , O Lord , from my youth . Psa 71:6 Upon you I have leaned from before my birth ; you are he who took me from my mother’s womb . My praise is continually of you. Psa 71:7 I have been as a portent to many , but you are my strong refuge . Psa 71:8 My mouth is filled with your praise , and with your glory all the day . Psa 71:9 Do not cast me off in the time of old age ; forsake me not when my strength is spent . Psa 71:10 For my enemies speak concerning me; those who watch for my life consult together Psa 71:11 and say, “God has forsaken him; pursue and seize him, for there is none to deliver him.”

**praise** — Psa 71:6  ·  `H8416:prais#7`

_Story_

Here praise is the constant business of a life. Nothing in the verse names a single event that started it; instead it rises out of a lifetime of leaning on God from birth onward, so it feels less like a reaction and more like the steady air the person breathes. It isn't lodged in the mouth or heart alone but is simply what this life does all the time, turned toward God without a break. A person carried by God from the womb answers by praising him continually — praise as the settled weather of the soul.

_Reading_

The characteristic is praise (tehillah), and the sense (101) is its continual character — "my praise is continually of you." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is named: the verse gives no immediate prompt, and the surrounding passage instead grounds this praise in a lifelong leaning on God from the womb — so the praise reads not as reaction to a single deliverance but as the steady condition of a whole life. No seat (104) is recorded here; the praise is not tied to an organ but is the standing occupation of the person. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is simply to praise; the target (107) is God, the arena toward whom the whole life is turned. The manner (108) is "continually" — without break. The coupling (112) links this praise to the praise that fills his mouth all day in v8, marking the two as one unbroken movement across the psalm. The absences of intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the praise unmeasured, unqualified, its outcome unstated and unopposed — the verse cares only that it never stops. On the inner being: a self sustained by God from before birth answers with praise as its constant inner weather, not an episode but a lifelong disposition.

*Why this reading differs:* This tehillah reading differs from its siblings in bearer and grounding: whereas the corporate readings (Psa 66, 100, 148) place praise in the mouth of all the earth or the saints, and the earlier Psa 48/51/65 readings name an explicit prompt (God's steadfast love, deliverance, his visiting of the earth) as the source, here the bearer is a single psalmist and no source is stated — the praise is grounded instead in a whole life's leaning on God, marking it as continual disposition rather than triggered response.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7660884, 7660885, 7660886, 7660887, 7660888, 7660889, 7660890, 7660891, 7660893, 7660894, 7660892</sub>

**praise** — Psa 71:8  ·  `H8416:prais#8`

_Story_

Now praise is so constant it fills the mouth to the brim. Nothing in the verse says what triggered it; it simply keeps pouring out of a life held by God. This time the mouth is named as the place it lives — the very organ of speech is packed with praise, and with the weight of God's glory, all day long. Every hour, God's greatness crowds out other words, so that speaking and praising become nearly the same thing.

_Reading_

The characteristic is praise (tehillah), the sense (101) being praise so full it brims — "my mouth is filled with your praise." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is named, so the praise is not tied to a fresh trigger but flows as the standing overflow of a God-sustained life. Unusually for this term, a seat (104) is recorded: the mouth — here the praise is located, the organ through which it pours, and the operation (106) is precisely to fill that mouth with praise. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the target (107) is God, paired with his glory, so that the object of praise is both God and the weight of his glory that fills the psalmist's speech. The manner (108) is "all the day" — unremitting across the daylight hours. The coupling (112) ties this filled mouth back to the continual praise of v6, welding the two verses into a single sustained movement. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the fullness ungraded, unnarrowed, its result unstated and unopposed — the point is the sheer saturation. On the inner being: praise here is so constant that it occupies the very instrument of speech, God's glory crowding out every other word all day long.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is distinguished from its tehillah siblings by a recorded seat (the mouth) where nearly all others leave the seat empty, and by a paired target (God together with his glory). Where v6 states continual praise as a disposition, v8 localises it in the organ of speech and saturates it — the shift from unlocalised constancy to a mouth filled all day is a meaningful intensification within the same psalm.

## Psa 71:13-24

> Psa 71:13 May my accusers be put to shame and consumed ; with scorn and disgrace may they be covered who seek my hurt . Psa 71:14 But I will hope continually and will praise you yet more and more . Psa 71:15 My mouth will tell of your righteous acts , of your deeds of salvation all the day , for their number is past my knowledge . Psa 71:16 With the mighty deeds of the Lord God I will come ; I will remind them of your righteousness , yours alone . Psa 71:17 O God , from my youth you have taught me, and I still proclaim your wondrous deeds . Psa 71:18 So even to old age and gray hairs , O God , do not forsake me, until I proclaim your might to another generation , your power to all those to come . Psa 71:19 Your righteousness , O God , reaches the high heavens . You who have done great things , O God , who is like you ? Psa 71:20 You who have made me see many troubles and calamities will revive me again ; from the depths of the earth you will bring me up again . Psa 71:21 You will increase my greatness and comfort me again . Psa 71:22 I will also praise you with the harp for your faithfulness , O my God ; I will sing praises to you with the lyre , O Holy One of Israel . Psa 71:23 My lips will shout for joy , when I sing praises to you; my soul also, which you have redeemed . Psa 71:24 And my tongue will talk of your righteous help all the day long, for they have been put to shame and disappointed who sought to do me hurt .

**praise** — Psa 71:14  ·  `H8416:prais#9`

_Story_

Here praise is not just kept up but keeps growing — "more and more." The verse names no new reason; it is a resolve held onto as old age and enemies press in. It doesn't belong to any one part of the person but is the whole self's stance, and it rises alongside a hope that never slackens. Instead of fading with the years, this worship climbs — the older the person grows, the louder the praise.

_Reading_

The characteristic is praise (tehillah), the sense (101) being praise that increases — "I will praise you yet more and more." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is named: the praise is not pinned to a fresh cause but is a resolve carried forward against the threat of old age and enemies in the surrounding verses. No seat (104) is recorded; the praise is the disposition of the whole self, not an organ's act. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is to praise; the target (107) is God. The manner (108) is the striking feature here — "yet more and more," praise not merely maintained but multiplying with the years. The coupling (112) pairs this increasing praise with continual hope, so that the two rise together: hope that does not slacken feeds praise that keeps growing. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the praise ungraded in force, unnarrowed, its outcome unstated and unopposed — the verse fixes attention on its upward direction. On the inner being: here worship is not a plateau but an ascent — the aging self, far from fading, answers threat and age by praising ever more, hope and praise climbing in tandem.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading's distinctive is its manner — praise "yet more and more," an increasing rather than merely continual movement — and its coupling with continual hope rather than with a filled mouth (v8) or a lifelong leaning (v6). Against the wider tehillah set, where praise is stated or rendered, here it is explicitly multiplying, and against age and threat, which makes the upward direction the meaningful point of difference.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7660940, 7660941, 7660942, 7660943, 7660944, 7660945, 7660946, 7660947, 7660949, 7660950, 7660948</sub>

**sing praises** — Psa 71:22  ·  `H2167:singprais#6`

_Story_

This is worship set to music — the psalmist singing to God on the lyre. No single event is named as the cause; the song simply rises out of a whole life's trust in God's faithfulness. It is the person's own act, addressed to God as the Holy One of Israel, and it comes paired with harp-playing, two instruments joined in one offering. Even in old age, this inner life stays tuned to God, its trust breaking out as song.

_Reading_

The characteristic is sung praise (zamar), the sense (101) being music offered to God — "I will sing praises to you with the lyre." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is named: the singing is not tied to a single trigger but rises from the whole psalm's confidence in God's faithfulness and coming revival. No seat (104) is recorded; the singing is the act of the person, not an organ. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is to sing praises; the target (107) is paired — God under the title "the Holy One of Israel," so the qualifier of holiness colours the whole address. The manner (108) is "with the lyre" — praise carried on an instrument. The coupling (112) pairs this lyre-song with the harp-praise (yadah) of the same verse, twin instruments in one act of worship. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the singing ungraded, unnarrowed, its effect unstated and unopposed — the verse records the offering, not its measure or result. On the inner being: the aged psalmist's inner life still tunes itself to God, its worship taking instrumental form, addressed to the Holy One who is his deliverer.

*Why this reading differs:* Against the earlier zamar readings (Psa 57, 59, 61) that each name a source — God's faithfulness reaching the clouds, his being a fortress, his steadfast love — this reading leaves the source unstated and instead specifies the target with a title (the Holy One of Israel) and the manner (the lyre). The difference is one of register: earlier the singing answers a named mercy; here it is instrumental worship addressed to God's holiness, paired with harp-praise, marking a corporate-liturgical rather than a purely responsive colour.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7661006, 7661007, 7661008, 7661009, 7661010, 7661011, 7661012, 7661013, 7661015, 7661016, 7661014</sub>

**praise** — Psa 71:22  ·  `H3034:prais#10`

_Story_

This is thankful praise, played out on the harp, offered because God has proved faithful. The verse doesn't spell out a separate cause — the thanks itself points to God's tested reliability as its reason. It is the psalmist's own act, aimed squarely at the faithfulness that never let him down, and it sounds alongside the lyre, two instruments in one grateful offering. A rescued person answers God's dependability with music, gratitude taking the form of a played string.

_Reading_

The characteristic is praise as thanksgiving (yadah), the sense (101) being grateful acknowledgement offered on the harp — "I will praise you with the harp for your faithfulness." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is formally recorded, yet the target itself carries the reason: God's proven faithfulness, the qualifier that never failed him. No seat (104) is named; the thanksgiving is the person's act, not an organ's. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is to praise or give thanks; the target (107) is paired — God, for his faithfulness — so that gratitude is directed not at God abstractly but at his tested reliability. The manner (108) is "with the harp," instrumental. The coupling (112) pairs this harp-thanks with the lyre-song (zamar) of the same verse, the two welded into one musical act. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the thanks ungraded, unnarrowed, unmeasured in outcome and unopposed — the verse fixes on the offering for faithfulness. On the inner being: the delivered self answers God's dependable faithfulness with grateful music, thanksgiving taking the shape of the harp.

*Why this reading differs:* This yadah reading differs from the mortality-argument of Psa 30 (where the psalmist pleads that dust cannot praise) and from the corporate refrains of Psa 67 (let the peoples praise you) by its instrumental manner and its target paired with a named ground — thanks "for your faithfulness." It also stands apart from its own sibling at Psa 76:10 (praise wrung from human wrath): here the bearer praises willingly and gratefully, there praise is overruled from rebellion. The distinction — glad, chosen thanksgiving versus compelled acknowledgement — is the meaningful contrast.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7660995, 7660996, 7660997, 7660998, 7660999, 7661000, 7661001, 7661002, 7661004, 7661005, 7661003</sub>

**sing praises** — Psa 71:23  ·  `H2167:singprais#7`

_Story_

This is the singing that bursts out alongside a shout of joy. The verse names no separate trigger and attaches no instrument — it is simply the plain song of a soul that knows it has been rescued. Lips, tongue and soul are all caught up in it, and the singing and the joyful shout are really one overflowing act. When a person feels wholly redeemed, the gladness cannot stay quiet; it spills over into song.

_Reading_

The characteristic is sung praise (zamar), the sense (101) being the song that rises with the joyful shout — "when I sing praises to you." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is named; the singing is not pinned to a fresh cause but flows from the redeemed soul spoken of in the same verse. No seat (104) is recorded, though the wider verse gathers lips, tongue and soul — the singing is the whole redeemed self's act, not one organ's. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is to sing praises; the target (107) is God. The manner (108) is recorded as none — the verse does not attach an instrument or mode here, leaving the singing plain and unadorned, its whole force in the fact that it happens. The coupling (112) pairs it with the shout of joy of the same verse, so the singing and the shout are one overflowing act. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111), prohibition (113) and manner (108) leave the song stripped to essentials: a redeemed soul, singing, toward God. On the inner being: worship here is the overflow of a soul that knows itself redeemed — joy so full that lips shout and the whole self sings.

*Why this reading differs:* Where the neighbouring zamar reading (v22) specifies both a target-title (Holy One of Israel) and an instrument (the lyre), this reading records no instrument at all (manner none) and pairs the singing with the shout of joy rather than with harp-praise. The song here is stripped to the bare overflow of a redeemed soul — the absence of the instrumental frame, and the coupling to a shout, marks a shift from crafted liturgical offering to spontaneous exultation.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7661029, 7661030, 7661031, 7661032, 7661033, 7661034, 7661035, 7661036, 7661038, 7661039, 7661037</sub>

## Psa 74:17-23

> Psa 74:17 You have fixed all the boundaries of the earth ; you have made summer and winter . Psa 74:18 Remember this , O Lord , how the enemy scoffs , and a foolish people reviles your name . Psa 74:19 Do not deliver the soul of your dove to the wild beasts ; do not forget the life of your poor forever . Psa 74:20 Have regard for the covenant , for the dark places of the land are full of the habitations of violence . Psa 74:21 Let not the downtrodden turn back in shame ; let the poor and needy praise your name . Psa 74:22 Arise , O God , defend your cause ; remember how the foolish scoff at you all the day ! Psa 74:23 Do not forget the clamor of your foes , the uproar of those who rise against you, which goes up continually !

**Praise** — Psa 74:21  ·  `H1984:prais#7`

_Story_

This is the praise the psalmist longs to hear from the crushed and the poor — that they would honour God's name rather than turn back in shame. Its reason is named: God is the one who works salvation in the earth. It belongs to the lowly and afflicted, aimed at God's very name, and it is the praise they will offer once he rescues them. The answer to enemies who mock God is not the silence of the beaten-down but their voices lifted in praise.

_Reading_

The characteristic is praise (halal), the sense (101) being the praise of God's name longed for from the afflicted — "let the poor and needy praise your name." The type (102) is action. Here a source (103) is recorded: the God who works salvation in the earth (v12), so the praise is grounded in God's proven saving work, the qualifier behind it. No seat (104) is named; the praise is the whole afflicted person's act, not an organ's. The bearer (105) is the poor and needy, the downtrodden of the psalm; the operation (106) is to praise; the target (107) is paired — God's name, the object toward which the praise is directed. The manner (108) is recorded as none, leaving the praise unadorned. The coupling (112) ties this to the praise the delivered poor will offer, marking it as a hoped-for future response to rescue. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the praise ungraded and unopposed, its outcome unstated — yet its whole force lies in what it answers: the enemy's reviling of God's name. On the inner being: the psalmist longs for the afflicted self, not to be silenced by shame, but to turn upward in praise — the fitting counter to blasphemy is not the poor's silence but the poor's praise, drawn from the God who saves.

*Why this reading differs:* This halal reading is distinguished within its large set by a recorded source (the God who works salvation, v12) where nearly all its siblings leave the source empty, and by its bearer — the poor and needy — set against a backdrop of enemy blasphemy. Unlike the settled house-dwellers of Psa 84:4 who "ever" praise, here the praise is longed-for and future, the hoped-for reversal of shame, which gives it a note of plea rather than established fact.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7662624, 7662625, 7662632, 7662626, 7662627, 7662628, 7662629, 7662630, 7662631, 7662634, 7662635, 7662633</sub>

## Psa 75:4-10

> Psa 75:4 I say to the boastful , ‘Do not boast ,’ and to the wicked , ‘Do not lift up your horn ; Psa 75:5 do not lift up your horn on high , or speak with haughty neck .’” Psa 75:6 For not from the east or from the west and not from the wilderness comes lifting up , Psa 75:7 but it is God who executes judgment , putting down one and lifting up another . Psa 75:8 For in the hand of the Lord there is a cup with foaming wine , well mixed , and he pours out from it, and all the wicked of the earth shall drain it down to the dregs . Psa 75:9 But I will declare it forever ; I will sing praises to the God of Jacob . Psa 75:10 All the horns of the wicked I will cut off , but the horns of the righteous shall be lifted up .

**sing praises** — Psa 75:9  ·  `H2167:singprais#8`

_Story_

This is worship in the face of judgment — the psalmist singing to the God of Jacob just where the wicked are made to drink the cup of God's wrath. No separate cause is named; the song rises from trust in God as the one who judges rightly. It is the person's own act, joined to the resolve to declare God's judgment forever. When justice falls, the believer does not gloat but sings — the inner life gladly taking the side of the Judge.

_Reading_

The characteristic is sung praise (zamar), the sense (101) being song offered to the God of Jacob — "I will sing praises to the God of Jacob." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is named directly, though the passage sets it against God's just judgment: the wicked drain the cup of wrath while the believer sings. No seat (104) is recorded; the singing is the person's act, not an organ's. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is to sing praises; the target (107) is the God of Jacob — God named by covenant relation. The manner (108) is recorded as none, the singing left plain. The coupling (112) pairs it with declaring God's judgment forever, so the song is welded to the confession that God alone judges. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the singing ungraded, unnarrowed, its outcome unstated and unopposed. On the inner being: precisely where the wicked meet wrath, the believing self answers with song — worship as the trusting response to God's judgment, the inner life siding gladly with the Judge.

*Why this reading differs:* This zamar reading is set apart by its context and coupling: rather than answering a named mercy (as in Psa 57/59/61) or offering instrumental praise (as in Psa 71:22), the singing is paired with declaring God's judgment and stands against the backdrop of the wicked drinking the cup of wrath. The praise here answers God's justice rather than his kindness, which gives it a distinct, sobering colour within the family.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7663036, 7663037, 7663038, 7663039, 7663040, 7663041, 7663042, 7663043, 7663045, 7663046, 7663044</sub>

## Psa 76:7-12

> Psa 76:7 But you , you are to be feared ! Who can stand before you when once your anger is roused ? Psa 76:8 From the heavens you uttered judgment ; the earth feared and was still , Psa 76:9 when God arose to establish judgment , to save all the humble of the earth . Selah Psa 76:10 Surely the wrath of man shall praise you; the remnant of wrath you will put on like a belt . Psa 76:11 Make your vows to the Lord your God and perform them; let all around him bring gifts to him who is to be feared , Psa 76:12 who cuts off the spirit of princes , who is to be feared by the kings of the earth .

**praise** — Psa 76:10  ·  `H3034:prais#11`

_Story_

Here praise is dragged out of human rage itself — "the wrath of man shall praise you." There is no love or thanks behind it; it is honour forced out of fury by God's overruling power. The one doing this praising is the rebel, not the worshipper, and he does it unwillingly, against everything in him. Even human anger, bent against its own grain, ends up acknowledging God — the fury that fights him is turned into evidence for him.

_Reading_

The characteristic is praise as rendered honour (yadah), and the sense (101) is stark: "the wrath of man shall praise you." The type (102) is action — but an action overruled, not chosen. No source (103) is named; the praise does not arise from love or gratitude but is compelled out of human fury by God's sovereignty. No seat (104) is recorded; it is not an organ's act but the whole bent trajectory of human anger. The bearer (105) is man, humanity itself, even in its wrath — the striking point: the one who praises is the rebel, not the worshipper. The operation (106) is to praise or render honour; the target (107) is God. The manner (108) is "unwilling, overruled" — praise wrung from resistance, the very opposite of the glad songs elsewhere in this family. The coupling (112) binds the wrath of man to being made to render this honour, so the rebellion itself becomes the instrument. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the paradox unmeasured and unopposed — the verse simply states that even fury ends up glorifying God. On the inner being: read without valence bias, this shows the darkest inner movement — human rage — bent against its own grain to acknowledge God; the self that will not praise is made to praise, its wrath turned into testimony.

*Why this reading differs:* This is the sharpest divergence in the whole yadah set: every sibling has a willing bearer — psalmist, David, the peoples, the sailors — offering glad thanks to God, but here the bearer is man in his wrath and the manner is unwilling and overruled. Praise is not chosen but compelled from rebellion. The shift from grateful worship to coerced acknowledgement is deeply meaningful, showing the same word covering both the freely given and the sovereignly extracted.

## Psa 81:1-2

> To the choirmaster : according to The Gittith . Of Asaph . Psa 81:1 Sing aloud to God our strength ; shout for joy to the God of Jacob ! Psa 81:2 Raise a song ; sound the tambourine , the sweet lyre with the harp .

**sing aloud** — Psa 81:1  ·  `H7442:singaloud#3`

_Story_

This is a call to loud, exuberant singing at the start of a feast — "sing aloud to God our strength." It isn't traced to any one cause; it is summoned, an invitation for the whole gathering to break into song. It belongs to the worshippers together, aimed at God as their strength, and it comes paired with a shout of joy. The community's gladness is called out at full volume to open the celebration.

_Reading_

The characteristic is exuberant song (ranan), the sense (101) being to sing aloud — "Sing aloud to God our strength." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is named; this is a summons that opens a feast, so the singing is called forth rather than traced to a prior cause. No seat (104) is recorded; the singing is the whole assembly's act, not an organ's. The bearer (105) is the worshippers, a corporate body; the operation (106) is to sing aloud and shout; the target (107) is God under the title "our strength." The manner (108) is recorded as none here, the loudness itself carried by the verb. The coupling (112) pairs the singing with the joyful shout, twinning voice and cry in one summoned praise. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the praise ungraded and unopposed, its outcome unstated — the verse is a call, not a report. On the inner being: this is the collective inner life summoned to full-throated exuberance, the community's joy called out toward the God who is their strength, praise commanded to open the festival.

*Why this reading differs:* Unlike the earlier singaloud readings where the penitent sings of God's righteousness (Psa 51) or the psalmist of his steadfast love (Psa 59) — each naming a theme or source — this reading is a corporate summons with no source stated, its bearer the whole worshipping assembly rather than a single voice. The shift from an individual singing of a named mercy to a festival-opening call to all worshippers marks a move from response to summons.

## Psa 84:1-12

> To the choirmaster : according to The Gittith . A Psalm of the Sons of Korah . Psa 84:1 How lovely is your dwelling place , O Lord of hosts ! Psa 84:2 My soul longs , yes , faints for the courts of the Lord ; my heart and flesh sing for joy to the living God . Psa 84:3 Even the sparrow finds a home , and the swallow a nest for herself, where she may lay her young , at your altars , O Lord of hosts , my King and my God . Psa 84:4 Blessed are those who dwell in your house , ever singing your praise ! Selah Psa 84:5 Blessed are those whose strength is in you, in whose heart are the highways to Zion. Psa 84:6 As they go through the Valley of Baca they make it a place of springs ; the early rain also covers it with pools . Psa 84:7 They go from strength to strength ; each one appears before God in Zion . Psa 84:8 O Lord God of hosts , hear my prayer ; give ear , O God of Jacob ! Selah Psa 84:9 Behold our shield , O God ; look on the face of your anointed ! Psa 84:10 For a day in your courts is better than a thousand elsewhere. I would rather be a doorkeeper in the house of my God than dwell in the tents of wickedness . Psa 84:11 For the Lord God is a sun and shield ; the Lord bestows favor and honor . No good thing does he withhold from those who walk uprightly . Psa 84:12 O Lord of hosts , blessed is the one who trusts in you !

**sing for joy** — Psa 84:2  ·  `H7442:singforjoy#3`

_Story_

This is the whole self singing for joy — heart and body alike — toward the living God. It rises out of a deep longing for God's dwelling, a soul that faints to be near him. Not just the inner person but flesh and heart together are caught up in the cry. A pilgrim aching for God finds that ache turning into song, the entire self reaching out toward him.

_Reading_

The characteristic is joyful song (ranan), the sense (101) being to sing for joy — "my heart and flesh sing for joy to the living God." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is formally recorded, though the surrounding longing for God's courts supplies the ache behind it: the singing rises from a soul that faints for God's dwelling. No seat (104) is named as such, yet the verse gathers heart and flesh — the whole self, inner and bodily, drawn into the cry, which the coupling (112) confirms by pairing the singing with heart and flesh together. The bearer (105) is the pilgrim; the operation (106) is to sing for joy; the target (107) is the living God. The manner (108) is recorded as none, the joy carried by the verb. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the song ungraded and unopposed, its outcome unstated — the point is the wholeness of the response. On the inner being: here the entire self, not just the mind, exults toward God — heart and flesh together crying out, longing turned into song, the whole person leaning toward the living God.

*Why this reading differs:* This ranan reading is marked off by its bearer and coupling: where Psa 63:7 names God's help as the source and Psa 92:4 responds to the works of God's hands, here no source is stated and instead the singing is paired with "heart and flesh," the whole embodied self. The distinctive is the totality of the response — not a reaction to a specific deed but the entire person, inner and bodily, exulting out of longing for God's presence.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7666501, 7666502, 7666503, 7666504, 7666505, 7666506, 7666507, 7666508, 7666510, 7666511, 7666509</sub>

**Praise** — Psa 84:4  ·  `H1984:prais#8`

_Story_

This is praise that never stops — the settled worship of those who live in God's house. No particular event sets it off; it is simply what dwelling near God is like. It belongs to the ones who make their home with him, and it is bound up with their blessedness: to live there, to be blessed, and to praise are all one thing. For such people praise isn't an occasional act but the very air they breathe.

_Reading_

The characteristic is praise (halal), the sense (101) being unbroken praise — "blessed are those who dwell in your house, ever singing your praise." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is named; the praise is not tied to a trigger but is simply the ongoing occupation of those who dwell with God. No seat (104) is recorded; it is the whole dweller's act, not an organ's. The bearer (105) is those who dwell in God's house; the operation (106) is to praise ever, continually; the target (107) is God. The manner (108) is recorded as none, though the "ever" of the verse carries its constancy. The coupling (112) pairs the praise with their blessedness, so that dwelling, being blessed, and praising are one condition — praise is the very air of the house. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the praise ungraded and unopposed, its outcome unstated — its whole weight is in its permanence. On the inner being: for the one who dwells with God, praise is not an act interrupting ordinary life but the continuous condition of the blessed self, worship as the atmosphere of nearness.

*Why this reading differs:* Against its sibling at Psa 74:21, where praise is a longed-for future reversal drawn from the afflicted and grounded in a named source (God who saves), this reading has no source and a bearer already settled in God's house, praising "ever." The contrast is between praise as hoped-for rescue-response and praise as the established, continuous condition of those who dwell with God — plea versus possession.

## Psa 86:9-17

> Psa 86:9 All the nations you have made shall come and worship before you, O Lord , and shall glorify your name . Psa 86:10 For you are great and do wondrous things ; you alone are God . Psa 86:11 Teach me your way , O Lord , that I may walk in your truth ; unite my heart to fear your name . Psa 86:12 I give thanks to you, O Lord my God , with my whole heart , and I will glorify your name forever . Psa 86:13 For great is your steadfast love toward me; you have delivered my soul from the depths of Sheol . Psa 86:14 O God , insolent men have risen up against me; a band of ruthless men seeks my life , and they do not set you before them . Psa 86:15 But you , O Lord , are a God merciful and gracious , slow to anger and abounding in steadfast love and faithfulness . Psa 86:16 Turn to me and be gracious to me; give your strength to your servant , and save the son of your maidservant . Psa 86:17 Show me a sign of your favor , that those who hate me may see and be put to shame because you , Lord , have helped me and comforted me .

**glorify** — Psa 86:12  ·  `H3513:glorify#2`

_Story_

This is the vow to honour God's name forever — to give it real weight and glory. Though the verse itself names no cause, the surrounding words point to God's great love and the rescue of the psalmist's soul from death. It is a whole-hearted act, joined to his thanks, so that honouring and thanking God become one. A rescued person resolves to lift up God's name without end, as the lasting answer of his whole heart to great mercy.

_Reading_

The characteristic is glorifying (kabad), the sense (101) being to weight God with honour — "I will glorify your name forever." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is formally recorded, though the surrounding verses supply the ground: God's great steadfast love and the deliverance of the psalmist's soul from Sheol. No seat (104) is named; the glorifying is the whole self's act — indeed the same passage vows it "with my whole heart" — not one organ's. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is to glorify; the target (107) is God's name, and "forever." The manner (108) is recorded as none. The coupling (112) pairs the glorifying with the thanks he gives, so honouring and thanksgiving are one movement of the whole heart. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the honouring ungraded and unopposed, its outcome unstated — its weight is in the vow's endlessness. On the inner being: the delivered self resolves to honour God's name without end, giving weight and glory to it as the settled, lifelong response of a whole heart to great mercy.

*Why this reading differs:* This kabad reading differs from its sibling at Psa 86:9 chiefly in bearer and scope: here the bearer is the single psalmist vowing to glorify God's name "forever" and the act is coupled with his personal thanks, whereas v9 has all the nations glorifying God's name as an act of worship. The distinction is between the individual's lifelong personal vow and the universal, corporate honouring of God — the same operation at two scales.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7667258, 7667259, 7667260, 7667261, 7667262, 7667263, 7667264, 7667265, 7667267, 7667268, 7667266</sub>

**glorify** — Psa 86:9  ·  `H3513:glorify#3`

_Story_

This is all the nations honouring God's name — every people he has made coming to worship before him. The verse doesn't spell out a cause on its own, though the next line points to God's greatness and his wondrous works. The ones doing this are the whole sweep of humanity, and their honouring is bound up with their coming to worship. Every nation is pictured turning to give weight and glory to God's name, as the fitting posture of all peoples toward their Maker.

_Reading_

The characteristic is glorifying (kabad), the sense (101) being to honour God's name — "all the nations you have made shall come and worship before you and shall glorify your name." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is formally named, though the following verse supplies the ground: God is great and does wondrous things, he alone is God. No seat (104) is recorded; the glorifying is the nations' whole act of worship, not an organ's. The bearer (105) is all the nations — the widest possible human range, every people God has made. The operation (106) is to glorify; the target (107) is God's name. The manner (108) is recorded as none. The coupling (112) pairs the glorifying with their coming to worship before God, so that approach and honouring are one movement. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the honouring ungraded and unopposed, its outcome unstated — the verse fixes on the universal scope. On the inner being: here the honouring impulse is projected across all humanity — every nation's inner life turning to give weight to God's name, worship as the destined posture of all peoples toward their Maker.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is set apart from its sibling at Psa 86:12 by its bearer and scope: v12 is the psalmist's personal, lifelong vow to glorify God's name forever, coupled with his own thanks, while v9 has all the nations glorifying God's name, coupled with their coming to worship. The same honouring operation moves from the single grateful heart to the universal turning of all peoples — a meaningful widening from the personal to the cosmic.

## Psa 87:7

> Psa 87:7 Singers and dancers alike say, “All my springs are in you.”

**singers** — Psa 87:7  ·  `H7891:singer#2`

_Story_

This is a scene of singers and dancers together declaring, "All my springs are in you." No cause is spelled out; their celebration is simply the life of people who find everything they need in God and his city. Singers and dancers alike are caught up in it, and their song is tied to the living springs of Zion. Every source of their joy and life is located in God — and out of that conviction they sing and dance.

_Reading_

The characteristic is the singers (shir), the sense (101) being those who make song — "singers and dancers alike say, 'All my springs are in you.'" The type (102) is action. No source (103) is named; the singing is not traced to a trigger but is the standing activity of those who find their life in God and Zion. No seat (104) is recorded; the celebration is the whole company's act, not an organ's. The bearer (105) is the singers and dancers together; the operation (106) is to sing and celebrate; the target (107) is the confession that all springs — every source of life — are in God. The manner (108) is recorded as none. The coupling (112) pairs the singers with the springs in Zion, so that their song and their rootedness in God's city are one. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the celebration ungraded and unopposed, its outcome unstated — the verse holds a single tableau. On the inner being: here the celebrating self locates the whole source of its life in God — song and dance flowing from the conviction that every wellspring of joy and being is found in him.

*Why this reading differs:* This singer reading differs from its sibling at Psa 68:25, where the singers are in front of a moving procession leading into a call to bless God, by presenting instead a static tableau of singers and dancers together confessing that all their springs are in God. The shift is from a processional role to a settled scene of celebration rooted in Zion — movement toward praise versus praise as the resting-place of joy.

## Psa 89:1-10

> A Maskil of Ethan the Ezrahite . Psa 89:1 I will sing of the steadfast love of the Lord forever ; with my mouth I will make known your faithfulness to all generations . Psa 89:2 For I said , “Steadfast love will be built up forever ; in the heavens you will establish your faithfulness .” Psa 89:3 You have said, “I have made a covenant with my chosen one ; I have sworn to David my servant : Psa 89:4 ‘I will establish your offspring forever , and build your throne for all generations .’” Selah Psa 89:5 Let the heavens praise your wonders , O Lord , your faithfulness in the assembly of the holy ones ! Psa 89:6 For who in the skies can be compared to the Lord ? Who among the heavenly beings is like the Lord , Psa 89:7 a God greatly to be feared in the council of the holy ones , and awesome above all who are around him ? Psa 89:8 O Lord God of hosts , who is mighty as you are, O Lord , with your faithfulness all around you ? Psa 89:9 You rule the raging of the sea ; when its waves rise , you still them . Psa 89:10 You crushed Rahab like a carcass ; you scattered your enemies with your mighty arm .

**sing** — Psa 89:1  ·  `H7891:sing#9`

_Story_

This is the opening resolve of a whole psalm — "I will sing of the Lord's steadfast love forever." No single event sets it off; it is a settled decision to make God's covenant love the theme of song. It is the psalmist's own act, reaching toward God's love without end. And the singing is joined to telling out God's faithfulness to every generation, so that the song is also a witness passed on down the years.

_Reading_

The characteristic is singing (shir), the sense (101) being song of God's steadfast love — "I will sing of the steadfast love of the Lord forever." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is formally named; the singing is a resolve, opening the psalm, not traced to a fresh trigger but reaching toward God's covenant love as its theme. No seat (104) is recorded; the singing is the person's whole act, not an organ's. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is to sing; the target (107) is God's steadfast love, and "forever." The manner (108) is recorded as none. The coupling (112) pairs the singing with making known God's faithfulness to all generations, so that song and testimony are one act — the locus note here marks it as "paired with making known his faithfulness." The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the singing ungraded and unopposed, its outcome unstated — the verse is an opening resolve. On the inner being: the self sets itself to sing of God's covenant love endlessly, worship becoming testimony — the inner resolve to praise doubling as a witness handed to every generation.

*Why this reading differs:* Within the large shir set this reading is distinguished by its target and coupling: unlike the many corporate calls to "sing a new song" (Psa 96, 98, 149) or the refusal-to-sing in exile (Psa 137), here the individual psalmist resolves to sing specifically of God's steadfast love forever, paired with making that faithfulness known to all generations. The distinctive is the fusion of endless praise with generational testimony — song as a handed-down witness.

## Psa 90:5-17

> Psa 90:1 A Prayer of Moses, the man of God.Lord, you have been our dwelling place in all generations. Psa 90:2 Before the mountains were brought forth , or ever you had formed the earth and the world , from everlasting to everlasting you are God . Psa 90:3 You return man to dust and say, “Return, O children of man!” Psa 90:5 You sweep them away as with a flood ; they are like a dream , like grass that is renewed in the morning : Psa 90:6 in the morning it flourishes and is renewed ; in the evening it fades and withers . Psa 90:7 For we are brought to an end by your anger ; by your wrath we are dismayed . Psa 90:8 You have set our iniquities before you, our secret sins in the light of your presence . Psa 90:9 For all our days pass away under your wrath ; we bring our years to an end like a sigh . Psa 90:10 The years of our life are seventy , or even by reason of strength eighty ; yet their span is but toil and trouble ; they are soon gone , and we fly away . Psa 90:11 Who considers the power of your anger , and your wrath according to the fear of you? Psa 90:12 So teach us to number our days that we may get a heart of wisdom . Psa 90:13 Return , O Lord ! How long ? Have pity on your servants ! Psa 90:14 Satisfy us in the morning with your steadfast love , that we may rejoice and be glad all our days . Psa 90:15 Make us glad for as many days as you have afflicted us, and for as many years as we have seen evil . Psa 90:16 Let your work be shown to your servants , and your glorious power to their children . Psa 90:17 Let the favor of the Lord our God be upon us, and establish the work of our hands upon us; yes, establish the work of our hands !

**trouble** — Psa 90:10  ·  `H0205:troubl#2`

_Story_

This is not praise but the sorrow woven into a human life — "their span is but toil and trouble." It is not something a person does but a condition they are in, and the verse names no outside cause for it: the trouble is simply part of what it means to be mortal. It fills the whole of life rather than sitting in one place, and it comes bound together with toil and sorrow, so that hard labour and grief define the short span of our years. Life is brief and heavy at once, and this heaviness is its own inner weather.

_Reading_

The characteristic here is trouble (aven), and unlike the praise-terms around it, the sense (101) is the sorrow that fills a mortal life — "their span is but toil and trouble." The type (102) is a state, not an action: this is a condition the life is in, not a deed it does. No source (103) is named; the verse does not trace the trouble to a cause outside the life itself — it reads as intrinsic to mortal existence under God's wrath, uncaused from within the verse, simply the given texture of the years. No seat (104) is recorded; the trouble is not lodged in an organ but saturates "our life" as a whole. The bearer (105) is our life — human existence itself carries it; the operation (106) is to be full of trouble; the target (107) is paired with sorrow, so trouble and sorrow name one burdened condition. The manner (108) is recorded as none. The coupling (112) pairs the trouble with toil, so that labour and sorrow together define the brief human span. The locus (116) is internal — an inner-being state, not something directed outward. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the trouble ungraded and unopposed, its outcome unstated — its whole weight is in being the fabric of a life that is "soon gone." On the inner being, read without softening: human life at its inner core is marked by toil and sorrow, a burdened state that mortality itself imposes, brief and heavy at once.

*Why this reading differs:* This aven reading diverges sharply from its sibling at Psa 55:3, where the trouble is malice dropped upon the psalmist by the wicked — an evil inflicted from outside, with the wicked as bearer and the psalmist as target. Here the bearer is "our life" itself and the locus is internal: the trouble is not harm done by enemies but the intrinsic sorrow of mortal existence, paired with toil. The shift from externally inflicted mischief to an inward condition of the mortal span is deeply meaningful — the same word names both an assault and a state of being.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7669028, 7669029, 7669030, 7669031, 7669032, 7669033, 7669034, 7669035, 7669037, 7669038, 7669036</sub>

## Psa 92:1-12

> A Psalm . A Song for the Sabbath . Psa 92:1 It is good to give thanks to the Lord , to sing praises to your name , O Most High ; Psa 92:2 to declare your steadfast love in the morning , and your faithfulness by night , Psa 92:3 to the music of the lute and the harp , to the melody of the lyre . Psa 92:4 For you, O Lord , have made me glad by your work ; at the works of your hands I sing for joy . Psa 92:5 How great are your works , O Lord ! Your thoughts are very deep ! Psa 92:6 The stupid man cannot know ; the fool cannot understand this : Psa 92:7 that though the wicked sprout like grass and all evildoers flourish , they are doomed to destruction forever ; Psa 92:8 but you, O Lord , are on high forever . Psa 92:9 For behold , your enemies , O Lord , for behold , your enemies shall perish ; all evildoers shall be scattered . Psa 92:10 But you have exalted my horn like that of the wild ox ; you have poured over me fresh oil . Psa 92:11 My eyes have seen the downfall of my enemies ; my ears have heard the doom of my evil assailants . Psa 92:12 The righteous flourish like the palm tree and grow like a cedar in Lebanon .

**sing praises** — Psa 92:1  ·  `H2167:singprais#9`

_Story_

This is worship named as the right and good thing to do — "it is good to sing praises to your name, O Most High." No particular event prompts it; the singing is simply the day's fitting occupation. It belongs to the worshipper, aimed at God's name as the Most High, and it comes hand in hand with giving thanks. Praise and thankfulness together are held up as what a person is simply meant to do — the soul's proper work.

_Reading_

The characteristic is sung praise (zamar), the sense (101) being song to God's name — "to sing praises to your name, O Most High." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is named; the singing is declared simply "good," a fitting occupation rather than a triggered response. No seat (104) is recorded; the singing is the worshipper's whole act, not an organ's. The bearer (105) is the worshipper; the operation (106) is to sing praises; the target (107) is God's name, under the title "Most High." The manner (108) is recorded as none. The coupling (112) is given at the locus as "paired with giving thanks," so that thanksgiving and sung praise are one twinned act opening the Sabbath song. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the singing ungraded and unopposed, its outcome unstated — the verse commends it as good and fit. On the inner being: here worship is presented as the day's proper business — the inner life finding its right occupation in singing to the Most High, thanksgiving and song joined as what it is simply good to do.

*Why this reading differs:* This zamar reading is distinguished by its framing as what is "good" to do and its coupling with giving thanks, rather than being a response to a named mercy (Psa 57/59/61), instrumental worship of the Holy One (Psa 71:22), or an answer to God's judgment (Psa 75:9). Here the singing is commended as the fitting, right occupation of the day itself — praise as duty and delight rather than reaction, marking a reflective, endorsing note within the family.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7669620, 7669621, 7669622, 7669623, 7669624, 7669625, 7669626, 7669627, 7669629, 7669630, 7669628</sub>

**sing for joy** — Psa 92:4  ·  `H7442:singforjoy#4`

_Story_

This is joy breaking into song at the sight of God's works — "at the works of your hands I sing for joy." The verse itself gives the reason: God's doings have made the psalmist glad. It is the person's own response, aimed at what God has made and done. And the gladness and the singing are really one thing: the joy stirred by God's works simply overflows into song, unable to stay quiet.

_Reading_

The characteristic is joyful song (ranan), the sense (101) being to sing for joy — "at the works of your hands I sing for joy." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is formally recorded in the field, yet the verse itself names the ground: God's work, which has made the psalmist glad — the singing is the direct response to what God has done. No seat (104) is recorded; the singing is the whole self's act, not an organ's. The bearer (105) is the psalmist; the operation (106) is to sing for joy; the target (107) is the works of God's hands, the deeds that stir the song. The manner (108) is recorded as none. The coupling (112) is given at the locus as "paired with the gladness," so that being made glad and singing for joy are one continuous movement — gladness overflowing into song. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the singing ungraded and unopposed, its outcome unstated — the point is the overflow. On the inner being: God's works stir gladness in the self, and that gladness cannot stay contained — it breaks out as joyful song, the inner response to seeing what God has done.

*Why this reading differs:* This ranan reading differs from its sibling at Psa 84:2, where the whole self (heart and flesh) sings for joy out of longing for God's presence, by being a direct response to God's works: gladness stirred by what God has done overflows into song. Where Psa 84 is longing turned to song, Psa 92 is accomplished deed turned to song — the shift from ache-driven to work-driven joy is a meaningful distinction of what occasions the same exultation.

## Psa 95:1-2

> Psa 95:1 Oh come , let us sing to the Lord ; let us make a joyful noise to the rock of our salvation ! Psa 95:2 Let us come into his presence with thanksgiving ; let us make a joyful noise to him with songs of praise !

**sing** — Psa 95:1  ·  `H7442:sing#1`

_Story_

This is a call to come and sing — "Oh come, let us sing to the Lord." It isn't traced to a single event; it is an invitation to worship, with God named as the rock of our salvation. It belongs to the whole gathering, aimed at the Lord, and it comes joined to a joyful shout. The community's way of drawing near to the God who saves them is simply to sing together and make a joyful noise.

_Reading_

The characteristic is singing (ranan), the sense (101) being the summons to sing — "Oh come, let us sing to the Lord." The type (102) is action. No source (103) is named; this is a call to worship, not a response traced to a prior cause, though the parallel line names God as "the rock of our salvation." No seat (104) is recorded; the singing is the whole gathered body's act, not an organ's. The bearer (105) is the worshippers, summoned together; the operation (106) is to sing; the target (107) is the Lord. The manner (108) is recorded as none. The coupling (112) is given at the locus as "paired with the joyful noise," so that singing and the joyful shout to the rock of salvation are one act of approach. The silences on intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) and prohibition (113) leave the singing ungraded and unopposed, its outcome unstated — the verse is an invitation, not a report. On the inner being: this is the collective inner life called out toward God — worship summoned as song and joyful noise, the community's approach to the rock of its salvation taking the form of shared praise.
