## Psa 96:2-4

> Psa 96:1 Oh sing to the Lord a new song; sing to the Lord, all the earth! Psa 96:2 Sing to the Lord , bless his name ; tell of his salvation from day to day . Psa 96:3 Declare his glory among the nations , his marvelous works among all the peoples ! Psa 96:4 For great is the Lord , and greatly to be praised ; he is to be feared above all gods .

**sing** — Psa 96:1  ·  `H7891:sing#11`

_Story_

Here the call is not just to sing but to sing something new to the LORD - praise made fresh rather than repeated by habit. And it is asked of everyone, the whole earth. The verse never says what has prompted this newness; it comes as a summons, not as a reaction to some named event. Nothing places the impulse in one part of a person, and nothing is said about its force or what it brings about. What stands is simply the pull toward fresh, God-directed praise, offered by all.

_Reading_

The characteristic is singing (101 sense: shir), an action (102 type), and here the operation (106) is simply 'sing', but its target (107) is distinctive: 'a new song to the LORD'. The novelty is the point - the interior does not merely repeat received praise but generates fresh praise, worship renewed rather than rote. The bearer (105) is 'all the earth' (inferred), so this making-new is asked of the whole human range, and the coupling (112) external:god keeps the whole motion directed at God; the locus (116) is 'paired with the new song', reinforcing that this is worship reinvented. The source (103) is absent, and here the silence carries weight: no cause is named for the freshness, so the new song is presented as self-arising summons, not extracted from a fresh deliverance the verse specifies. There is no seat (104: none) - the renewal is not seated in a faculty but treated as a whole-person turning. Manner (108) none, intensity (109) absent, specifier (110) absent, effect (111) absent: the verse gives the directed, renewed act stripped of any account of how, how hard, or with what result. No prohibition (113). The inner process traced: an uncaused impulse toward freshness issues as newly-made song aimed at God, spread over all the earth, with no measured intensity and no stated outcome - the movement of worship refusing to stay old.

*Why this reading differs:* The sibling readings of this verb include tightly-grounded personal songs (Psa 59:16 sung because God has been a fortress; Psa 89:1 of his steadfast love forever) and the striking negative case of exiles unable to sing (Psa 137:4). This reading is marked out by its 'new song' target and its cosmic bearer ('all the earth'), and by the absence of any stated cause; the difference is meaningful - it foregrounds the renewing, generative side of the characteristic rather than gratitude for a specific act.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7670747, 7670748, 7670749, 7670750, 7670751, 7670752, 7670753, 7670754, 7670756, 7670757, 7670755</sub>

**sing** — Psa 96:1  ·  `H7891:sing#12`

_Story_

The call to sing here reaches out to everyone: all the earth is summoned to sing to the LORD. It stands next to the call for a fresh song, but its own emphasis is width - praise for all peoples, not just one voice. The verse gives no reason or trigger; the singing is simply commanded. Nothing locates it in the heart or throat, and nothing is said about how strongly it is sung or what it accomplishes. It is worship at its most far-reaching - a single God-directed song stretched across the whole world.

_Reading_

This is again singing (101 sense: shir), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'sing', but the accent falls on breadth: the target (107) is simply 'to the LORD' and the bearer (105) is 'all the earth', so the verse widens worship to all peoples rather than deepening one person's song. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with the new song', so this call to the whole earth stands beside the summons to freshness in the same verse. The source (103) is absent - no motivating cause is stated, so the widening of praise is issued as a bare summons, not as the earth's response to a named intervention; the silence reads as worship commanded universally, prior to any argued reason. There is no seat (104: none): the singing is not fixed in an organ but asked of the whole person, indeed the whole earth. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) all absent - the verse names the directed act without describing its tone, force, or fruit. No prohibition (113). Traced as inner process: an uncaused impulse issues as song directed at the LORD, extended across all the earth, with no measured heat and no stated outcome - the characteristic at its most outward-spreading.

*Why this reading differs:* Where sibling readings often narrow to the psalmist's own voice grounded in a specific mercy (Psa 13:6; 57:7; 104:33), this reading pushes to the opposite pole - 'all the earth' as bearer, target simply 'to the LORD', and no stated cause. The distinction from its own near-twin (sing#11) is real: that reading stresses the newness of the song, this one stresses the universality of the singers; both belong to the same verse but pull the characteristic in different directions.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7670758, 7670759, 7670760, 7670761, 7670762, 7670763, 7670764, 7670765, 7670767, 7670768, 7670766</sub>

**sing** — Psa 96:2  ·  `H7891:sing#10`

_Story_

In this verse to sing is also to bless: the same breath that lifts a song speaks well of the LORD's name. And it is not one person's song but the whole earth's - everyone is called into it. Nothing in the verse says what stirred the singing; it simply arrives as a command, not traced back to any rescue or feeling. Nor is it lodged in the heart or the throat in particular, and nothing is said about how loud it is or what it achieves. What we are left with is the plain shape of praise itself - a movement of the inner life turned entirely outward toward God.

_Reading_

The inner act here is singing (101 sense: shir), classed as an action (102 type), and in this verse it is welded to a second motion: the operation (106) is 'sing / bless', so the same interior impulse that lifts a song also blesses the LORD's name. The bearer (105) is 'all the earth' (inferred) - not one worshipper but the whole created human range, so the characteristic is presented as universal rather than private. Its target (107) is the LORD's name, and the coupling (112) is external:god, confirming that the song moves outward toward God, not back on the self; the locus (116) notes it is paired with blessing his name, which is what the sung praise does with its breath. Every internal-cause field is silent, and the silence is itself the finding: the source (103) is absent, so no prior stimulus is named - the song is issued as a summons, uncaused within the verse, not traced to a rescue or a felt emotion. There is no seat (104: none), so the singing is not located in an organ of the inner life - it is treated as a whole-person act, not the work of heart or throat as such. The manner (108) is none and the intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all absent, so the verse gives us the pure directed act without describing how loudly, in what register, or to what result it is sung. There is no prohibition (113). Read as inner process: an unnamed impulse issues as sung, name-blessing praise aimed wholly at God, spread across all the earth, with no measured heat and no stated outcome - worship as bare, universal, God-directed motion.

*Why this reading differs:* Across its siblings this same verb of singing ranges from the private psalmist singing for God's bounty (Psa 13:6) or vowing to sing lifelong (Psa 104:33) to whole kingdoms and kings singing (Psa 68:32; 138:5), and even to exiles who cannot sing the LORD's song in a strange land (Psa 137:4). This reading sits at the widest, most corporate end - the bearer is 'all the earth' - and, unlike siblings where singing is grounded in a stated cause (God's strength, his steadfast love), here the source is silent; the difference is meaningful, marking this as a universal summons rather than a response to a named deliverance.

## Psa 96:6-13

> Psa 96:6 Splendor and majesty are before him; strength and beauty are in his sanctuary . Psa 96:7 Ascribe to the Lord , O families of the peoples , ascribe to the Lord glory and strength ! Psa 96:8 Ascribe to the Lord the glory due his name ; bring an offering , and come into his courts ! Psa 96:9 Worship the Lord in the splendor of holiness ; tremble before him, all the earth ! Psa 96:10 Say among the nations , “The Lord reigns ! Yes , the world is established ; it shall never be moved ; he will judge the peoples with equity .” Psa 96:11 Let the heavens be glad , and let the earth rejoice ; let the sea roar , and all that fills it; Psa 96:12 let the field exult , and everything in it! Then shall all the trees of the forest sing for joy Psa 96:13 before the Lord , for he comes , for he comes to judge the earth . He will judge the world in righteousness , and the peoples in his faithfulness .

**Ascribe** — Psa 96:7  ·  `H3051:ascrib#2`

_Story_

To ascribe is to give God what already belongs to him - here, glory and strength handed over to the LORD. And it is asked not of a single worshipper but of whole clans and nations. The verse names no reason or trigger; the giving is simply commanded, worship treated as a debt rather than a burst of feeling. Nothing fixes it in one part of a person, and nothing is said about how intensely it is done or what it brings about. It is the inner life doing something quiet and deliberate: rendering to God his due.

_Reading_

The characteristic is ascribing (101 sense: yahab), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'ascribe', and its inner logic is rendering-to: the self gives God what is his due rather than expressing an emotion. The bearer (105) is 'the families of the peoples' (inferred) - a wide, corporate human body, the clans of the nations - and the target (107) is 'glory and strength to the LORD', so the movement hands over to God the very attributes that are his. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) is 'paired with the repeated ascribing', for the verse doubles the call. The source (103) is absent, and the silence is telling: no prior cause is named, so the ascribing is issued as a summons, worship as a debt to be paid rather than a reaction the verse traces to a felt stimulus. There is no seat (104: none) - the act is not lodged in an organ of the inner life but asked of the whole person and the whole clan. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent: the verse names the directed act without its tone, force, or result. No prohibition (113). As inner process: an uncaused, dutiful impulse issues as the surrender of glory and strength to God, borne by the families of the peoples, aimed wholly at the LORD, with no measured heat and no stated outcome - worship as the giving of what is owed.

*Why this reading differs:* The one earlier sibling of this rare verb (Psa 29:1) sets the same summons on the lips of heavenly beings. This reading shifts the bearer down to earth - 'the families of the peoples' - so the same act of rendering glory now belongs to the nations rather than the angelic court; the difference is meaningful, moving the characteristic from the heavenly assembly into universal human worship.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7670813, 7670814, 7670815, 7670816, 7670817, 7670818, 7670819, 7670820, 7670822, 7670823, 7670821</sub>

**Ascribe** — Psa 96:7  ·  `H3051:ascrib#3`

_Story_

The verse says it twice: the peoples are to give the LORD glory and strength - and give it. The doubling is the emphasis; the insistence itself carries the weight, not any stated measure of feeling. As before, no reason is named and the act is simply commanded, treated as tribute owed to God. Nothing locates it in one part of a person, and nothing is said about its force or its result. It is the inner life handing over to God what is his, and being told to do it again.

_Reading_

This is the second stroke of ascribing (101 sense: yahab) in the same verse, an action (102 type), operation (106) 'ascribe', with bearer (105) 'the peoples' and target (107) 'glory and strength to the LORD'. The repetition itself is the finding: the interior is pressed to render, and render again, so the characteristic is intensified by insistence rather than by a stated degree. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with the first ascribing', tying this reading to its twin. The source (103) is absent - no cause is named, so the giving is a bare summons, worship as owed tribute rather than traced response. There is no seat (104: none): the act is not seated in a faculty but asked of the peoples as a whole. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so even the doubling is not quantified - the verse insists by repeating, not by measuring. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an uncaused, dutiful impulse issues as the surrender of glory and strength to God, borne by the peoples, aimed at the LORD, unmeasured and without stated outcome - the same act said twice, worship reinforced by iteration.

*Why this reading differs:* Against the heavenly-beings reading (Psa 29:1) this is earthly; against its own verse-twin (ascrib#2, 'the families of the peoples') the bearer is stated more simply as 'the peoples'. The distinction is one of iteration within the verse - this reading captures the second, reinforcing call - and preserving it keeps the deliberate doubling of the summons rather than collapsing the two strokes into one.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7670824, 7670825, 7670826, 7670827, 7670828, 7670829, 7670830, 7670831, 7670833, 7670834, 7670832</sub>

**Ascribe** — Psa 96:8  ·  `H3051:ascrib#4`

_Story_

Now the giving becomes exact: the peoples are to render the LORD the very honour that his name is owed - and to bring an offering with it, so the inner tribute walks in alongside a gift in the hands. No reason is stated; the act is simply the due being paid. Nothing fixes it in one part of a person, and nothing is said about its intensity or what it produces. It is the inner life measuring out to God precisely what fits his name, and carrying a gift to match.

_Reading_

The third ascribing (101 sense: yahab), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'ascribe', now specifies its object more precisely: the target (107) is 'the glory due God's name' - not merely glory, but the exact honour owed. The bearer (105) is 'the peoples' and the coupling (112) external:god; the locus (116) is 'paired with bringing an offering', so the inner act of rendering is set beside a concrete gift carried into the courts, worship as both interior tribute and material offering. The source (103) is absent: no cause is named, so the giving remains a commanded due rather than a traced reaction; the silence marks it as obligation, not impulse. There is no seat (104: none) - the act belongs to the whole worshipping people, not to a faculty. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse gives the precise object ('the glory due his name') without quantifying the act. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an uncaused, dutiful impulse issues as the surrender of the honour owed to God's name, borne by the peoples, paired with a physical offering, aimed at God, unmeasured and without stated result - worship rendering exactly what is fitting.

*Why this reading differs:* Among the ascribing readings this one alone specifies the object as 'the glory due his name' and pairs the act with bringing an offering, where the others (Psa 29:1; 96:7 twice) call simply for 'glory and strength'. The difference is meaningful: the characteristic here tightens from general acclaim to owed, fitting tribute joined to a material gift, showing the same verb move from acclamation toward exact obligation.

## Psa 98:1-6

> A Psalm . Psa 98:1 Oh sing to the Lord a new song , for he has done marvelous things ! His right hand and his holy arm have worked salvation for him. Psa 98:2 The Lord has made known his salvation ; he has revealed his righteousness in the sight of the nations . Psa 98:3 He has remembered his steadfast love and faithfulness to the house of Israel . All the ends of the earth have seen the salvation of our God . Psa 98:4 Make a joyful noise to the Lord , all the earth ; break forth into joyous song and sing praises ! Psa 98:5 Sing praises to the Lord with the lyre , with the lyre and the sound of melody ! Psa 98:6 With trumpets and the sound of the horn make a joyful noise before the King , the Lord !

**sing** — Psa 98:1  ·  `H7891:sing#13`

_Story_

Once more the call is for a new song to the LORD, sung by the gathered worshippers - and this time the verse hints at why: he has done wonderful things. Yet the singing itself is set down as a command rather than tied tightly to a cause, arriving as a summons to fresh praise. Nothing places it in one part of a person, and nothing is said about how loudly it is sung or what it achieves. It stands beside a joyful shout, the inner life breaking into new song after God has acted.

_Reading_

The characteristic is singing (101 sense: shir), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'sing', with target (107) 'a new song to the LORD'. As in Psalm 96 the freshness is the point, but here the surrounding verse supplies the ground the dimension row leaves unnamed - the song is called for because 'he has done marvellous things'. The bearer (105) is 'the worshippers' (inferred), a gathered human body, and the coupling (112) is external:god; the locus (116) is 'paired with the joyful noise', so the new song stands beside jubilant sound. Formally the source (103) is absent in the coded row, and that silence is worth reading: the characteristic itself is issued as summons, its cause left outside the item even though the verse gestures at God's wonders - the sung act is presented as command, not as measured emotional output. There is no seat (104: none): the singing is a whole-person act, not an organ's work. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent - the verse names the directed act without tone, force, or result. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an impulse toward freshness (its cause hovering in the verse but uncoded) issues as newly-made song aimed at the LORD, borne by the worshippers, unmeasured, without stated outcome - worship renewed in the wake of God's works.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading shares the 'new song' target with Psa 96:1 but differs in bearer - 'the worshippers' rather than 'all the earth' - narrowing the cosmic summons to the assembly. Set against the personal, cause-grounded siblings (Psa 59:16; 104:33), it keeps freshness and corporate voice together; the difference from its Psalm 96 twin is a matter of scope, meaningfully drawing the same renewing act in from the whole earth to the congregation.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7671327, 7671328, 7671329, 7671330, 7671331, 7671332, 7671333, 7671334, 7671336, 7671337, 7671335</sub>

**sing praises** — Psa 98:4  ·  `H2167:singprais#10`

_Story_

To sing praises here is to make music to God - and it bursts out of a joyful shout, the two belonging together. It is asked of all the earth, not one singer. Unlike many of its kin, this call names no reason; the praise is simply commanded, without a stated cause behind it. Nothing lodges it in one part of a person, and nothing is said about its force or what it accomplishes. It is the whole world's inner life turned to music before God.

_Reading_

The characteristic is singing praises (101 sense: zamar), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'sing praises', with bearer (105) 'all the earth' and target (107) 'to God'. This is the making of music-praise, and the verse plants it in an eruption: it follows 'break forth into joyous song'. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with the joyous song', so the sung praise and the joyful outburst belong together. The source (103) is absent - here a real shift from this verb's usual pattern, where a cause is often named (God's faithfulness, his being a fortress); its absence presents this reading as bare summons, praise commanded of the whole earth without a stated trigger. There is no seat (104: none): the act is not seated in a faculty but asked of all the earth as one. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse gives the directed act without measuring its heat or naming its fruit, even though the neighbouring 'joyous' colours the scene. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an uncaused impulse issues as sung praise aimed at God, borne by all the earth, coupled to a joyful outburst, unmeasured and without stated outcome - worship as music offered universally.

*Why this reading differs:* Many siblings of this verb are grounded and personal - the psalmist sings praises because God is his fortress and shows steadfast love (Psa 59:17), in response to God's love and faithfulness (Psa 61:8), among the nations (Psa 57:9). This reading differs by taking 'all the earth' as bearer and by leaving the source silent; the shift from a caused, private praise to an uncaused, universal summons is meaningful, marking the corporate, commanded pole of the characteristic.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7671360, 7671361, 7671362, 7671363, 7671364, 7671365, 7671366, 7671367, 7671369, 7671370, 7671368</sub>

**joyous song** — Psa 98:4  ·  `H7442:joyoussong#1`

_Story_

This is worship that bursts out: the whole earth breaking into joyful song to God. The word itself carries the joy, so even though nothing separately measures how glad or how loud it is, the exuberance is unmistakable. Strikingly, no cause is named - an outburst that seems to need a trigger simply erupts on command, without the verse tracing where it came from. Nothing fixes it in one part of a person, and nothing is said about what it brings about. It is joy overflowing into song before God, offered by all.

_Reading_

The characteristic is joyous song (101 sense: ranan), an action (102 type), and the operation (106) is 'break into joyous song' - the verb of a sudden outburst, praise erupting rather than measured out. The bearer (105) is 'all the earth' (inferred) and the target (107) is 'to God'; the coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with singing praises', so the outburst and the sung praise beside it form one jubilant motion. The source (103) is absent, and the silence reads pointedly here: an outburst by nature seems to demand a trigger, yet none is coded, so the joyous song is presented as self-arising exultation, an eruption the verse commands without tracing to a cause. There is no seat (104: none): the burst is not located in heart or voice but asked of all the earth as a whole. Manner (108) none - notable for an act whose whole colour is joy, yet the verse leaves the manner uncoded, letting the sense-word 'joyous' carry the tone rather than a separate manner field. Intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so even this exuberant act is given without a measured degree or a stated result. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an uncaused impulse erupts as joyful song aimed at God, borne by all the earth, coupled to sung praise, unmeasured and without named outcome - worship as spontaneous overflow.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7671349, 7671350, 7671351, 7671352, 7671353, 7671354, 7671355, 7671356, 7671358, 7671359, 7671357</sub>

**sing praises** — Psa 98:5  ·  `H2167:singprais#11`

_Story_

Here singing praises takes up an instrument: the worshippers make music to the LORD on the lyre, so the praise of the heart passes into the strings of the hands. No reason is named; the praise is simply called for. Nothing locates it in one part of a person, and nothing is said about how strongly it is played or what it produces. It stands beside a joyful shout - the inner life turned to God and given a lyre to carry its song.

_Reading_

Again singing praises (101 sense: zamar), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'sing praises', with bearer (105) 'the worshippers' and target (107) 'to the LORD with the lyre'. What distinguishes this reading is the instrument: the sung praise is now embodied in music played on the lyre, so the inner act takes on a physical, crafted vehicle. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with the joyful noise'. The source (103) is absent - no cause is named, so the instrumented praise is issued as command rather than as traced response; the silence marks it as summons. There is no seat (104: none): the act is not organ-located but asked of the worshippers, and its 'seat' is if anything the instrument rather than a faculty. Manner (108) none in the coded row, though 'with the lyre' functions as a means; intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse names the directed, instrumented act without measuring its force or naming its fruit. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an uncaused impulse issues as sung praise carried on the lyre, aimed at the LORD, borne by the worshippers, unmeasured and without stated outcome - worship becoming music with an instrument in hand.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading differs from its near-twin in the same passage (singprais#10, bearer 'all the earth') by narrowing to 'the worshippers' and, above all, by naming the lyre - the praise is now instrument-borne. Against the caused, personal siblings (Psa 71:22, praise on the harp grounded in God's faithfulness), it keeps the instrument but drops the stated cause; the distinction is meaningful, foregrounding the musical, embodied vehicle of the characteristic.

## Psa 99:1-7

> Psa 99:1 The Lord reigns ; let the peoples tremble ! He sits enthroned upon the cherubim ; let the earth quake ! Psa 99:2 The Lord is great in Zion ; he is exalted over all the peoples . Psa 99:3 Let them praise your great and awesome name ! Holy is he ! Psa 99:4 The King in his might loves justice . You have established equity ; you have executed justice and righteousness in Jacob . Psa 99:5 Exalt the Lord our God ; worship at his footstool ! Holy is he ! Psa 99:6 Moses and Aaron were among his priests , Samuel also was among those who called upon his name . They called to the Lord , and he answered them . Psa 99:7 In the pillar of the cloud he spoke to them; they kept his testimonies and the statute that he gave them . Psa 99:8 O Lord our God, you answered them; you were a forgiving God to them, but an avenger of their wrongdoings. Psa 99:9 Exalt the Lord our God , and worship at his holy mountain ; for the Lord our God is holy !

**praise** — Psa 99:3  ·  `H3034:prais#12`

_Story_

Praise here is the response that holiness draws out: the peoples are called to praise God's great and awesome name, and the verse answers itself, 'Holy is he!' The nearness of that awe is felt, yet the praise is set down as a command rather than tightly tied to it. Nothing places the act in one part of a person, and nothing is said about how strong the awe is or what the praise brings about. It is the inner life of whole peoples turned toward a holy God, giving him the acclaim his name deserves.

_Reading_

The characteristic is praise (101 sense: yadah), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'praise', with bearer (105) 'the peoples' and target (107) 'God's awesome name'. Here praise is the fitting response to holiness: the verse frames it with 'Holy is he!', so the inner act rises as the answer that God's great and awesome name calls for. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with the holiness of God'. The source (103) is absent in the coded row, yet the verse's 'awesome name' and 'Holy is he' hover as the ground; formally the silence presents praise as summons - the peoples are told to praise, the cause left outside the item even as the context names holiness. There is no seat (104: none): the act is not seated in a faculty but asked of the peoples as a body. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse gives the directed act without measuring how the awe is felt or what it yields. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an impulse (its ground the holiness voiced beside it, though uncoded) issues as praise aimed at God's awesome name, borne by the peoples, unmeasured and without stated outcome - worship as the answer holiness demands.

*Why this reading differs:* This verb ranges widely among its siblings: praise broadcast among the nations (Psa 18:49), the sober argument that the dust cannot praise (Psa 30:9), praise misdirected horizontally at the prosperous (Psa 49:18), even the wrath of man made to render praise (Psa 76:10). This reading is a straightforward corporate acclaim of God's holy name, its bearer the peoples; the difference from the darker or introspective siblings is meaningful - here the characteristic is simply the fit answer to holiness, without argument or misdirection.

**Exalt** — Psa 99:5  ·  `H7311:exalt#2`

_Story_

To exalt is to lift God high, and here the worshippers raise the LORD their God even as they bow at his footstool - the same breath that lifts him up bends them low. No reason is named; the lifting is simply called for. Nothing locates it in one part of a person, and nothing is said about how high the adoration reaches or what it produces. It is the inner life raising God above itself, adoration and humility in one motion.

_Reading_

The characteristic is exalting (101 sense: rum), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'exalt', with bearer (105) 'the worshippers' and target (107) 'the LORD our God'. The inner logic is lifting-up: the self raises God high, a movement of adoration that ascends toward its object. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with worshipping at his footstool', so the lifting-up of God is set beside the worshipper's bowing at his footstool - the same verse pairs height and lowliness, God exalted while the worshipper stoops. The source (103) is absent: no cause is named, so the exalting is issued as summons rather than traced response; the silence marks it as commanded adoration. There is no seat (104: none) - the act is not organ-located but asked of the worshippers as a whole. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse gives the directed act without measuring its lift or naming its result. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an uncaused impulse issues as the lifting-up of God, borne by the worshippers, aimed at the LORD, paired with self-lowering worship, unmeasured and without stated outcome - adoration that raises God as it bends the self.

*Why this reading differs:* The first sibling of this verb (Psa 66:7) points the opposite way: there the rebellious exalt themselves against God - the same verb turned into self-elevation and defiance. Read against that descent, this ascending exaltation of the LORD is its mirror image; the difference is deeply meaningful, showing one interior motion ('lifting high') that can raise either God or the self, worship or rebellion.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7671520, 7671521, 7671522, 7671523, 7671524, 7671525, 7671526, 7671527, 7671529, 7671530, 7671528</sub>

**Exalt** — Psa 99:9  ·  `H7311:exalt#3`

_Story_

The psalm ends where it turned before: lift the LORD our God high. But the setting rises with it - earlier the worshippers bowed at his footstool, now they worship at his holy mountain, and the adoration climbs with the ground. No reason is given; the repeated call is itself the emphasis. Nothing fixes the act in one part of a person, and nothing is said about its force or its result. It is the inner life raising God once more, the psalm sealing its worship at the summit.

_Reading_

This is exalting (101 sense: rum) again, an action (102 type), operation (106) 'exalt', bearer (105) 'the worshippers', target (107) 'the LORD our God' - the closing refrain of the psalm, the same lifting-up repeated as a frame. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with worshipping at his holy mountain', so where verse 5 set the exalting beside his footstool, here it stands beside his holy mountain: the adoration climbs from footstool to mountain, God lifted at the height of holiness. The source (103) is absent - no cause is named, so the refrain is bare summons, its recurrence the emphasis rather than a stated reason. There is no seat (104: none): the act belongs to the worshippers as a body, not to a faculty. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so even as a climactic refrain the act is unquantified - it insists by returning, not by measuring. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an uncaused impulse issues as the lifting-up of God, borne by the worshippers, aimed at the LORD, now paired with his holy mountain, unmeasured and without stated outcome - the psalm's adoration returning to seal itself.

*Why this reading differs:* Against the self-exalting rebellious of Psa 66:7 this is again God-directed adoration, and against its own verse-5 twin the pairing rises from 'his footstool' to 'his holy mountain'. The difference is meaningful on both counts: it separates worship's lifting-up from pride's self-elevation, and within the psalm it marks an ascent - the refrain returning at a higher, holier vantage.

## Psa 100:1-2

> A Psalm for giving thanks . Psa 100:1 Make a joyful noise to the Lord , all the earth ! Psa 100:2 Serve the Lord with gladness ! Come into his presence with singing ! Psa 100:3 Know that the Lord, he is God! It is he who made us, and we are his; we are his people, and the sheep of his pasture.

**singing** — Psa 100:2  ·  `H7445:sing#1`

_Story_

Here singing is how you arrive: the worshippers come into God's presence with song, so the approach itself is set to music, carried along by gladness. No reason is named; the singing is simply the way in, called for as a command. Nothing places it in one part of a person, and nothing is said about how joyful or how loud it is, or what it achieves. It is the inner life drawing near to God, and doing so in song.

_Reading_

The characteristic is singing (101 sense: renanah), an action (102 type), and the operation (106) is 'come with singing' - the song is not static but a mode of approach, the interior drawing near to God in sung joy. The bearer (105) is 'the worshippers' and the target (107) 'into God's presence', so the movement is toward God's very presence, with the coupling (112) external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with gladness' - the singing rides on gladness in the same command to 'serve the LORD with gladness'. The source (103) is absent: no cause is named, so the singing approach is issued as summons, worship commanded rather than traced to a stated stimulus. There is no seat (104: none) - the singing is not seated in an organ but is the whole person's way of entering. Manner (108) none in the coded row, though 'with singing' is itself the manner of the coming; intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse gives the directed approach without measuring its joy or naming its result. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an uncaused impulse issues as song that is itself the manner of approach to God, borne by the worshippers, coupled to gladness, unmeasured and without stated outcome - worship as the sung way of coming near.

## Psa 100:4-5

> Psa 100:4 Enter his gates with thanksgiving , and his courts with praise ! Give thanks to him; bless his name ! Psa 100:5 For the Lord is good ; his steadfast love endures forever , and his faithfulness to all generations .

**praise** — Psa 100:4  ·  `H8416:prais#10`

_Story_

Praise here is what you bring through the door: the worshippers enter God's courts with praise, just as they enter his gates with thanksgiving - the adoration is the very way in. No reason is named in the moment, though the next line remembers that the LORD is good and his love lasts forever. Nothing fixes the act in one part of a person, and nothing is said about how strong it is or what it produces. It is the inner life carrying its praise across the threshold into God's presence.

_Reading_

The characteristic is praise (101 sense: tehillah), an action (102 type), and the operation (106) is 'enter with praise' - praise is the thing carried through the gates, the mode of entry into God's courts. The bearer (105) is 'the worshippers' and the target (107) 'God's courts'; the coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with thanksgiving', for the verse doubles it: 'enter his gates with thanksgiving, and his courts with praise'. The source (103) is absent - no cause is named within the item, so the praise-entry is issued as summons; yet verse 5 stands nearby ('the LORD is good; his steadfast love endures forever'), the ground hovering outside the coded row. There is no seat (104: none): praise is not lodged in a faculty but is what the whole worshipper brings through the gate. Manner (108) none in the coded row, though 'with praise' is the manner of entering; intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse gives the directed act without measuring its warmth or naming its fruit. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an uncaused impulse issues as praise that is itself the manner of entry into God's courts, borne by the worshippers, paired with thanksgiving, unmeasured and without stated outcome - worship as the adoration one walks in with.

*Why this reading differs:* Many siblings of this noun of praise are richly grounded - praise prompted by God's steadfast love (Psa 48:10), by deliverance and salvation (Psa 51:15), filling the mouth all day (Psa 71:8). This reading strips the source from the coded item and makes praise instead the manner of entering God's courts, paired with thanksgiving; the difference is meaningful, presenting the characteristic as a threshold-act of approach rather than a response traced to a specific mercy.

## Psa 101:1-8

> A Psalm of David . Psa 101:1 I will sing of steadfast love and justice ; to you, O Lord , I will make music . Psa 101:2 I will ponder the way that is blameless . Oh when will you come to me? I will walk with integrity of heart within my house ; Psa 101:3 I will not set before my eyes anything that is worthless . I hate the work of those who fall away ; it shall not cling to me . Psa 101:4 A perverse heart shall be far from me; I will know nothing of evil . Psa 101:5 Whoever slanders his neighbor secretly I will destroy . Whoever has a haughty look and an arrogant heart I will not endure . Psa 101:6 I will look with favor on the faithful in the land , that they may dwell with me; he who walks in the way that is blameless shall minister to me . Psa 101:7 No one who practices deceit shall dwell in my house ; no one who utters lies shall continue before my eyes . Psa 101:8 Morning by morning I will destroy all the wicked in the land , cutting off all the evildoers from the city of the Lord .

**make music** — Psa 101:1  ·  `H2167:makemusic#1`

_Story_

David opens with music to the LORD - and what follows is a vow to live blamelessly, so the song stands at the head of a resolve to keep his heart clean. Nothing names what stirred the music; it comes as David's own choice, a devotion he undertakes rather than a reaction to some event. Nothing places it in one part of him, and nothing is said about how full or how loud it is, or what it brings about. It is a man setting his life in order before God, and beginning with a song.

_Reading_

The characteristic is making music (101 sense: zamar), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'make music', with bearer (105) 'David' and target (107) 'to the LORD'. Its setting is striking: this sung devotion opens a psalm of moral resolve - 'I will ponder the way that is blameless... I will walk with integrity of heart' - so the music-making frames a vow of integrity, worship and self-governance bound together at the psalm's head. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with the singing'. The source (103) is absent: no prior cause is named, so David's music is issued as resolve, a chosen act of devotion rather than a reaction to a stated event; the silence reads as worship undertaken by decision. There is no seat (104: none) - the music is not organ-located but is David's whole-person devotion, even as the psalm goes on to speak of the 'perverse heart' he will keep far. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse names the directed act without measuring it or naming its result. No prohibition (113) attaches to the music itself, though the psalm is full of things David will not endure. Inner process: a self-chosen, uncaused impulse issues as music aimed at the LORD, borne by David, framing a resolve to integrity, unmeasured and without stated outcome - worship at the doorway of a life ordered toward God.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7671872, 7671873, 7671874, 7671875, 7671876, 7671877, 7671878, 7671879, 7671881, 7671882, 7671880</sub>

**sing** — Psa 101:1  ·  `H7891:sing#14`

_Story_

David sings of steadfast love and justice - and the rest of the psalm is his pledge to live by exactly those things, so the song names the character he means to grow into. It is still turned to the LORD, joined to the music he makes for God. Nothing says what prompted it; it comes as his own resolve. Nothing places it in one part of him, and nothing is said about its force or its result. It is a man singing the virtues he intends to embody, worship and self-shaping in one breath.

_Reading_

The characteristic is singing (101 sense: shir), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'sing', with bearer (105) 'David' and target (107) 'of steadfast love and justice'. Distinctively, the object of the song is not God directly but two virtues - steadfast love and justice - which David then means to embody through the psalm's vow of integrity, so the sung praise names the very qualities he resolves to live by. The coupling (112) is external:god (the singing still turns to the LORD, 'to you, O LORD, I will make music') and the locus (116) 'paired with making music to God'. The source (103) is absent: no cause is named, so the singing is David's chosen resolve rather than a traced response. There is no seat (104: none) - the song is not organ-located but a whole-person act, though the psalm's concern is precisely the ordering of the heart. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse gives the directed act without measuring it or naming its result. No prohibition (113). Inner process: a self-chosen, uncaused impulse issues as song about steadfast love and justice, borne by David, turned toward the LORD, unmeasured and without stated outcome - worship that rehearses the virtues the singer intends to become.

*Why this reading differs:* Where most sibling readings sing directly of God or to his name (Psa 89:1 of his steadfast love; Psa 96 to the LORD), this one takes 'steadfast love and justice' as its subject - the qualities David vows to embody rather than God himself as object. The difference is meaningful: here the characteristic turns reflexive, the song rehearsing the virtues the singer means to become, uniquely binding praise to self-formation.

## Psa 102:18-23

> Psa 102:18 Let this be recorded for a generation to come , so that a people yet to be created may praise the Lord : Psa 102:19 that he looked down from his holy height ; from heaven the Lord looked at the earth , Psa 102:20 to hear the groans of the prisoners , to set free those who were doomed to die , Psa 102:21 that they may declare in Zion the name of the Lord , and in Jerusalem his praise , Psa 102:22 when peoples gather together , and kingdoms , to worship the Lord . Psa 102:23 He has broken my strength in midcourse ; he has shortened my days . Psa 102:26 They will perish , but you will remain ; they will all wear out like a garment . You will change them like a robe , and they will pass away ,

**Praise** — Psa 102:18  ·  `H1984:prais#9`

_Story_

This praise belongs to people who do not yet exist: the writer records his words now so that a generation still to come may one day praise the LORD. Nothing names what will move them; the praise is set down as a purpose, a hope committed to writing. It is fixed in no single part of a person, and nothing is said about how fervent it will be or what it will accomplish. It is the inner life of worship reaching across time - praise saved up in a record, waiting for voices not yet born.

_Reading_

The characteristic is praise (101 sense: halal), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'praise', with bearer (105) 'a people yet to be created' and target (107) 'the LORD'. This reading is unusual for its bearer: the praisers do not yet exist - the verse looks forward, 'let this be recorded... so that a people yet to be created may praise the LORD', so the characteristic is projected into the future, worship written down now for voices not yet born. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with the recorded testimony'. The source (103) is absent - no cause is named for the future praise, so it is issued as intended purpose rather than traced response; the silence marks it as a hope committed to record. There is no seat (104: none): the praise is not organ-located but belongs to a whole future people. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse names the directed act without measuring it or naming its result. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an uncaused impulse, projected forward, issues as praise aimed at the LORD, borne by a people yet to be created, tied to a written testimony, unmeasured and without stated outcome - worship reaching across generations, recorded so it can one day be spoken.

*Why this reading differs:* This verb elsewhere is mostly present and personal - the psalmist boasting in God's word (Psa 56:10), his mouth praising with joyful lips (Psa 63:5), the servants praising the name (Psa 113:1). This reading is set apart by its future, not-yet-existing bearer, praise projected forward and preserved in writing; the difference is meaningful, showing the characteristic reaching beyond the living worshipper to generations still to come.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7672407, 7672408, 7672409, 7672410, 7672411, 7672412, 7672413, 7672414, 7672416, 7672417, 7672415</sub>

**praise** — Psa 102:21  ·  `H8416:prais#11`

_Story_

This praise rises from the rescued: God has heard the groans of prisoners and freed those doomed to die, and now they declare his praise from Jerusalem. The deliverance stands close behind it, though the moment itself simply sets the praise down as the point of the rescue. It is lodged in no single part of a person, and nothing is said about its intensity or its result. It is the inner life of the freed, lifting God's name in the restored city - worship as the answer of those who were let go.

_Reading_

The characteristic is praise (101 sense: tehillah), an action (102 type), and the operation (106) is 'render praise', with bearer (105) 'the redeemed' and target (107) 'to God in Jerusalem'. The setting gives the praise its ground in the verse: it rises from those God has freed - 'to hear the groans of the prisoners, to set free those who were doomed to die, that they may declare... his praise' - so the praise is the released captives' answer, offered from the restored city. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with declaring his name'. The source (103) is absent in the coded row, yet the deliverance of the prisoners hovers as the ground; formally the praise is issued as the purpose of the rescue, its cause left just outside the item. There is no seat (104: none): the praise is not organ-located but belongs to the redeemed as a body. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse names the directed act without measuring it or naming its result. No prohibition (113). Inner process: an impulse (its ground the release of the doomed, though uncoded) issues as praise aimed at God, borne by the redeemed, rising from Jerusalem, paired with declaring his name, unmeasured and without stated outcome - worship as the freed captive's answer in the restored city.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading pairs with prais#9 (halal) in the same passage but uses a different praise-word and a different bearer - 'the redeemed' rather than 'a people yet to be created' - and grounds the praise in a concrete deliverance of prisoners rather than projecting it into the future. Against the source-rich personal siblings (Psa 51:15; 71:8), it keeps the causal ground in the surrounding verse while leaving the coded source silent; the distinction is meaningful, marking this as the freed captives' present answer from Jerusalem.

## Psa 104:31-35

> Psa 104:31 May the glory of the Lord endure forever ; may the Lord rejoice in his works , Psa 104:32 who looks on the earth and it trembles , who touches the mountains and they smoke ! Psa 104:33 I will sing to the Lord as long as I live ; I will sing praise to my God while I have being . Psa 104:34 May my meditation be pleasing to him, for I rejoice in the Lord . Psa 104:35 Let sinners be consumed from the earth , and let the wicked be no more ! Bless the Lord , O my soul ! Praise the Lord !

**sing praises** — Psa 104:33  ·  `H2167:singprais#12`

_Story_

This is a lifelong pledge: the psalmist promises to sing praise to his God for as long as he has breath. Nothing outside stirs it into being; it rises as his own settled resolve. It is fixed in no single part of him, and nothing is said about how fervent it is or what it achieves. It is the inner life binding itself to praise - not for a moment, but for the whole length of a life.

_Reading_

The characteristic is singing praise (101 sense: zamar), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'sing praise', with bearer (105) 'the psalmist' and target (107) 'to God while he has being'. What marks this reading is its temporal reach: the praise is pledged for the whole span of existence - 'I will sing praise to my God while I have being' - so the inner act is stretched across a lifetime, a vow rather than a single burst. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with the singing'. The source (103) is absent - no external cause is named for the vow, so it arises as the psalmist's own settled resolve rather than a traced response; the silence reads as devotion chosen from within. There is no seat (104: none): the praise is not organ-located but is the psalmist's whole-life commitment. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse gives the directed, lifelong act without measuring its warmth or naming its result. No prohibition (113). Inner process: a self-chosen, uncaused impulse issues as sung praise aimed at God, borne by the psalmist, extended over the whole of his being, unmeasured and without stated outcome - worship pledged for as long as life lasts.

*Why this reading differs:* Many siblings of this verb are grounded in a specific mercy (Psa 59:17 God his fortress; Psa 61:8 his steadfast love) or set among the nations (Psa 57:9; 108:3). This reading is distinguished by its lifelong horizon - praise coextensive with 'being' - and by the absence of any named cause; the difference is meaningful, presenting the characteristic as an enduring vow of the self rather than a response to a single event.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7673302, 7673303, 7673304, 7673305, 7673306, 7673307, 7673308, 7673309, 7673311, 7673312, 7673310</sub>

**sing** — Psa 104:33  ·  `H7891:sing#15`

_Story_

The psalmist vows to sing to the LORD for as long as he lives, setting the song beside a lifelong resolve to make music. Behind it lies the whole psalm's wonder at God's works, though the moment simply lays the vow down as his own choice. It is lodged in no single part of him, and nothing is said about how strong the singing is or what it brings about. It is a life given over to song before God - praise meant to run the full length of the singer's days.

_Reading_

The characteristic is singing (101 sense: shir), an action (102 type), operation (106) 'sing', with bearer (105) 'the psalmist' and target (107) 'to the LORD as long as he lives'. Paired in the same verse with the vow to make music, this is the lifelong personal response to all God's works in the psalm - 'I will sing to the LORD as long as I live'. The coupling (112) is external:god and the locus (116) 'paired with singing praise'. The source (103) is absent in the coded row, though the psalm's long meditation on God's works stands behind it; formally the vow arises as the psalmist's own resolve, its cause left outside the item. There is no seat (104: none): the singing is not organ-located but is the psalmist's whole-life commitment. Manner (108) none, intensity (109), specifier (110), effect (111) all absent, so the verse names the directed, lifelong act without measuring it or naming its result. No prohibition (113). Inner process: a self-chosen, uncaused impulse issues as song aimed at the LORD, borne by the psalmist, extended across the whole of his life, unmeasured and without stated outcome - worship pledged to last as long as the singer does.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading pairs with singprais#12 in the same verse - one vows to make music, this one to sing, both stretched across a lifetime. Against the corporate, cosmic siblings ('all the earth', Psa 96) and the exiles unable to sing (Psa 137:4), this is the private, enduring vow of a single worshipper; the difference is meaningful, drawing the characteristic in from universal summons to a personal, lifelong commitment.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7673291, 7673292, 7673293, 7673294, 7673295, 7673296, 7673297, 7673298, 7673300, 7673301, 7673299</sub>

**Praise** — Psa 104:35  ·  `H1984:prais#10`

_Story_

Here the psalmist simply praises God, and it is the very first 'Praise the LORD!' in the whole book of Psalms. Nothing in the verse says what set off this praise; it is not shown as an answer to being saved from anything, but rises on its own to seal a song already sung. The verse does not place the praise in any one part of a person - no single organ of feeling - nor does it tell us how loud or how fierce it is; it just records that the whole self praises. In the same breath the psalmist turns inward and tells his own soul to bless the LORD, so one person is both commanding himself and lifting his voice to God. And this bright praise stands right next to a stark wish that the wicked be swept away, a hard neighbour the verse does not smooth over.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal) and the type (102) is an action, an act the inner life performs rather than a state it undergoes. The bearer (105) is the psalmist, inferred from the closing self-address, and the operation (106) is to praise, directed at its target (107), the LORD, in the terse cry that gives the psalm its final word. The source (103) is marked present:false: nothing in the passage names what triggers the praise, so this movement is not shown answering a rescue or a fear but arising of itself, sealing a hymn already sung. The seat (104) is explicitly none, so the praise is not located in a particular organ or faculty; it is voiced by the whole self rather than lodged in heart or mouth as such. Manner (108), intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all absent, so the reading records the bare fact of praise without telling us how loud, how fervent, what kind, or what it accomplishes - the act stands unqualified. The coupling (112) is external toward God, the single relation this movement establishes; the prohibition (113) is absent, so nothing here restrains or forbids it. The discovery note fixes this as the first Hallelujah of the Psalter, and the locus (116) pairs it with the closing blessing 'Bless the LORD, O my soul,' so within the inner life the same self both turns inward to command its own soul and outward to praise God. The two verses just before it - a wish that sinners be consumed - are read at full weight here: the praise stands immediately beside a longing for the wicked to be no more, and the reading does not soften that adjacency.

*Why this reading differs:* Across the many H1984:prais readings some carry an explicit source-pair (praise prompted by God shown as a fortress in Zion, #1; by the God who works salvation, #7) or a doubled-refrain coupling (#2-4); this reading names no source and couples plainly toward God. Its distinctive marks are its place as the Psalter's first Hallelujah and its locus beside the self-command 'Bless the LORD, O my soul' - praise here is spontaneous and self-turned, capping a creation-hymn rather than answering a rescue, and the difference is meaningful.

## Psa 105:1-11

> Psa 105:1 Oh give thanks to the Lord ; call upon his name ; make known his deeds among the peoples ! Psa 105:2 Sing to him, sing praises to him; tell of all his wondrous works ! Psa 105:3 Glory in his holy name ; let the hearts of those who seek the Lord rejoice ! Psa 105:4 Seek the Lord and his strength ; seek his presence continually ! Psa 105:5 Remember the wondrous works that he has done , his miracles , and the judgments he uttered , Psa 105:6 O offspring of Abraham , his servant , children of Jacob , his chosen ones ! Psa 105:7 He is the Lord our God ; his judgments are in all the earth . Psa 105:8 He remembers his covenant forever , the word that he commanded , for a thousand generations , Psa 105:9 the covenant that he made with Abraham , his sworn promise to Isaac , Psa 105:10 which he confirmed to Jacob as a statute , to Israel as an everlasting covenant , Psa 105:11 saying , “ To you I will give the land of Canaan as your portion for an inheritance .”

**sing praises** — Psa 105:2  ·  `H2167:singprais#13`

_Story_

The worshippers are called to sing praises to God. The command simply summons the singing; it does not, right here, name what stirred it, so the song is issued as a call rather than as a reply to something just received. The verse does not lodge this singing in any one part of a person or say how skilful or how strong it is - it holds only the fact that the self is to make music toward God. It sits right beside two companions: plain singing, and telling out the wonderful things God has done, so the same worship comes out both as melody and as speech.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is to sing praises (zamar) and the type (102) is an action of the inner life. The bearer (105) is the worshippers, inferred from the summons of verse 2, and the operation (106) is to sing praises to their target (107), God. The source (103) is present:false: the imperative gives no stated cause inside this reading, so the movement is issued as a call rather than as a response to a named prompt - though the surrounding psalm will go on to rehearse God's covenant deeds, this reading itself leaves the trigger unspoken. The seat (104) is none, so the singing is not tied to a particular inner organ; it is the whole self summoned to melody. Manner (108) is none and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all absent, so the reading holds only the fact of sung praise - not how skilfully, how strongly, of what kind, or to what result. The coupling (112) is external toward God, the one relation set up, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The locus (116) pairs it with singing and with telling of God's wondrous works, so within the inner life the melodic act stands beside plain narration - the same worship expressed both as song and as speech. The reading captures worship in its imperative, congregational form: a self calling itself and others to make music toward God.

*Why this reading differs:* Earlier H2167:singprais readings often carry an explicit source-pair - singing prompted because God's faithfulness reaches the clouds (Psa 57:9) or because he is the singer's fortress (Psa 59:17) - and a target reaching 'among the nations.' This reading names no source and couples plainly toward God, framed as a corporate imperative rather than a personal vow; the shift from motivated personal praise to an unmotivated summons is the meaningful difference.

**sing** — Psa 105:2  ·  `H7891:sing#16`

_Story_

The worshippers are told to sing to God. The verse gives no reason for the song right here; it is simply a call, not a reply to something named, though the psalm around it goes on to recall God's faithfulness. The singing is not tied to any one part of a person, and nothing tells us how it sounds or how strongly it is felt - only that the self sings toward God. It stands next to two companions, singing praises and telling out what God has done, and the plain act of singing is kept distinct from the making of melody beside it.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is to sing (shir) and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the worshippers, inferred, and the operation (106) is to sing toward the target (107), God. The source (103) is present:false: this reading names no cause for the song, so it stands as a bare summons rather than a response to a stated deed, even though the psalm around it will recite God's covenant faithfulness. The seat (104) is none - the singing is not placed in a single inner organ but sounded by the whole self. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so we are told only that the self sings, not how, how much, of what kind, or to what end. The coupling (112) is external toward God, the sole relation, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The locus (116) pairs this singing with singing praises and with telling of God's works, so this reading is deliberately held distinct from its verse-mate 'sing praises' (zamar): here is the plain act of song, there the making of melody, and the two are not collapsed. The movement is worship in its simplest voiced form - the inner life turned to God in sound.

*Why this reading differs:* Several H7891:sing readings carry an explicit source-pair - singing because God is to be exalted above the heavens (Psa 57:7), because he has been a fortress in distress (Psa 59:16) - or name a fresh 'new song' (Psa 96:1; 98:1). This reading names no source and issues a plain corporate summons; the loss of a stated motive, and its pairing beside the distinct act of making melody, mark it off from the more motivated or crafted songs elsewhere.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7673896, 7673897, 7673898, 7673899, 7673900, 7673901, 7673902, 7673903, 7673905, 7673906, 7673904</sub>

**Glory** — Psa 105:3  ·  `H1984:glory#1`

_Story_

The worshippers are to glory - to boast and exult - in God's holy name. No cause is named, so this is not a reaction to some fresh event but a deliberate turning of the self's boasting outward. The verse does not fix this in any one part of a person, nor say how strong it is or what it produces; it simply records that the self boasts in God rather than in itself. The heart that might have puffed itself up is turned to exult in God's name instead, and it stands right beside the glad heart that seeks him - the boast and the joy moving together.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is to glory (halal) - here in the register of boasting or exulting - and the type (102) is an action of the inner life. The bearer (105) is the worshippers, inferred, and the operation (106) is to glory or boast, with its target (107) being God's holy name. The source (103) is present:false: no cause is named, so the exulting is not shown as an answer to a specific event but as a self-directed act of the inner life fixing its boast outside itself. The seat (104) is none, so the glorying is not lodged in a particular faculty. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so the reading records only that the self boasts in God's name, without measure or stated result. The coupling (112) is external toward God, the one relation, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note draws out the movement's inner logic: this is exulting in God's name rather than in self - the same faculty that could boast in its own strength turned instead outward. The locus (116) pairs it with the seeking hearts that rejoice (v3), so glorying stands beside a heart-level gladness that seeks God: the boast and the joy are companion movements of the one inner life. Read at full weight, this is the inner faculty of self-exaltation redirected - the boast that might have rested on the self now resting on God's name.

*Why this reading differs:* The only sibling reading, H1984:glory#2 (Psa 106:5), has the psalmist glorying together with God's inheritance and pairs the act with rejoicing over God's people. This reading, by contrast, aims the boast at God's holy name itself and pairs it with seeking hearts; the shift of target from the community-shared inheritance to God's name is the meaningful difference between the two.

## Psa 105:40-45

> Psa 105:36 He struck down all the firstborn in their land , the firstfruits of all their strength . Psa 105:38 Egypt was glad when they departed , for dread of them had fallen upon it . Psa 105:40 They asked , and he brought quail , and gave them bread from heaven in abundance . Psa 105:41 He opened the rock , and water gushed out ; it flowed through the desert like a river . Psa 105:42 For he remembered his holy promise , and Abraham , his servant . Psa 105:43 So he brought his people out with joy , his chosen ones with singing . Psa 105:44 And he gave them the lands of the nations , and they took possession of the fruit of the peoples’ toil, Psa 105:45 that they might keep his statutes and observe his laws . Praise the Lord !

**singing** — Psa 105:43  ·  `H7440:sing#1`

_Story_

The rescued people come out of Egypt singing. The verse itself does not spell out a cause for the song, though the surrounding lines tell how God brought them out; the singing is recorded simply as what accompanied their release. It is not tied to any one part of a person, and nothing measures how loud or fervent it is - only that the freed people sing. The singing rides directly on their joy: gladness inside, song outside, the two set side by side as the inner and outer face of the same rescue.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is singing (rinnah), a ringing cry of song, and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the chosen ones, inferred, and the operation (106) is to sing, its target (107) fixed at the moment of the Exodus - the song of a people brought out of Egypt. The source (103) is present:false at the level of this reading: though the surrounding verses recount the deliverance, the reading itself does not encode a stated cause, so the song is registered as an accompaniment of the going-out rather than a labelled response. The seat (104) is none, so the singing is not placed in one inner organ; it is the whole redeemed self voicing joy. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so we have the fact of song without measure or outcome. The coupling (112) is external toward God, the one relation. The prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note reads this as the song of the redeemed, joy voiced in praise, and the locus (116) pairs it with the joy of verse 43 - 'he brought his people out with joy, his chosen ones with singing.' So within the inner life the movement is gladness overflowing into sound: joy is the inner state, singing its audible form. The two are set as parallel companions, the inner and outer face of the same deliverance-response.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7674138, 7674139, 7674140, 7674141, 7674142, 7674143, 7674144, 7674145, 7674147, 7674148, 7674146</sub>

**Praise** — Psa 105:45  ·  `H1984:prais#11`

_Story_

The worshippers praise the LORD in the one word that closes the psalm. Nothing in the line names a fresh reason for it; it comes as a seal on the whole story of God's dealings just told. The praise is not placed in any single part of a person, and nothing measures its force - it is simply the act of praise. It stands right beside the call to keep and obey God's laws, so worship and obedience close the psalm together, the remembered history turned into one upward cry.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal) and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the worshippers, inferred, and the operation (106) is to praise their target (107), the LORD, in the single closing cry of the psalm. The source (103) is present:false: the reading names no immediate trigger, so the Hallelujah stands as a seal rather than a stated reaction, even though the whole psalm behind it has rehearsed God's covenant history. The seat (104) is none, so the praise is not lodged in a particular faculty. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so only the bare act of praise is recorded. The coupling (112) is external toward God, the one relation, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note marks this as the closing Hallelujah sealing the covenant-history, and the locus (116) pairs it with keeping and observing God's statutes (v45) - so within the inner life, praise here sits directly beside obedience: the psalm's stated purpose is that the redeemed 'keep his statutes,' and the praise closes on that note. The movement is worship as a full stop to remembered history, the inner life turning a rehearsed story into a single upward cry set right next to the will to obey.

*Why this reading differs:* Unlike H1984:prais readings that carry an explicit source-pair (praise prompted by God's fortress or salvation) or a doubled refrain, this reading names no source and couples plainly toward God. Its distinctive locus is beside 'keeping and observing' God's statutes: praise here is set next to obedience as a closing Hallelujah, which marks it off from the rescue-prompted or refrain-doubled instances - a meaningful shift toward praise-as-seal.

## Psa 106:1-8

> Psa 106:1 Praise the Lord ! Oh give thanks to the Lord , for he is good , for his steadfast love endures forever ! Psa 106:2 Who can utter the mighty deeds of the Lord , or declare all his praise ? Psa 106:3 Blessed are they who observe justice , who do righteousness at all times ! Psa 106:4 Remember me, O Lord , when you show favor to your people ; help me when you save them , Psa 106:5 that I may look upon the prosperity of your chosen ones , that I may rejoice in the gladness of your nation , that I may glory with your inheritance . Psa 106:6 Both we and our fathers have sinned ; we have committed iniquity ; we have done wickedness . Psa 106:7 Our fathers , when they were in Egypt , did not consider your wondrous works ; they did not remember the abundance of your steadfast love , but rebelled by the sea , at the Red Sea . Psa 106:8 Yet he saved them for his name’s sake, that he might make known his mighty power .

**Praise** — Psa 106:1  ·  `H1984:prais#12`

_Story_

The worshippers open the psalm praising the LORD, and the verse at once gives the reason in plain words - because he is good and his love lasts forever. The praise is not tied to any one part of a person, and nothing measures its strength; it is simply the act of praise, joined here to giving thanks. What is striking is where it sits: this glad opening stands at the front of a song that will go on to confess that the people and their fathers have sinned. The self lifts up God's lasting love just as it braces to admit its own failure.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal) and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the worshippers, inferred, and the operation (106) is to praise the target (107), the LORD, in the opening cry of the psalm. The source (103) is present:false as an encoded field, yet the verse itself immediately gives the ground - 'for he is good, for his steadfast love endures forever' - so the reading registers no separate source-pair while the praise nonetheless opens straight onto God's goodness. The seat (104) is none, so the praise is not fixed in a single organ. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so the bare act is recorded without measure or outcome. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note marks this as the opening Hallelujah of a confession-hymn, and the locus (116) pairs it with giving thanks. The setting is striking and must be read at full weight: this bright opening praise stands at the head of a psalm that will confess 'both we and our fathers have sinned.' So within the inner life the movement is praise placed deliberately before confession - the self opens on God's enduring love precisely as it prepares to own its own failure, and the reading does not filter that tension.

*Why this reading differs:* Against H1984:prais siblings that encode an explicit source-pair or a doubled refrain, this reading logs no source-pair, yet the verse itself supplies the ground ('for he is good') and pairs the praise with thanksgiving. Its distinctive setting - an opening Hallelujah heading a confession of sin - marks it off from the rescue-prompted instances; the meaningful difference is praise deliberately placed before confession.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7674676, 7674677, 7674678, 7674679, 7674680, 7674681, 7674682, 7674683, 7674685, 7674686, 7674684</sub>

**Glory** — Psa 106:5  ·  `H1984:glory#2`

_Story_

The psalmist longs to glory - to exult and boast - together with God's people. This is voiced as a wish rather than a reaction; nothing outside is named as its cause, only the desire to share in the gladness of God's own. The verse does not place this in any one part of a person or measure how strong it is; it holds simply the wish to exult. It comes in a run of longings - to see God's people prosper, to rejoice in their gladness, to glory with them - so the self is reaching to be swept up into a shared joy it does not yet hold.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is to glory (halal), in the register of exulting or boasting, and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the psalmist, inferred, and the operation (106) is to glory, its target (107) being 'with your inheritance' - the psalmist longs to exult together with God's people. The source (103) is present:false: no external cause is encoded, and the glorying is voiced as a desire ('that I may glory') rather than a response - a movement of longing reaching toward a hoped-for exultation. The seat (104) is none, so the exulting is not lodged in a single organ. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so the reading holds the wish to glory without measure or result. The coupling (112) is external toward God, the one relation, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note draws out that this is the psalmist's desire to exult with God's people, boasting in him. The locus (116) pairs it with rejoicing (v5), and the verse strings it in a chain - looking on prosperity, rejoicing in gladness, glorying with the inheritance - so the movement is the inner life's yearning to be gathered into a shared, corporate exultation. It is glory not as present possession but as longed-for participation in the joy of God's people.

*Why this reading differs:* The sibling H1984:glory#1 (Psa 105:3) aims the boast at God's holy name and pairs it with seeking hearts. This reading instead has the psalmist glorying 'with God's inheritance' and pairs it with rejoicing, and it is cast as a longing ('that I may glory') rather than a summons. The shift of target from God's name to shared participation with his people, and from summons to desire, is the meaningful difference.

## Psa 106:10-16

> Psa 106:10 So he saved them from the hand of the foe and redeemed them from the power of the enemy . Psa 106:11 And the waters covered their adversaries ; not one of them was left . Psa 106:12 Then they believed his words ; they sang his praise . Psa 106:13 But they soon forgot his works ; they did not wait for his counsel . Psa 106:14 But they had a wanton craving in the wilderness , and put God to the test in the desert ; Psa 106:15 he gave them what they asked , but sent a wasting disease among them . Psa 106:16 When men in the camp were jealous of Moses and Aaron , the holy one of the Lord ,

**sang** — Psa 106:12  ·  `H7891:sang#1`

_Story_

At the edge of the sea, just rescued, the people believed God's words and sang his praise. The verse does not separately name what set off the song, but its neighbour makes it clear - the singing came straight out of a fresh belief. The song is not placed in any one part of a person or measured for strength; it is simply their praise. And it carries a hard edge: the very next line says they soon forgot what God had done. Faith and song rise together here, and both quickly drain away - a real praise that proved shallow.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is to sing (shir), here in past narration - 'they sang' - and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the fathers, inferred, and the operation (106) is to sing their target (107), God's praise, at the shore of the sea after the rescue. The source (103) is present:false: the reading encodes no stated cause, though the verse-mate makes the trigger plain - 'then they believed his words; they sang his praise' - so the song rides on a fresh belief the reading does not itself label as source. The seat (104) is none, so the singing is not fixed in one inner organ. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so the bare act of sung praise stands unmeasured. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note carries the sting: this is the song of the redeemed at the sea, worship soon forgotten - verse 13 immediately adds 'but they soon forgot his works.' The locus (116) pairs the singing with believing. So the movement must be read at full weight in both directions: the inner life here believes and sings in one motion, and that same inner life is shown, a breath later, forgetting. The reading holds a genuine praise that proves shallow - song and faith rising together, and both draining away.
