## Psa 106:38-48

> Psa 106:36 They served their idols , which became a snare to them. Psa 106:38 they poured out innocent blood , the blood of their sons and daughters , whom they sacrificed to the idols of Canaan , and the land was polluted with blood . Psa 106:39 Thus they became unclean by their acts , and played the whore in their deeds . Psa 106:40 Then the anger of the Lord was kindled against his people , and he abhorred his heritage ; Psa 106:41 he gave them into the hand of the nations , so that those who hated them ruled over them . Psa 106:42 Their enemies oppressed them, and they were brought into subjection under their power . Psa 106:43 Many times he delivered them, but they were rebellious in their purposes and were brought low through their iniquity . Psa 106:44 Nevertheless, he looked upon their distress , when he heard their cry . Psa 106:45 For their sake he remembered his covenant , and relented according to the abundance of his steadfast love . Psa 106:46 He caused them to be pitied by all those who held them captive . Psa 106:47 Save us, O Lord our God , and gather us from among the nations , that we may give thanks to your holy name and glory in your praise . Psa 106:48 Blessed be the Lord , the God of Israel , from everlasting to everlasting ! And let all the people say , “ Amen !” Praise the Lord ! Book Five

**glory** — Psa 106:47  ·  `H7623:glory#1`

_Story_

From the middle of exile and judgment, the people pray to be saved and gathered so that they may give thanks and glory in God's praise. The exulting is not something they now hold; it is what they hope to do once rescued - a triumph prayed for, not yet arrived. The verse does not tie it to any one part of a person or measure its force; it holds only the intended act of joyful praise, set beside giving thanks. The striking thing is that this bright hope rises out of a psalm full of bloodshed and captivity - the self reaching, from inside its punishment, toward a praise it longs one day to sing.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is to glory (shabach), in the register of exulting or triumphing, and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the redeemed, inferred, and the operation (106) is to glory or triumph, its target (107) being 'in God's praise.' The source (103) is present:false: no encoded cause, and the glorying is voiced as a hoped-for outcome of rescue - 'Save us... and gather us... that we may give thanks... and glory in your praise' - so the movement is a longed-for exultation contingent on a deliverance not yet granted. The seat (104) is none, so the exulting is not lodged in a single organ. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so the reading holds only the intended act of triumphant praise. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note reads this as the exulting the gathered would render. The locus (116) pairs it with giving thanks. The setting weighs on the movement: this bright triumph-in-praise is prayed for at the end of a psalm heavy with idolatry, poured-out blood, and captivity - the inner life reaching, from within judgment, toward a praise it hopes to voice once gathered home. It is glory as petitioned goal, not present fact.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7675287, 7675288, 7675289, 7675290, 7675291, 7675292, 7675293, 7675294, 7675296, 7675297, 7675295</sub>

**Praise** — Psa 106:48  ·  `H1984:prais#13`

_Story_

The whole people praise the LORD in the closing cry of the psalm. Nothing names a fresh cause; it comes as a seal on everything before it. The praise is not lodged in any one part of a person or measured for strength - it is simply the act of praise. What sets this one apart is who does it: not a lone singer but the entire congregation, whose 'Amen' the praise crowns, joined to the blessing that ends the psalm. Worship here has become fully shared - one voice passing into the assent of all.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal) and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the people, inferred, and the operation (106) is to praise the target (107), the LORD, in the final cry of the psalm and of Book Four. The source (103) is present:false: no immediate trigger is encoded, so the Hallelujah stands as a corporate seal rather than a stated response. The seat (104) is none, so the praise is not fixed in one faculty. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so only the bare act is recorded. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note fixes this as the closing Hallelujah, and the locus (116) pairs it with the blessing - 'Blessed be the LORD... and let all the people say, Amen! Praise the LORD!' So the distinctive mark of this reading is its bearer: not a single psalmist but the whole people, whose 'Amen' the praise crowns. Within the inner life this is worship become fully corporate - the movement passing from one voice to the congregation's shared assent, sealing a psalm that has just prayed for gathering out of the nations. It is praise as the community's answering 'Amen.'

*Why this reading differs:* Where many H1984:prais readings have a single psalmist or a general 'worshippers' as bearer, this reading's bearer is 'the people' as a whole, and its locus is beside the corporate blessing and 'Amen' that close the psalm. The shift from an individual or generic voice to the entire congregation's answering assent is the meaningful difference.

## Psa 107:28-32

> Psa 107:28 Then they cried to the Lord in their trouble , and he delivered them from their distress . Psa 107:29 He made the storm be still , and the waves of the sea were hushed . Psa 107:30 Then they were glad that the waters were quiet , and he brought them to their desired haven . Psa 107:31 Let them thank the Lord for his steadfast love , for his wondrous works to the children of man ! Psa 107:32 Let them extol him in the congregation of the people , and praise him in the assembly of the elders . Psa 107:34 a fruitful land into a salty waste , because of the evil of its inhabitants .

**Praise** — Psa 107:32  ·  `H1984:prais#14`

_Story_

Sailors who were saved from a deadly storm praise God, and they do it out loud in the gathering of the elders. The verse does not separately label the cause, but the passage makes it plain - they cried out in trouble, God calmed the sea and brought them safely to harbour, and this praise answers that rescue. It is not placed in any one part of a person or measured for strength; it is simply their praise, set beside their lifting God high in the assembly. What stands out is where it happens: not in private but in a public gathering, the rescued turning their gratitude outward for the whole community to hear.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal) and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the sailors, inferred - the storm-tossed of verses 28-30 - and the operation (106) is to praise their target (107), God, 'in the assembly of the elders.' The source (103) is present:false as an encoded field, though the passage supplies the ground: they cried in trouble, he stilled the storm and brought them to their haven, and the praise answers that rescue - yet the reading logs no separate source-pair. The seat (104) is none, so the praise is not fixed in a single organ. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so the bare act is recorded without measure or outcome. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note reads this as the worship of the delivered before the elders, and the locus (116) pairs it with extolling in the congregation. The distinctive feature is the setting of the act: praise is directed not privately but into a public assembly of elders. So within the inner life the movement is gratitude for rescue pressed outward into a communal, witnessed form - the delivered self does not keep its thanks inward but lifts it where the community can hear.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading's bearer is the storm-rescued sailors and its locus is a public assembly of elders, paired with extolling in the congregation - a communal, witnessed praise. That marks it off from H1984:prais instances voiced by a solitary psalmist or as a bare Hallelujah; the meaningful difference is rescue-gratitude pressed deliberately into public, congregational form.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7676192, 7676193, 7676194, 7676195, 7676196, 7676197, 7676198, 7676199, 7676201, 7676202, 7676200</sub>

**extol** — Psa 107:32  ·  `H7311:extol#2`

_Story_

The rescued sailors lift God high in the gathering of the people. The verse does not separately name the cause, but the passage supplies it - the storm was stilled and they were brought safely home. The act is not tied to any one part of a person or measured for force; it is simply the lifting-up of God's name. It sits beside a companion act of praising him in the assembly, but this word carries its own note: not just thanking God but raising him high before others. The gratitude of the delivered turns outward into a public exalting of God's name.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is to extol (rum), literally to lift high, and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the sailors, inferred, and the operation (106) is to extol their target (107), God, 'in the congregation of the people.' The source (103) is present:false as an encoded field, though the passage gives the ground in the stilled storm and the safe haven; the reading logs no separate source-pair. The seat (104) is none, so the extolling is not lodged in a particular organ. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so only the bare act of lifting God high is recorded. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note reads this as the public praise of the delivered, gratitude among the assembly, and the locus (116) pairs it with praising in the assembly of elders. This reading is held distinct from its verse-mate 'praise' (halal): extolling (rum) carries the specific movement of raising God high, exalting his standing, whereas the paired halal is the broader act of praise - the two are companion but not identical. Within the inner life this is gratitude that does not merely voice thanks but lifts God's name up in the sight of the gathered people.

*Why this reading differs:* H7311:extol#1 (Psa 30:1) is a personal 'I extol you' for being drawn up from the depths, and #3 (Psa 118:28) is again the psalmist's own extolling. This reading's bearer is the storm-rescued sailors extolling God in the congregation - a corporate, public act rather than a solitary voice. The shift from individual thanksgiving to communal exalting before the assembly is the meaningful difference.

## Psa 108:1-8

> A Song . A Psalm of David . Psa 108:1 My heart is steadfast , O God ! I will sing and make melody with all my being ! Psa 108:2 Awake , O harp and lyre ! I will awake the dawn ! Psa 108:3 I will give thanks to you, O Lord , among the peoples ; I will sing praises to you among the nations . Psa 108:4 For your steadfast love is great above the heavens ; your faithfulness reaches to the clouds . Psa 108:5 Be exalted , O God , above the heavens ! Let your glory be over all the earth ! Psa 108:6 That your beloved ones may be delivered , give salvation by your right hand and answer me ! Psa 108:7 God has promised in his holiness : “With exultation I will divide up Shechem and portion out the Valley of Succoth . Psa 108:8 Gilead is mine; Manasseh is mine; Ephraim is my helmet , Judah my scepter . Psa 108:9 Moab is my washbasin; upon Edom I cast my shoe; over Philistia I shout in triumph.”

**make melody** — Psa 108:1  ·  `H2167:makemelody#2`

_Story_

The psalmist declares that his heart is steady and resolves to make music to God with his whole being. Nothing outside is named as the cause; the melody rises out of a settled, fixed heart. The verse does not lodge the music in any single feeling, yet it unusually names the whole inner self as its source - the steadfast heart and all his being. Nothing measures how strong or skilful it is; it is simply the making of music, joined to singing. The self, already made firm inside, pours itself out in song to God.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is to make melody (zamar) and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the psalmist, inferred, and the operation (106) is to make melody to the target (107), God. The source (103) is present:false as an encoded field, yet the verse names the inner condition from which the music flows - 'My heart is steadfast, O God! I will sing and make melody with all my being' - so the melody rises from a fixed heart even though no external cause is logged. The seat (104) is none as a recorded organ, but the verse itself supplies a striking inner backdrop: the steadfast heart and 'all my being' are named as the ground, so the reading's 'no seat' sits against a text that unusually foregrounds the whole inner self. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so the bare act of music-making is recorded without measure or result. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note reads this as the music of worship from a fixed heart, and the locus (116) pairs it with singing. So the movement is the inner life, settled and resolved, pouring itself out as music - praise that begins in a heart already made firm and carries the whole self into song.

*Why this reading differs:* The sibling H2167:makemelody#1 (Psa 57:7) also pairs making melody with singing and names a steadfast heart, but there the melody answers the God 'to be exalted above the heavens.' This reading logs no such source and rests the music simply on the fixed heart and 'all my being'; the difference is slight, a shift from an exalted-God ground to a purely inward, heart-settled ground for the same music.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7676853, 7676854, 7676855, 7676856, 7676857, 7676858, 7676859, 7676860, 7676862, 7676863, 7676861</sub>

**sing** — Psa 108:1  ·  `H7891:sing#17`

_Story_

The psalmist, his heart steady, resolves to sing to God with his whole being. Nothing outside is named as the cause; the song comes out of a fixed, settled heart. The verse does not tie the singing to one feeling, yet it names the steadfast heart and the whole self as its ground. Nothing measures how loud or strong it is - it is simply singing, set alongside the making of music, the plain song kept distinct from the melody beside it. The self, already firm within, lifts its whole being to God in song.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is to sing (shir) and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the psalmist, inferred, and the operation (106) is to sing to the target (107), God. The source (103) is present:false as an encoded field, but the verse supplies the inner ground - 'My heart is steadfast, O God! I will sing... with all my being' - so the song flows from a fixed heart though no external cause is logged. The seat (104) is none as a recorded organ, yet the verse itself names the steadfast heart and 'all my being' as the ground, so the reading's absence of a seat sits beside a text that foregrounds the whole inner self. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so the bare act of singing stands unmeasured. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note reads this as the sung praise of the steadfast heart, and the locus (116) pairs it with making melody and the steadfast heart. This reading is kept distinct from its verse-mate 'make melody' (zamar): here is the plain act of song, there the music - the two named together but not collapsed. The movement is the settled inner life voicing itself in song, the whole being carried up to God from a heart already firm.

*Why this reading differs:* Several H7891:sing readings carry an explicit source-pair (God to be exalted above the heavens, Psa 57:7; God as fortress, Psa 59:16) or summon a fresh 'new song.' This reading logs no source and rests the song on the steadfast heart and 'all my being,' pairing it with making melody. The distinctive ground - a settled inner self rather than a named external prompt - is the meaningful difference.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7676842, 7676843, 7676844, 7676845, 7676846, 7676847, 7676848, 7676849, 7676851, 7676852, 7676850</sub>

**sing praises** — Psa 108:3  ·  `H2167:singprais#14`

_Story_

The psalmist resolves to sing God's praises out among the nations. Nothing outside is named as the trigger; it comes as a settled resolve. The singing is not tied to any one part of a person or measured for strength - it is simply sung praise, joined to giving thanks. What marks it out is the setting: the praise is lifted to God but voiced in front of the nations, out in the open. The self will not keep its worship private but carries it onto the widest stage, before the watching peoples.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is to sing praises (zamar) and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the psalmist, inferred, and the operation (106) is to sing praises to the target (107), God, 'among the nations.' The source (103) is present:false: no external cause is encoded here, so the sung praise is issued as a resolve rather than a labelled response. The seat (104) is none, so the singing is not lodged in one organ. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so the bare act of sung praise stands unmeasured. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note reads this as sung praise before the peoples, and the locus (116) pairs it with giving thanks. The distinctive feature is the arena of the target: the praise is aimed at God but voiced 'among the nations,' so within the inner life this is worship that refuses to stay private - the self resolves to sing God's praise out in the open, before the watching peoples. It is praise turned public not toward a congregation of the faithful but toward the nations at large, the inner life projecting its worship onto the widest possible stage.

*Why this reading differs:* Some H2167:singprais readings carry an explicit source-pair (God's faithfulness reaching the clouds, Psa 57:9; God as fortress, Psa 59:17). This reading logs no source and, distinctively, aims the sung praise 'among the nations,' pairing it with thanksgiving. The shift to a public, nation-wide arena for the same act, without a stated prompt, is the meaningful difference.

## Psa 109:1-8

> To the choirmaster . A Psalm of David . Psa 109:1 Be not silent , O God of my praise ! Psa 109:2 For wicked and deceitful mouths are opened against me, speaking against me with lying tongues . Psa 109:3 They encircle me with words of hate , and attack me without cause . Psa 109:4 In return for my love they accuse me, but I give myself to prayer . Psa 109:5 So they reward me evil for good , and hatred for my love . Psa 109:6 Appoint a wicked man against him; let an accuser stand at his right hand . Psa 109:7 When he is tried , let him come forth guilty ; let his prayer be counted as sin ! Psa 109:8 May his days be few ; may another take his office ! Psa 109:9 May his children be fatherless and his wife a widow! Psa 109:10 May his children wander about and beg , seeking food far from the ruins they inhabit ! Psa 109:12 Let there be none to extend kindness to him, nor any to pity his fatherless children ! Psa 109:13 May his posterity be cut off; may his name be blotted out in the second generation!

**praise** — Psa 109:1  ·  `H8416:prais#12`

_Story_

Under attack from lying, hateful voices, the psalmist calls out to the God he praises - 'the God of my praise' - begging him not to stay silent. His praise here is not a burst of celebration but a settled relationship he leans on; the God he has always praised is the one he now needs to speak up for him. The verse does not tie this praise to any one part of a person or measure its strength - it simply names it as the ground of his plea. Out of a dark, embattled place, the worshipping self appeals on the strength of its own long worship, asking God to break his silence.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (tehillah), praise as a named thing rather than only an act, and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the psalmist, inferred, and the operation (106) is to praise the target (107), God - here God addressed as 'God of my praise.' The source (103) is present:false: no cause is encoded, and the praise is invoked not as a fresh response but as a settled relation - the God whom the psalmist already praises is now begged not to stay silent. The seat (104) is none, so the praise is not lodged in a single organ. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so only the bare naming of praise is recorded. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note captures the striking setting: 'Be not silent, O God of my praise!' - the psalmist's worship becomes the very ground of his appeal, and the locus (116) pairs it with the plea that God not be silent against his slanderers. So the movement must be read at full weight in a dark context: surrounded by lying, hateful mouths, the inner life leans on its habitual praise of God as the basis for crying out. Praise here is not celebration but a standing relationship invoked under attack - the worshipping self appealing, on the strength of its praise, to a God it needs to speak.

*Why this reading differs:* Most H8416:prais readings encode praise flowing out toward God (filling the mouth, Psa 71:8; rendered in Zion, Psa 65:1) or prompted by his deliverance. This reading is distinctive: praise appears as a settled relation ('God of my praise') invoked from within an assault by slanderers, as the ground of a plea that God not stay silent. The move from outflowing celebration to praise-as-basis-of-appeal under attack is the meaningful difference.

## Psa 109:28-31

> Psa 109:27 Let them know that this is your hand; you, O Lord, have done it! Psa 109:28 Let them curse , but you will bless ! They arise and are put to shame , but your servant will be glad ! Psa 109:29 May my accusers be clothed with dishonor ; may they be wrapped in their own shame as in a cloak ! Psa 109:30 With my mouth I will give great thanks to the Lord ; I will praise him in the midst of the throng . Psa 109:31 For he stands at the right hand of the needy one , to save him from those who condemn his soul to death.

**Praise** — Psa 109:30  ·  `H1984:prais#15`

_Story_

At the close of a psalm full of bitter conflict, the psalmist vows to praise God out loud in the middle of the crowd. The verse does not separately label the cause, but the passage makes it clear - his accusers are put to shame and God stands beside the needy to save him, so the praise answers that rescue. It is not lodged in any one part of him, though he names his mouth as the instrument of thanks. Nothing measures its strength; it is simply praise, joined to thanksgiving. The vindicated self turns from its enemies to lift praise openly among the people, ending a dark psalm on a public note of gratitude.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal) and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the psalmist, inferred, and the operation (106) is to praise the target (107), God, 'in the midst of the throng.' The source (103) is present:false as an encoded field, yet the passage supplies the ground: 'they arise and are put to shame, but your servant will be glad,' and God 'stands at the right hand of the needy one, to save him' - so the praise answers a vindication, though no separate source-pair is logged. The seat (104) is none, so the praise is not fixed in one organ; the verse does name the mouth ('With my mouth I will give great thanks'), but praise itself is not located as a faculty. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so the bare act stands unmeasured. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note reads this as the public praise of the delivered, and the locus (116) pairs it with giving thanks. So the movement, arising at the end of a psalm of bitter accusation, is the vindicated self turning from its enemies to praise God openly among the crowd - gratitude for rescue lifted deliberately into a public setting, the inner life closing a dark psalm on an outward, witnessed note of praise.

*Why this reading differs:* Unlike the many H1984:prais readings that stand as opening or closing Hallelujahs, this reading is a personal vow of public praise ('I will praise him in the midst of the throng') arising out of a psalm of bitter accusation, paired with thanksgiving. The distinctive marks - a vindicated individual, an explicit public throng, a dark context resolved in praise - set it apart, and the difference is meaningful.

## Psa 111:1-10

> Psa 111:1 Praise the Lord ! I will give thanks to the Lord with my whole heart , in the company of the upright , in the congregation . Psa 111:2 Great are the works of the Lord , studied by all who delight in them. Psa 111:3 Full of splendor and majesty is his work , and his righteousness endures forever . Psa 111:4 He has caused his wondrous works to be remembered ; the Lord is gracious and merciful . Psa 111:5 He provides food for those who fear him; he remembers his covenant forever . Psa 111:6 He has shown his people the power of his works , in giving them the inheritance of the nations . Psa 111:7 The works of his hands are faithful and just ; all his precepts are trustworthy ; Psa 111:8 they are established forever and ever , to be performed with faithfulness and uprightness . Psa 111:9 He sent redemption to his people ; he has commanded his covenant forever . Holy and awesome is his name ! Psa 111:10 The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom ; all those who practice it have a good understanding . His praise endures forever !

**Praise** — Psa 111:1  ·  `H1984:prais#16`

_Story_

The psalmist opens with 'Praise the LORD!' and vows to thank him with his whole heart, among the upright, in the gathering. Nothing names a fresh cause; the praise opens as a call, with the psalm behind it recounting God's great works. It is not lodged in any single feeling, yet the verse ties the thanks to the whole heart - the entire self engaged. Nothing measures its strength; it is simply praise, joined to thanksgiving in the congregation. And the psalm ends by saying God's praise lasts forever, so this whole-hearted opening is one moment of a praise meant never to stop.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal) and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the psalmist, inferred, and the operation (106) is to praise the target (107), the LORD, in the opening Hallelujah. The source (103) is present:false: no immediate cause is encoded, so the opening praise stands as a summons rather than a stated response, though the psalm behind it will catalogue God's great works. The seat (104) is none as a located organ, yet the verse at once names 'my whole heart' - 'I will give thanks to the LORD with my whole heart' - so the reading's absence of a seat sits against a text that grounds the thanks in the entire heart. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so the bare act of praise is recorded without measure or result. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note reads this as worship of the whole self, and the locus (116) pairs it with giving thanks 'in the company of the upright, in the congregation.' So the movement is praise voiced with the whole heart and set within a gathered assembly - the inner life engaging entirely and publicly. Notably the psalm closes on 'His praise endures forever,' so this opening act is one instance of a praise the psalm frames as unending.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is distinctive among H1984:prais openings in that the verse grounds the act in 'my whole heart' and sets it 'in the company of the upright, in the congregation,' with the psalm closing on praise that 'endures forever.' Against bare or rescue-prompted Hallelujahs elsewhere, the whole-hearted, congregational, enduring framing is the meaningful difference.

## Psa 112:1-10

> Psa 112:1 Praise the Lord ! Blessed is the man who fears the Lord , who greatly delights in his commandments ! Psa 112:2 His offspring will be mighty in the land ; the generation of the upright will be blessed . Psa 112:3 Wealth and riches are in his house , and his righteousness endures forever . Psa 112:4 Light dawns in the darkness for the upright ; he is gracious , merciful , and righteous . Psa 112:5 It is well with the man who deals generously and lends ; who conducts his affairs with justice . Psa 112:6 For the righteous will never be moved ; he will be remembered forever . Psa 112:7 He is not afraid of bad news ; his heart is firm , trusting in the Lord . Psa 112:8 His heart is steady ; he will not be afraid , until he looks in triumph on his adversaries . Psa 112:9 He has distributed freely; he has given to the poor ; his righteousness endures forever ; his horn is exalted in honor . Psa 112:10 The wicked man sees it and is angry ; he gnashes his teeth and melts away ; the desire of the wicked will perish !

**Praise** — Psa 112:1  ·  `H1984:prais#17`

_Story_

The psalm opens with 'Praise the LORD!' and then turns to describe the person who fears God and delights in his commands. Nothing names a cause for the praise; it simply opens the psalm as a call. It is not tied to any one part of a person or measured for strength - it is just the act of praise. What is unusual is what follows: rather than listing God's deeds, the psalm paints the inner steadiness of the blessed man, whose heart is firm and unafraid of bad news because he trusts God. So the praise here opens a portrait of a settled, fearless, God-fearing heart.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal) and the type (102) is an action. The bearer (105) is the worshipper, inferred, and the operation (106) is to praise the target (107), the LORD, in the opening Hallelujah that heads the psalm. The source (103) is present:false: no immediate cause is encoded, so the praise opens as a summons rather than a labelled response. The seat (104) is none, so the praise is not lodged in a single organ. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so only the bare act is recorded. The coupling (112) is external toward God, and the prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note reads this as the opening Hallelujah framing the portrait of the blessed man, and the locus (116) pairs it with the blessing of the God-fearer - 'Blessed is the man who fears the LORD.' So the distinctive feature is what the praise introduces: it stands at the head of a psalm that is not chiefly about God's deeds but about the character and steadiness of the one who fears God - the firm heart that is not afraid of bad news, trusting in the LORD. Within the inner life, then, this praise opens a meditation on the inner make-up of the blessed self: worship framing a portrait of a settled, unafraid, God-fearing heart.

*Why this reading differs:* Among H1984:prais openings, this Hallelujah is distinctive in what it frames: not a recital of God's works but a portrait of the God-fearer's inner steadiness (a firm, unafraid, trusting heart). The pairing with 'the blessing of the God-fearer' rather than with thanksgiving or God's deeds marks it off from the sibling openings, and the difference is meaningful.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7678298, 7678299, 7678300, 7678301, 7678302, 7678303, 7678304, 7678305, 7678307, 7678308, 7678306</sub>

**honor** — Psa 112:9  ·  `H3519:honor#1`

_Story_

Unlike the acts of praise around it, this is about a condition the person comes to hold: honour. The generous, God-fearing man - the one who gives freely to the poor and whose right living lasts - is lifted up in honour, pictured as a raised horn, a sign of strength and dignity. The verse does not spell out the cause in so many words, but the psalm's flow makes it plain: this dignity crowns his generosity and righteousness, and it comes from God. Nothing places it in one part of him or measures it; it is simply a settled standing of honour. And where the surrounding movements reach outward to God, this one rests inward - a dignity that has come to live in the self as the fruit of its own goodness, granted from above.

_Reading_

This reading stands apart from the praise-acts around it. The sense (101) is honour (kabod) and the type (102) is a state, not an action - a condition the inner life comes to hold rather than a movement it performs. The bearer (105) is the man, the righteous God-fearer of the psalm, inferred, and the operation (106) is to be exalted in honour, its target (107) being 'by God' - the honour is conferred, its giver God. The source (103) is present:false as an encoded field, though the psalm's logic supplies the ground: the man 'has distributed freely... given to the poor,' and 'his righteousness endures forever,' so the exaltation crowns his generosity. The seat (104) is none, so the honour is not lodged in a single organ; the verse images it as 'his horn is exalted,' a figure of raised strength and dignity rather than a located faculty. Manner (108) is none, and intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are absent, so the bare state of dignity is recorded without measure or further outcome. Crucially the coupling (112) differs from every other reading in this set: it is internal - an inner-being state - rather than external toward God, marking honour as a condition resident in the self, not a relation the self directs outward. The prohibition (113) is absent. The discovery note reads this as the dignity God gives the God-fearer, exaltation crowning his righteousness, and the locus (116) pairs it with the horn exalted. So within the inner life this is not worship offered but standing received: the generous, righteous self is lifted into honour, a dignity settled inwardly as the fruit of its own uprightness and given from above.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7678529, 7678530, 7678531, 7678532, 7678533, 7678534, 7678535, 7678536, 7678538, 7678539, 7678537</sub>

## Psa 113:1-9

> Psa 113:1 Praise the Lord ! Praise , O servants of the Lord , praise the name of the Lord ! Psa 113:2 Blessed be the name of the Lord from this time forth and forevermore ! Psa 113:3 From the rising of the sun to its setting , the name of the Lord is to be praised ! Psa 113:4 The Lord is high above all nations , and his glory above the heavens ! Psa 113:5 Who is like the Lord our God , who is seated on high , Psa 113:6 who looks far down on the heavens and the earth? Psa 113:7 He raises the poor from the dust and lifts the needy from the ash heap , Psa 113:8 to make them sit with princes , with the princes of his people . Psa 113:9 He gives the barren woman a home , making her the joyous mother of children . Praise the Lord !

**Praise** — Psa 113:1  ·  `H1984:prais#18`

_Story_

Psalm 113 opens with a plain call to praise the Lord. Nothing in the verse says what stirred this praise or where inside a person it comes from — it simply begins as a command answered, a turning of the whole self outward toward God. There is no measure of how loud or how deep it runs, no description of manner, and no note of anything it produces in the one who praises; the act is caught purely as a direction, aimed entirely at God. It stands alongside the call to praise his name, the simplest shape of a heart lifted away from itself toward the one it worships.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal), typed as an action (102): the opening Hallelujah that launches Psalm 113. The source (103) is marked absent, and the seat (104) is explicitly none [res=none] — the psalm names nothing inside the person that generates this praise and locates it in no organ of the inner life. Read as evidence, the silence says the praise here is not traced to a prior cause or a felt inward pressure; it arises as a summoned act, an imperative that begins from nothing but the call itself. The bearer (105) is the worshippers and the target (107) is the LORD, so the movement is wholly outward: the inner life turns from itself toward God, who is the arena the act reaches. The operation (106) is simply to praise, and the manner (108) is none [res=none] — no described tone, no gesture, only the bare directing of praise. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false: the verse measures no degree, adds no qualifier, and records no result rebounding on the worshipper — the act is captured as pure orientation, not as something that changes the one praising. The coupling (112) is external:god, confirming the whole process points away from the self and toward the LORD. There is no prohibition (113). The locus (116) pairs this praise with the servants' praise of his name, and the role (115) is characteristic — this is the inner-life movement of adoration in its plainest form: unsourced, unlocated, unqualified, and fully turned toward its object.

*Why this reading differs:* Among this term's many readings, some name a ground (Psa 48:1 praise of God made a fortress, Psa 74:21 of the God who works salvation) or shift the bearer (the poor and needy, the sailors, all nations, a people yet to be created). This reading is the bare opening imperative with no source stated and a generic body of worshippers — a pure summons rather than a grounded response, which is a meaningful difference in shape though the core act is the same.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7678682, 7678683, 7678684, 7678685, 7678686, 7678687, 7678688, 7678689, 7678691, 7678692, 7678690</sub>

**Praise** — Psa 113:1  ·  `H1984:prais#19`

_Story_

The same opening call narrows to a particular group: the servants of the Lord are told to praise. The verse does not say what feeling drives this or where in a person it rises — it comes as a summons to those who already serve, turning their service outward into worship of the one they belong to. No strength, no tone, and no result in the worshipper is described; the act is simply aimed at God and set beside the call to praise his name.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal), an action (102), here the summons 'Praise, O servants of the LORD'. The source (103) is absent and the seat (104) none [res=none]: the verse gives no inner cause and no bodily or faculty location for the praise — the silence reads as praise not welling from a named feeling but issued as a call to be obeyed. The bearer (105) is specifically the servants of the LORD and the target (107) is the LORD, so the inner movement is the servant-self turning in adoration toward the master it serves; God is the arena the act is aimed at. The operation (106) is to praise; the manner (108) is none [res=none], leaving the act undescribed in tone. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false — no degree, no qualifier, no rebound on the servant is recorded, so the praise is captured as sheer directed act. The coupling (112) is external:god and there is no prohibition (113). The locus (116) pairs it with praising his name, and the role (115) is characteristic. What distinguishes this reading is the bearer: the praise is laid on those who already stand in service, so the movement is the natural overflow of the servant relationship into worship, though the verse itself adds nothing about how or how strongly.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading differs from its siblings mainly in the bearer — not the general worshippers, the nations, or the psalmist, but the LORD's own servants specifically summoned. The praise is framed as the outflow of an existing service-bond rather than a fresh or grounded response, a distinction of who is praising more than of what praise does.

The sense (101) is praise (halal), an action (102): 'Praise the name of the LORD.' The source (103) is absent and the seat (104) none [res=none] — no inward cause, no located organ; the silence marks the praise as a called act rather than a traced-back inward stirring. The bearer (105) is the servants and, distinctively, the target (107) is the name of the LORD rather than the LORD directly — the inner movement fastens onto God as named and known, his revealed self, which is the arena the act reaches toward. The operation (106) is to praise; the manner (108) is none [res=none]. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false: no degree, no qualifier, no result on the servant — the act is pure orientation. The coupling (112) is external:god, there is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) pairs it with blessing his name, and the role (115) is characteristic. The one feature that sets this reading apart is the target being the name: praise here is worship of God as revealed and nameable, the inner life addressing not an abstraction but the known character God has made public.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is set apart from most siblings by its target: praise aimed at the name of the LORD rather than at the LORD directly (as in #18/#19) or at his word (as in the Psalm 119 readings). The object is God as revealed and nameable, a real shift in what the praise takes hold of.

The psalm ends as it began, with a call to praise the Lord — now sealing a song about the God who raises the poor from the dust and gives the barren woman a joyful home. The operation (106) is to praise; the manner (108) is none [res=none]. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false — no measured degree, no qualifier, no rebound on the worshipper. The coupling (112) is external:god, there is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) pairs it with the whole psalm's praise, and the role (115) is characteristic. *Why this reading differs:* This reading differs from its siblings by position rather than shape: it is the closing Hallelujah that seals the psalm, paired with the whole psalm's praise, whereas #18–#20 are the opening calls. The act is identical; the placement as a bracket-close is the distinction.

## Psa 115:15-18

> Psa 115:15 May you be blessed by the Lord , who made heaven and earth ! Psa 115:16 The heavens are the Lord’s heavens , but the earth he has given to the children of man . Psa 115:17 The dead do not praise the Lord , nor do any who go down into silence . Psa 115:18 But we will bless the Lord from this time forth and forevermore . Praise the Lord !

**Praise** — Psa 115:18  ·  `H1984:prais#22`

_Story_

Psalm 115 closes with praise from the living, just after noting that the dead are silent and praise no more. The verse gives no inner cause for this praise and points to no place inside where it forms; it is simply what the living do, blessing God now and forever. No strength or manner or result is described — only the quiet weight that this is the worship of those still able to give it, set beside the blessing of the Lord.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal), an action (102): the closing Hallelujah of Psalm 115, set against v17's stark note that 'the dead do not praise the LORD.' The source (103) is absent and the seat (104) none [res=none] — no inward cause named, no organ located; the silence reads as praise that is simply the living self's act, undissected. The bearer (105) is the worshippers and the target (107) is the LORD, so the inner movement is outward toward God, the arena. The operation (106) is to praise; the manner (108) is none [res=none]. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false — no degree, no qualifier, no result on the praiser. The coupling (112) is external:god, there is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) pairs it with blessing the LORD, and the role (115) is characteristic. Its distinctive lies in context: the passage contrasts the living who bless God 'from this time forth and forevermore' with the silent dead, so this praise is quietly marked as the act of the living — the inner life exercising, while it can, the movement the grave forecloses.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading shares the bare shape of its siblings but sits in a passage that contrasts the living praisers with the silent dead, giving the ordinary Hallelujah a distinctive weight as the act of the living. The dims themselves record no source or intensity, so the distinction is carried by the surrounding text, not by a shift in the item's coding.

## Psa 117:1-2

> Psa 117:1 Praise the Lord , all nations ! Extol him, all peoples ! Psa 117:2 For great is his steadfast love toward us, and the faithfulness of the Lord endures forever . Praise the Lord !

**Praise** — Psa 117:1  ·  `H1984:prais#24`

_Story_

The shortest psalm opens by calling all nations to praise the Lord. Nothing is said of what stirs this praise or where it forms inside a person; it is flung out as a summons to the whole world. No strength or tone or result is described — the striking thing is simply its reach: every nation is called to the same turning toward God, set beside the call to extol him.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal), an action (102): 'Praise the LORD, all nations!' — the summons of the shortest psalm, widening worship beyond Israel. The source (103) is absent and the seat (104) none [res=none]: no inward cause is stated and no organ located, so the silence reads as praise issued as a universal call rather than a feeling traced within any one people. The bearer (105) is, distinctively, all nations, and the target (107) is the LORD — the inner-life movement of adoration is here demanded of the whole human world, all turning outward toward the one God who is the arena. The operation (106) is to praise; the manner (108) is none [res=none]. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false — no degree, no qualifier, no rebound recorded. The coupling (112) is external:god, there is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) pairs it with extolling him, and the role (115) is characteristic. Its distinctive is the bearer's scope: praise is thrown open to every nation, the inner act of worship claimed as the proper movement of all mankind, not one people.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading's distinctive among its siblings is the universal bearer — all nations rather than Israel, the servants, or the individual psalmist. Praise is widened to the whole human race, a meaningful expansion of who is summoned even though the act itself is the same bare Godward call.

**Extol** — Psa 117:1  ·  `H7623:extol#1`

_Story_

Alongside the call to praise, Psalm 117 tells all peoples to extol the Lord — to lift him high in commendation. The verse names no inner cause and no inward seat for this; it is a summons to the whole world to magnify God aloud. No strength or manner or effect is described. It stands beside the call to praise, its own distinct act of lauding, and the next verse gives the reason the psalm offers: God's great and lasting love.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is extol (shabach), an action (102): 'Extol him, all peoples!' — the parallel summons to Psalm 117's call to praise. The source (103) is absent and the seat (104) none [res=none]: the verse names no inner cause and locates the act in no organ; the silence reads as a called act of lauding rather than a traced inward pressure. The bearer (105) is all peoples and the target (107) is the LORD, so the inner movement is the whole human world lifting God up in commendation, God being the arena. The operation (106) is to extol — distinct from the paired 'praise' as a lauding, a public magnifying; the manner (108) is none [res=none]. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false — no degree, no qualifier, no rebound on the peoples. The coupling (112) is external:god, there is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) pairs it with praising him, and the role (115) is characteristic. The reading holds a distinct verb from its praise-parallel and is preserved as its own movement: to extol is to lift God high in the mouths of the nations, and v2 supplies the ground the psalm gives — God's great steadfast love and enduring faithfulness — though that ground is not coded here as a formal source.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7679607, 7679608, 7679609, 7679610, 7679611, 7679612, 7679613, 7679614, 7679616, 7679617, 7679615</sub>

No strength or manner or result is described — just the whole world's praise, sealed and turned toward God.

_Reading_

The source (103) is marked absent in the coding and the seat (104) is none [res=none] — the verse names no organ and the formal source-field is empty, even though v2 supplies the psalm's stated ground; read as evidence, the praise-word itself is captured as a plain sealing act, not dissected into an inner cause. The bearer (105) is all nations and the target (107) is the LORD, so the inner movement is the whole world's adoration turned Godward, the arena. The operation (106) is to praise; the manner (108) is none [res=none]. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false — no degree, no qualifier, no rebound. The coupling (112) is external:god, there is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) pairs it with the ground of his steadfast love, and the role (115) is characteristic. Its distinctive: this closing praise is the one whose locus explicitly ties it to God's steadfast love and faithfulness — the inner act of worship rounded off and quietly grounded in the covenant kindness the psalm has just named.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading's distinctive is its locus tying the closing praise to God's steadfast love and faithfulness (v2), whereas the opening call (#24) simply summons all nations without a stated ground. The praise-act is the same; here it is the grounded seal rather than the bare opening summons.

## Psa 118:28-29

> Psa 118:28 You are my God , and I will give thanks to you; you are my God ; I will extol you . Psa 118:29 Oh give thanks to the Lord , for he is good ; for his steadfast love endures forever !

**extol** — Psa 118:28  ·  `H7311:extol#3`

_Story_

The psalmist declares, 'You are my God, and I will extol you' — a personal resolve to lift God high. Nothing is said of what feeling drives this or where it rises inside him; it is stated as a decision, twinned with giving thanks. No strength or manner or result is described. What stands out is its intimacy: the self claims God as its own and pledges to exalt him.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is extol (rum), an action (102): 'you are my God; I will extol you.' The source (103) is absent and the seat (104) none [res=none] — no inward cause is named and no organ located; the silence reads as a personal resolve to lift God high that is stated as a decision rather than traced to a felt pressure. The bearer (105) is the psalmist and the target (107) is God, so the inner movement is the individual self raising God up in worship, God being the arena reached. The operation (106) is to extol — to lift high, exalt; the manner (108) is none [res=none], the resolve stated without described tone. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false — no degree, no qualifier, no rebound on the psalmist. The coupling (112) is external:god, there is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) pairs it with giving thanks, and the role (115) is characteristic. Its distinctive: this is the exalting movement in the first person, welded to thanksgiving — 'I will give thanks to you … I will extol you' — the inner life claiming God as its own ('my God') and resolving to raise him high.

*Why this reading differs:* Unlike sibling #1 (Psa 30:1), where the exalting is prompted by God drawing the self up from the depths, and #2 (Psa 107:32), where sailors extol God in the congregation, this reading gives no rescue-ground: it is a bare first-person resolve welded to thanksgiving, claiming God as 'my God'. The difference is a shift from prompted, situational exalting to a plain personal vow.

## Psa 119:1-18

> Aleph Psa 119:1 Blessed are those whose way is blameless , who walk in the law of the Lord ! Psa 119:2 Blessed are those who keep his testimonies , who seek him with their whole heart , Psa 119:3 who also do no wrong , but walk in his ways ! Psa 119:4 You have commanded your precepts to be kept diligently . Psa 119:5 Oh that my ways may be steadfast in keeping your statutes ! Psa 119:6 Then I shall not be put to shame , having my eyes fixed on all your commandments . Psa 119:7 I will praise you with an upright heart , when I learn your righteous rules . Psa 119:8 I will keep your statutes ; do not utterly forsake me! Beth Psa 119:9 How can a young man keep his way pure ? By guarding it according to your word . Psa 119:10 With my whole heart I seek you; let me not wander from your commandments ! Psa 119:11 I have stored up your word in my heart , that I might not sin against you . Psa 119:12 Blessed are you , O Lord ; teach me your statutes ! Psa 119:13 With my lips I declare all the rules of your mouth . Psa 119:14 In the way of your testimonies I delight as much as in all riches . Psa 119:15 I will meditate on your precepts and fix my eyes on your ways . Psa 119:16 I will delight in your statutes ; I will not forget your word . Gimel Psa 119:17 Deal bountifully with your servant , that I may live and keep your word . Psa 119:18 Open my eyes , that I may behold wondrous things out of your law .

**praise** — Psa 119:7  ·  `H3034:prais#13`

_Story_

The psalmist says he will praise God with an upright heart as he learns God's righteous rules. The verse does not trace this praise to a prior feeling; instead it ties it to learning — worship set to grow as understanding grows. The praise reaches toward God through his word rather than at God in the abstract, and it belongs to a thread of praise running through the whole long psalm. No degree or result is spelled out, but the same praising heart surfaces again later in the psalm.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (yadah), an action (102): 'I will praise you with an upright heart, when I learn your righteous rules.' The source (103) is absent as a coded field and the seat (104) none [res=none] — yet the verse names an 'upright heart' as the disposition of the praise and ties it to learning; the formal source is left empty, so the coding reads the praise not as caused by a prior stirring but as resolved to grow with understanding. The bearer (105) is the psalmist and, distinctively, the target (107) is God's word — the inner movement of thanksgiving-praise is aimed at God through his righteous rules, the arena being God as met in his instruction. The operation (106) is to praise; the manner (108) is none [res=none] despite the 'upright heart', which the coding leaves as an unelaborated condition rather than a formal manner. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false — no degree, no qualifier, no rebound. The coupling (112) is paired within its char-arc across the psalm, so this praise belongs to a thread running through Psalm 119; there is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) is external:god, and the role (115) is characteristic. Its distinctive is the conditional welding of praise to learning: praise is resolved to deepen precisely as the self learns God's righteous rules — worship growing with knowledge. The recurrence at Psa 119:62 shows the same praising-self reappearing later in the psalm.

*Why this reading differs:* This term's readings scatter widely: some praise God among the nations (Psa 18:49), some ask whether the dust can praise (Psa 30:9), and some are not Godward at all — nations praising the king (Psa 45:17), others praising the prosperous man (Psa 49:18). This reading is distinctive in aiming praise at God's word and welding it to learning within Psalm 119's sustained arc, rather than at God directly or at a human object.

*Recurs at:* Psa 119:62

## Psa 119:66-82

> Psa 119:65 TethYou have dealt well with your servant, O Lord, according to your word. Psa 119:66 Teach me good judgment and knowledge , for I believe in your commandments . Psa 119:67 Before I was afflicted I went astray , but now I keep your word . Psa 119:68 You are good and do good ; teach me your statutes . Psa 119:69 The insolent smear me with lies , but with my whole heart I keep your precepts ; Psa 119:70 their heart is unfeeling like fat , but I delight in your law . Psa 119:71 It is good for me that I was afflicted , that I might learn your statutes . Psa 119:72 The law of your mouth is better to me than thousands of gold and silver pieces. Yodh Psa 119:73 Your hands have made and fashioned me; give me understanding that I may learn your commandments . Psa 119:74 Those who fear you shall see me and rejoice , because I have hoped in your word . Psa 119:75 I know , O Lord , that your rules are righteous , and that in faithfulness you have afflicted me . Psa 119:76 Let your steadfast love comfort me according to your promise to your servant . Psa 119:77 Let your mercy come to me, that I may live ; for your law is my delight . Psa 119:78 Let the insolent be put to shame, because they have wronged me with falsehood ; as for me , I will meditate on your precepts . Psa 119:79 Let those who fear you turn to me, that they may know your testimonies . Psa 119:80 May my heart be blameless in your statutes , that I may not be put to shame ! Kaph Psa 119:81 My soul longs for your salvation ; I hope in your word . Psa 119:82 My eyes long for your promise ; I ask , “ When will you comfort me ?” Psa 119:83 For I have become like a wineskin in the smoke, yet I have not forgotten your statutes.

**wronged** — Psa 119:78  ·  `H5791:wrong#1`

_Story_

The insolent have wronged the psalmist with lies, and he asks that shame fall on them. The verse does not explain what stirred their malice or name any inner place it came from; it simply reports the wrong as done, aimed straight at another person and carried out with falsehood. No measure of it is given, and no result is spelled out beyond the plea for their shame. It is a plain act of injury from the proud against a fellow human, part of the psalm's ongoing clash between the arrogant and the one who holds to God's word.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is wrong (avath), an action (102): 'Let the insolent be put to shame, because they have wronged me with falsehood.' This is a descent-movement and is read at full weight. The source (103) is absent and the seat (104) none [res=none] — the verse names no inner cause for the insolent's wronging and locates it in no organ; the silence reads as malice enacted without stated inward origin, the wrong presented as done rather than psychologised. The bearer (105) is the insolent and the target (107) is the psalmist, so the movement runs from the proud outward against a person — here the arena is another human being, the one wronged. The operation (106) is to wrong the psalmist, done 'with falsehood'; the manner (108) is none [res=none] as a coded field, though the verse attaches the wrong to lies. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false — no degree, no formal qualifier, and no coded rebound, even though the psalmist prays that shame fall on them. The coupling (112) is paired within its char-arc across the psalm; there is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) is external:person, and the role (115) is characteristic. This is the inner life's outward act of injury: the proud discharge falsehood against another, an aggression of the inner being turned against a fellow human, set within the psalm's running contrast between the insolent and the one who keeps God's precepts.

## Psa 119:151-176

> Psa 119:151 But you are near , O Lord , and all your commandments are true . Psa 119:152 Long have I known from your testimonies that you have founded them forever . Resh Psa 119:153 Look on my affliction and deliver me, for I do not forget your law . Psa 119:154 Plead my cause and redeem me; give me life according to your promise ! Psa 119:155 Salvation is far from the wicked , for they do not seek your statutes . Psa 119:156 Great is your mercy , O Lord ; give me life according to your rules . Psa 119:157 Many are my persecutors and my adversaries , but I do not swerve from your testimonies . Psa 119:158 I look at the faithless with disgust , because they do not keep your commands . Psa 119:159 Consider how I love your precepts ! Give me life , O Lord , according to your steadfast love . Psa 119:160 The sum of your word is truth , and every one of your righteous rules endures forever . Sin and Shin Psa 119:161 Princes persecute me without cause , but my heart stands in awe of your words . Psa 119:162 I rejoice at your word like one who finds great spoil . Psa 119:163 I hate and abhor falsehood , but I love your law . Psa 119:164 Seven times a day I praise you for your righteous rules . Psa 119:165 Great peace have those who love your law ; nothing can make them stumble . Psa 119:166 I hope for your salvation , O Lord , and I do your commandments . Psa 119:167 My soul keeps your testimonies ; I love them exceedingly . Psa 119:168 I keep your precepts and testimonies , for all my ways are before you . Taw Psa 119:169 Let my cry come before you, O Lord ; give me understanding according to your word ! Psa 119:170 Let my plea come before you; deliver me according to your word . Psa 119:171 My lips will pour forth praise , for you teach me your statutes . Psa 119:172 My tongue will sing of your word , for all your commandments are right . Psa 119:173 Let your hand be ready to help me, for I have chosen your precepts . Psa 119:174 I long for your salvation , O Lord , and your law is my delight . Psa 119:175 Let my soul live and praise you, and let your rules help me . Psa 119:176 I have gone astray like a lost sheep ; seek your servant , for I do not forget your commandments .

**Praise** — Psa 119:164  ·  `H1984:prais#26`

_Story_

The psalmist praises God seven times a day for his righteous rules. The verse does not root this in a passing feeling or point to an inner organ; it presents praise as a steady, repeated habit, aimed at God through his word. The sevenfold rhythm marks how constant it is rather than how loud, and no particular result is described. This praise belongs to the long psalm's running thread of devotion, and the same praising heart appears again near its end.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (halal), an action (102): 'Seven times a day I praise you for your righteous rules.' The source (103) is absent and the seat (104) none [res=none] — no organ is named; the silence reads as praise made a settled, repeated practice rather than a single traced surge of feeling. The bearer (105) is the psalmist and, distinctively, the target (107) is God's word (his righteous rules), so the inner movement of praise is directed at God through his instruction, the arena being God as met in his rules. The operation (106) is to praise; the manner (108) is none [res=none]. Notably intensity (109) is present:false even though 'seven times a day' signals frequency — the coding treats the sevenfold repetition as a matter of recurrence, not of measured strength; specifier (110) and effect (111) are likewise present:false. The coupling (112) is paired within its char-arc across the psalm, tying this praise into Psalm 119's sustained thread; there is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) is external:god, and the role (115) is characteristic. Its distinctive is the rhythm: praise as a ceaseless, patterned discipline, 'seven times a day', the inner life returning again and again to worship God's righteous rules. The recurrence at Psa 119:175 shows the praising-self reappearing near the psalm's close.

*Why this reading differs:* Against most siblings, whose target is the LORD or his name, this reading aims praise at God's word (his righteous rules) and frames it as a ceaseless sevenfold daily discipline within Psalm 119's arc. The difference is meaningful: praise here is a patterned habit fastened on God's instruction rather than a single Godward summons or seal.

*Recurs at:* Psa 119:175

**praise** — Psa 119:171  ·  `H8416:prais#13`

_Story_

The psalmist says his lips will pour out praise because God teaches him his statutes. The verse does not trace this to a hidden feeling; it points to the lips overflowing and gives God's teaching as the cause — praise brimming over in response to being taught. The overflow is in the pouring itself rather than any stated measure, and no further result is described. It is worship welling up through God's word, part of the long psalm's running thread of devotion.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is praise (tehillah), an action (102): 'My lips will pour forth praise, for you teach me your statutes.' The source (103) is absent as a coded field and the seat (104) none [res=none] — yet the verse names the lips as the point of overflow and gives God's teaching as the reason; the formal source is left empty, so the coding reads the praise as pouring out in response to instruction rather than from a separately traced inner cause. The bearer (105) is the psalmist and the target (107) is God's word, so the inner movement is praise overflowing toward God through what he teaches, the arena being God as instructor. The operation (106), distinctively, is to pour forth praise — not a bare 'praise' but an overflow, praise brimming past the lips; the manner (108) is none [res=none]. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false — the overflow is in the verb itself, not in a coded degree, and no qualifier or rebound is recorded. The coupling (112) is paired within its char-arc across the psalm; there is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) is external:god, and the role (115) is characteristic. Its distinctive is the image of overflow tied to being taught: as God teaches his statutes, praise wells up and spills from the lips — the inner life so filled by instruction that worship pours out.

*Why this reading differs:* This term's siblings range from praise as a noun that reaches the ends of the earth (Psa 48:10, where operation and target are coded none) to continual praise filling the mouth all day (Psa 71:8). This reading is distinctive in its operation — praise poured forth from the lips — and in tying that overflow directly to God's teaching within Psalm 119's arc, an overflow-response rather than a standing or spatial praise.

## Psa 132:16-18

> Psa 132:16 Her priests I will clothe with salvation , and her saints will shout for joy . Psa 132:17 There I will make a horn to sprout for David ; I have prepared a lamp for my anointed . Psa 132:18 His enemies I will clothe with shame , but on him his crown will shine .”

**shout** — Psa 132:16  ·  `H7442:shout#1`

_Story_

God promises that Zion's faithful will shout for joy. The verse does not trace the shout to a stated feeling or an inner organ; it bursts out as the saints respond to God clothing their priests with salvation. No measure of it is given and no further result named — it is simply worship breaking into a loud cry, the outward sound of a gladness that lives inside, set beside the joy itself.

_Reading_

The sense (101) is shout (ranan), an action (102): 'her saints will shout for joy' — God's promise over Zion that its saints will cry aloud. The source (103) is absent and the seat (104) none [res=none] as coded fields; the wider verse roots the shouting in God clothing the priests with salvation, but the source-field is empty, so the coding reads the shout as an overflow-act rather than a traced inner cause, and it is located in no organ. The bearer (105) is the saints and the target (107) is 'for joy', so the inner movement is worship breaking out as vocal cry — the shout aimed at the expression of joy, with God as the arena the exultation answers. The operation (106) is to shout; the manner (108) is none [res=none]. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false — no measured degree, no qualifier, no coded rebound, though the shout is itself a loud act. The coupling (112) is external:god, there is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) pairs it with the joy, and the role (115) is characteristic. Its distinctive is that this is praise as sound over content: the saints' worship overflows God's blessing into a loud cry, the outward voicing of an inner gladness that the paired joy-reading holds.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading is paired with its sibling from the same verse (#2), which codes the very same word as joy — an inner state — rather than the shout. The distinction is meaningful and deliberate: #1 is the outward vocal cry aimed at God, #2 is the inner gladness that voices it, one word read twice to hold both the sound and the feeling.

<sub>cites ve_lexical: 7686101, 7686102, 7686103, 7686104, 7686105, 7686106, 7686107, 7686108, 7686110, 7686111, 7686109</sub>

**shout** — Psa 132:16  ·  `H7442:shout#2`

_Story_

The same promise that Zion's faithful will shout for joy is read here for the joy itself — the inner gladness beneath the cry. Nothing names what first stirred it or where it sits inside; it simply rises in the saints as they rest in God's salvation. No measure or result is described. Where the shout is the outward sound, this is the feeling within that the shout expresses.

_Reading_

The sense (101) here is joy (ranan), and the type (102) is a state — the same word as the shout, but read as the inner gladness rather than the outward cry. The source (103) is absent and the seat (104) none [res=none]; no inner cause is formally coded and no organ located, so the silence reads the joy as arising in the saints at God's salvation without a traced prior stirring or a named seat. The bearer (105) is the saints and the target (107) is 'in God's salvation', so the inner movement is gladness resting on and responding to what God has done, the arena being God's saving work. The operation (106) is to rejoice; the manner (108) is none [res=none]. Intensity (109), specifier (110) and effect (111) are all present:false — no degree, no qualifier, no coded rebound. Distinctively, the coupling (112) is internal:ib-state — unlike its paired shout, whose coupling is external:god, this reading is coded as an inner condition of the being rather than an outward-directed act. There is no prohibition (113), the locus (116) pairs it with the shout, and the role (115) is characteristic. This is the inward face of the same moment: the gladness itself, the felt state in the saints that the shout gives voice to.

*Why this reading differs:* This reading and its sibling #1 are the same word in the same verse, split into the inner state and the outward act: here the coupling is internal (an inner gladness) where #1's is external toward God (the voiced shout), and here the type is a state where #1 is an action. The difference carries real meaning — it preserves the distinction between the joy felt and the joy cried aloud.
